## Dedication

*For my brother, Ian  *

## Foreword

The address came on a postcard.

No return name. No message. A photograph of the Vistula taken from somewhere north of Płock, and on the back, in pencil, the coordinates of a farm two hours east of Warsaw and a date in October. Someone wants to be found, or wants me found, I thought, turning it over twice as if a second look might produce a signature. I didn't recognise the handwriting at first, too careful, too deliberate for a note meant to be casual. But I understood what I was looking at.

I had been going back anyway. The Guardian had sent me in for a week, my first time in the zone since the early days, since before any of this, and after years at the Bureau filing a version of the war that fit between the weather and the sport, I had wanted to go in on my own terms and stay long enough to see something true. The postcard did not send me. It only told me where to go once I was there.

I drove out on a Tuesday. The west bank had been retaken that summer and most of the checkpoints there were down, but once I crossed the river the road changed, and there were long stretches where you couldn't tell what year it was, only that no one had cut the verges. Pylons on their sides in the fields. A village with all its doors propped open. A quiet that didn't quite feel empty.

The farm was on a slight rise above what had once been a road. The barn was older than the house. Wooden, sagging at one corner, long past saving and not yet fallen. A woman watched me from the kitchen window as I parked. She did not come out.

Inside the barn it smelled of cold and damp and old hay, and something sweeter I couldn't place. I found what she had left me on a table in the middle of the floor, under a piece of grey canvas, weighted with a brick at each corner, like she had thought about wind.

The notebooks were not under the canvas with the rest. They had been wrapped on their own, in oilcloth, and set on top, four of them, the kind schoolchildren use, the cardboard covers warped with damp at the edges where the wrapping had not quite held. She had protected those before anything else. A person tells you what mattered to them by what they wrap twice. I knew before I opened them that this was the thing, and that the rest, however much there was of it, was the rest.

Under the canvas, then, the body of it. Three binders. A box of cassette tapes and some memory cards. A canvas roll of photographs. And laid across the notebooks, where I would find it first, a manila folder with my name on it in her handwriting, the same deliberate style, slanted forward and underlined twice.

Inside the folder, a single sheet.

*Alex. These are the protocols. They are not finished and they were never going to be. Do not come looking for me. Make them public. Tell them what they did. Tell them what we did. D.*

I sat down on an old chair and read the first page. Then I went out to the car, brought in the thermos, and read the second. The light through the barn boards moved while I sat.

What she had assembled was not a book. It was a survival. Ten voices, give or take, recorded between the second flood and the second winter. A man called Goya whose mouth never closed around the words he most needed. A girl who had stopped speaking the year her parents were taken. An engineer who could not sit still. A musician who had killed once. Diana had written notes against each transcript in the same pencil she used for everything, small and slanted forward, that was it, sometimes a question, sometimes only a word. *True. Hold. Ask again.* On a few pages she had written nothing at all.

Between the testimonies she had inserted what she called Protocols. Five of them. Cold documents. Lists, mostly. Maps. The names of streets that no longer existed under names that had not been used since 1944. A taxonomy of factions. A taxonomy of the dead. A pharmacology of the thing they called the Dendron.

When I drove out that evening the woman in the kitchen window was gone. The light was on. I did not knock.

The road to Łuków went on a long time and I drove most of it without seeing it. I had her voice in my head the whole way. The cadence of her notes. The small slanted D. *Make them public. Tell them what they did. Tell them what we did.*

That is what this book is.

I have changed nothing in the testimonies except the things she asked me to change. The protocols are reproduced as she left them. The order is hers. I have written nothing of my own except what is mine to write, which is this, and what comes between.

Diana Antonoff disappeared from the survivor outpost at Czerniaków on the night of the third flood, in the first January of the occupation's third year. She was twenty-eight years old. She lived alone on an abandoned houseboat moored at Żerań, at the northern edge of the wetlands, where the others said the water ran quietest. She had filed her last dispatch to Suspilne the previous Tuesday. No body was recovered. No statement was issued. The community she had documented filed her name among the missing and went on with the work she had taught them.

Nine months later, the postcard came.

If she is alive, she has chosen not to be found.

## Prologue: The Barn

**ALEX** · 2036

I met her once.

People assume I knew her well, because of the book, because of the notes in her hand and the way I write about her. I didn't. I met her once, for half an hour, at four in the morning at a farm in the grey zone south of the city, in the last days before she went in, and then I never saw her again.

I was heading south on the ZAC route, out of the zone. She was heading north into it. Alone. No guides, no scouts, no one who knew she was coming.

The grey zone then was a stretch of country maybe sixty kilometres deep between Warsaw and Lublin where neither side held anything and so both sides left it. On the maps it was nothing, white space, a few road numbers. On the ground it was farmland going back to itself. Wheat that no one had cut standing dead and silver in the fields. Whole villages with the power long gone, the houses dark, the occasional dog that had learned to live without people and watched you from a doorway without barking. You moved through it at night and lay up in the day, because the day was when the drones flew and the open ground between one treeline and the next was a thing you crossed quickly or not at all. The farms along the route were the daylight shelters. You held up in them until the light made the fields too dangerous, and you slept if you could, and you went on when the dark came back.

This one was a low brick farmhouse off the road behind a windbreak of poplars, the yard grown up with nettles, a collapsed tractor shed at the back. I had been there since before first light. There were four or five others I half knew, runners mostly, and we had taken the inner rooms where you could not be seen from outside and put someone on the window in shifts. I had been asleep maybe an hour when she came in.

I heard her before I saw her. The door, and then a voice, low, telling whoever was on the window who she was and why she was alone, and something in the voice made me sit up. It was too steady. People came through the grey zone frightened or they came through it numb, and she was neither.

She looked young. Younger than she was, I think now, though it was hard to tell by the one shielded lamp we allowed ourselves. A backpack half her size, set down against the wall with a care that told you what was in it mattered. When she opened it to find something I saw camera batteries, a great brick of them wrapped in cloth, more than seemed sensible, more than seemed survivable. Energy drinks. Those gels you slurp from a sachet, and she ate one while we talked and then another, one after the next, the way a child eats sweets. Fresh-faced. She looked like she had wandered in from a different decade, one that still had university campuses and deadlines and somewhere warm to go home to.

She looked innocent.

She wasn't.

We talked for half an hour, in the low voice you learn to use in those rooms, sitting against the wall with the grey coming up slow in the one window we hadn't blacked. I told her what was up there, or what I knew of it, which by then was already weeks out of date. The outposts that had gone quiet. The crossings that were watched now and the ones that weren't yet. I told her the rumours about the Dendron, the things people said about the water and what it did to a person who spent too long near it. The flatness that came into them. The way they stopped minding.

She didn't dismiss them. That is the thing I remember most clearly. She didn't laugh them off and she didn't go pale either. She just took them in, the way you take in a weather report for a place you have already decided to travel to. *If it's there, I'll find it and I'll document it and I'll tell the truth.* Not a boast. She said it the way you'd say you were going to the shop. Closer to a shrug.

I asked her, I think, who she was filing for. She told me. I asked if they knew she was coming in without anyone. She said something I don't remember exactly, but the sense of it was that people who waited for permission didn't get the story.

I thought at the time, ironically, if anyone's going to get the story, it'll be her.

Then the light was up and there was nothing to do but wait out the day, and at some point I slept again, properly this time, the way you sleep when you've decided a place is as safe as it is going to get.

When I woke to head south she was already gone.

She must have left at dusk, north, while I was still under. The corner where she had slept was immaculate. The blanket she'd been given was folded square and set on the sill. No wrapper, no print in the dust, no sign that anyone had been there at all, and I remember standing over that empty corner with the strange feeling that I had dreamed her, that there had been no girl with the batteries and the gels and the steady voice, only the cold coming up through the brick floor.

On the floor, where I would be sure to see it, she had left a note. A scrap torn from a notebook, folded once.

Just my name. And a number.

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 1 June 2034

## Diary 001: The Arrival

**SY/DA/0001\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date:** 1 June 2034, 21:50*

***Location: **houseboat, near bank of the Żerań canal, across from the power plant*

***Conditions:** clear, mist rising off the canal at dusk, no wind*

***Temperature:** 11°C*

***Pressure: **1018 hPa, steady*

***Flood: **stable. Waterline reported unchanged nine days.*

I'm in.

I did the last stretch by water, before first light, lying flat in the bottom of a reed boat while a man I will never see again worked the oars without sound. I had thought I would be frightened on the water and I was not. The mist sat on the canal the way breath sits on glass. The reeds went on a long time. When the light began the far bank came up out of it green, very green, greener than anything has a right to be in a drowned city, and I lay there and looked at it and thought, foolishly, that it was almost beautiful. The water beneath the green is dark and there is an oil to it that catches the light wrong. I have written that down and I do not yet know what it means. The chimney of the power station stood up out of the mist further north and took the first of the sun while everything under it was still grey.

They were waiting where the reeds give out. Two of them. Scouts. I had crossed sixty kilometres of open country alone, slept in barns, read the ground, and within a hundred metres of their water I was handed from one keeper to another like a parcel that had been expected. I understand it. I don't have to like it. They asked me three questions, told me four rules, and did not introduce themselves. We walked.

Getting to the OP took most of the morning and I will set down what I saw because that is the work. A city with the colour drained out of it. Whole streets gone under and the streets that remain the colour of weather, grey on grey, the render off the buildings, the glass long gone. Steam coming up out of the road in places where the plant still pushes heat through the old pipes, so that you pass through warm patches and cold ones, and the warm ones smell of rust and something underneath the rust. Hardly anyone. A face at a window. A man mending an engine who did not look up. The faces that did turn to me were not hostile. They were sullen in a way I have seen before and could not place, a flatness, as if the muscles that make an expression had been let go. I noted it. I will watch for it.

OP-Żerań came alive around me the moment we arrived, which after the dead streets was a shock. I had expected, I think, something medieval. People scratching at the mud. What I walked into was the opposite. There was a radio, and it was not a salvaged household thing but something built, an assembly of boards and scavenged parts and hand-wound coils mounted in a gutted instrument case, and a boy of about twelve had it open and was doing something to its inside with a tool he had clearly made himself. Cable ran up the stairwell and out toward the chimney. There was power here, routed and managed, in a city where I had not seen a working light in sixty kilometres. It was all made of other things. Nothing was what it had been built to be. I have seen field hospitals in Ukraine but I have never seen anything improvised at this level, and I stood in the doorway longer than was polite, recalibrating what kind of place I had come to. Not primitive. The reverse. Something had taught these people to be very, very clever with very little, and I'm not yet sure whether that something was the war or the thing in the water.

The scouts handed me up the chain and there was a discussion about me I was not part of, and at the end of it a woman took me out and showed me where I was to live, and it was not at the OP at all. To reach my boat you leave Żerań and go south, down toward the Vistula, and cross at the one bridge they still hold, and come back up the wrong side of the water through the trees. An hour, maybe more, for a place I could throw a stone at from the dock if the stone could cross water. The boat sits low on the near bank in a tangle of green so thick you are on top of it before you see it, willow and reed and something else with broad pale leaves that has come up through everything since the floods, and you would pass within twenty metres and never know a hull was there, never know a person was. One other boat lies moored further along that I think has someone in it. Otherwise the green has it all.

And yet I can see them from my deck. That is the strange part. Across the water the chimneys of the power plant stand up plain against the sky, striped red and white, smoking, the whole working heart of this place in full view, and at night I will hear the hum of it carry across. Hidden in the trees and in plain sight of everything. I understood from the way she showed me the boat that it was a thing of value, the safest moorage they had, and that I was meant to feel the size of the gift. I did. I thanked her properly. What I did not say is that they had set me as far from themselves as the zone allows, where the green closes over a person and the water keeps them, in sight of the OP and an hour's hard walk from a single soul, and that I couldn't yet tell whether that was trust or its opposite. Then she went back toward the bridge and the light went down toward evening and it was done. I was given. I was placed. I was, after all that distance under my own power, somewhere I had been put.

The boat has not been lived in for a long time. Stepping down into it was like stepping into a held breath. Everything as someone left it, years ago, before any of this, a cup on its side, a calendar on the wall with a month on it that means nothing now. Dust standing in the long bar of sun that comes through the western window, turning slowly, going nowhere. The bed is a settee that folds out and the cover on it has gone to threads, soft as moth-wing, and when I sat down on it the give of it under me and the smell of the room, dust and damp wood and old fabric and the river, undid something I had been holding all the way from the border. I haven't been this tired in a long time. The far chimney hums. Under the hum, when the air moves, there is another sound I can't account for, lower, that comes and goes, almost like a great many people groaning together a long way off, and then the breeze drops and it is gone and there is only the hum and the water against the hull.

It is the plant, probably. Or the wind in the structures. I will find out.

I am writing this before I sleep because if I don't write it now I'll sleep for a day and lose the shape of it, and the shape is the thing. My father kept his log every night of his life no matter the hour, the readings first, always the readings first, then the day. I have the readings above. Now I have the day. Tomorrow I begin.

## Chapter 1: The Blasphemer

**ALEX** · Gdańsk

Goya was the hardest of them.

I had been warned. Diana's note against his transcript was a single word, repeated, which I had not seen her do anywhere else. *Careful. Careful.* As if she had written it once, come back a day later, and written it again to be sure the warning held.

I found him at a veterans' facility outside Gdańsk, a low building that had been a sanatorium once and still had the long glassed corridors of one, men sitting along them in the afternoon light, not talking. He was at the end of the last corridor with his back to the wall, which I would learn was the only way he sat, in any room, for any reason. He did not want to talk to me. He told me so in terms I will not reproduce here, not out of delicacy but because to set them down flat on the page is to lose the thing that mattered about them, which was not their content but their function. He swore the way other men breathe. It was not anger. I understood that much within a minute. It was a wall, and he built it fast, and he built it high, and he had clearly built it many times before, against many people who had wanted things from him and been sent away.

I had come a long way to be sent away. I knew I had one card and that I would only get to play it once.

I put my recorder back in my bag, where he could see me do it. Then I told him I had been to see Janek.

He went quiet. The swearing simply stopped, the way a machine stops, mid-cycle, and the corridor was suddenly very large around us. Then he said, in a completely different voice, almost a boy's voice, *is he alright? *And the wall came down. Not all at once. Not all the way. But enough.

What follows is most of what he gave me over the two days after that, set against what Diana had recorded of him two years before, at the other end of his war. I have taken nothing out except the names of people still in the zone.

A word on her entries. Diana headed every diary page the same way, with the date, the place, the weather, the state of the water, set down in a fixed order before she let herself write a single line of what she had seen. An editor told me to cut them. I have kept them exactly as she made them. You do not understand her until you understand that she could not begin to tell you about a man until she had first told you the barometric pressure, and that this was not coldness. It was how she loved a thing. She measured it.

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 14 June 2034

**SY/GL/0002\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date:** 14 June 2034, 05:40*

***Location:** the wetlands, vicinity OP-Żerań*

***Conditions:** low cloud, mist off the water, no wind*

***Temperature:** 11°C*

***Pressure:** 1018 hPa, steady*

***Flood:** stable. Waterline unchanged four days.*

*He found me before I found anyone.*

*Three days I have been in the wetlands, sleeping rough, moving at night, certain I was being clever and certain no one knew I was here. This morning a man was simply sitting on the boat I had been using, a long way from where I had left it, cleaning a weapon, and he said without looking up: you walk like someone who thinks the ground is on her side.*

*I have met a great many dangerous men. The dangerous ones are usually loud. This one is quiet in a way that rearranges the air. He seemed to know where every Runner in the wetlands was at that hour. He told me which of the chimneys at Żerań still had a watcher on it and which did not, and he glanced at the tall one, the power station, the way you would glance at a clock. He had read the southern culvert off the mud on my boots, and he told me so not to frighten me but the way you would correct a child's grammar, mildly, so that I do better next time. He swore the whole while and at everything: the boat, the weapon, the sky, the dead, God, his own hands. After an hour of it I stopped hearing the words as words. They became weather.*

*He is the most damaged person I have met here, and I think the most necessary, and I don’t** yet know whether those are two facts or one.*

*\[later, in pencil: the truest thing I have written down. underlined twice.\]*

\* \* \*

**GOYA** · recording, Żerań

**SY/GL/0001\_GR** · *Goya Liszka, recorded at Żerań*

You want to know about the swearing. Everyone wants to know about the swearing. The girl wanted to know too. She was too polite to ask it straight out, but I could see it sitting in her the whole first week, behind the questions she did ask.

Alright. For her, then. Not for you.

In Mariupol, at the end, there were ten of us in a cellar under what had been a bakery. You could still smell the flour in the brick. That's a true thing. Under everything else that came later, under all of it, you could still smell bread. We held that position past the point where holding it meant anything to anyone above us, because the radio had stopped and we didn't know there was no one left to hold it for. When the shell came through, I was at the back. The back is the reason I'm sitting here using up your batteries. I won't tell you what the front of that room was. I lay in it three days. You understand me. I lay there three days, because to move was to be heard, and to be heard was to finish what the shell had started.

Three days is a long time to lie still next to men you ate with.

And I found, lying there, that there was a thing I could do with my mouth. Very quiet. Barely a breath. A thing that kept me on this side of it. Not prayers. I had no prayers left by then, they had gone out of me somewhere in the second year, I couldn't have told you the day. What I had left was the other words. The hard ones. I said them over and over into the floor, every filthy word I owned, in three languages, and each one was a small hammer, and with each one I was driving a nail, fixing myself to the floor of the world so that I would not float up off it and out of it and be gone like the rest. God's name was in the middle of all of it, over and over. But I wasn't asking him for anything. I was long past asking. I was using the name to hold the door shut.

People hear me now and they think I hold nothing sacred.

I hold exactly one thing sacred, and it's this: that I am here, and the nine of us are not, and that every word out of my mouth is me saying so, out loud, every hour I am awake, so that I never once forget what it cost to be the man at the back of the room. You can call that blasphemy if you like. The priest would. The priest did. Janek never did.

Janek heard me at it, early, when I first came into the zone and not one outpost would have me because of what I'm like. He sat down next to me on the dock at Żerań, this little man who owned nothing in the world but a coat and a book, and he listened to me curse God for ten minutes without a break, and when I finally ran dry he said: *that's the most honest prayer I've heard in years.*

That's a thing I've told no one. I'm telling you because you went and sat with him, and because he's alright, you say, and a man should know when something he did landed somewhere.

I came to Warsaw because there was nowhere left in the world that would take a man like me. Every camp, every column, they take one look. He's trouble. He doesn't sleep. He watches the door. And they were right, every one of them. But the zone didn't care what I was like. The zone only cared what I could do, and what I could do was hear a drone in the air before anyone else had lifted their head, and read a man's route off the mud on his boots, and keep the little ones moving through ground that ate grown soldiers whole. The Ducks. The Runners. Whatever they're calling them this year. The children who carry what the radios can't when the Russians jam the air, who go where a boat would be seen and a man would be shot.

In Mariupol I couldn't save nine men in a cellar.

Here I have lost none. Not one of mine taken, in all the years, not one drowned, not one walked into a patrol because I read the night wrong. You write that down exactly. Of everything that has ever come out of this mouth, in any language, that's the only sentence that's ever sounded to me like a prayer. And there's no God anywhere in it.

*(end of recording)*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · Gdańsk

I left him at the end of the second day. He walked me as far as the gate, which the staff told me afterwards he had not done for any visitor before, and at the gate he didn’t say goodbye. He said, *the girl. Diana.* He had not used her name once in two days. He asked me if it was true she was gone, and I said that it was, that no one had found her, and he nodded slowly, as if I had only confirmed a thing he had carried a long time and set down at last.

Then he said one more thing, and I have thought about it since more than I have thought about almost anything else he told me. He said she had asked him once how he stood it, carrying the nine of them everywhere, every day, never putting them down. And he had told her: you do not carry the dead by holding them. You carry them by making something they would not be ashamed of. A route kept clear. A child handed alive to the far bank.

He said she had written it down, there on the dock, the way she wrote everything down. And then she had said a strange thing back to him, almost to herself. She had said that not everyone got to choose what they carried the dead in. That some people only had pictures.

I did not understand it then. I understood it later, when I reached the second of her protocols, and met a girl who had nothing left of her family in all the world but a roll of photographs, and could not put them down.

## Interlude I: Wiktor Szeliga, Block C

*What follows is a reconstruction, and I should say at the outset of what sort. Wiktor Szeliga gave no testimony and kept no diary. He left a trace in four places: a ration ledger, kept in pencil by the block warden for the whole of that summer; a transcript of the Palace loudspeaker made by Magda Wiśniewska; nine minutes of Diana's tape, which I have used once, and say where; and a neighbour, who gave me her account in Lublin after the war. The ledger gives numbers. The transcript gives a voice he heard twice a day. The neighbour gives the rest, and I do not take her at her word, because she is kind, and kindness improves a story. I never met him. I have therefore written one day of his life in his voice, in the present tense, since a column of figures is not a man. Wherever I leave his voice for my own, the passage is in italics and in brackets, headed by its source: ledger, transcript, tape, neighbour, or mine where I have supplied it. Where the documents are silent I have kept silent too. Where they are not, I have copied. Everything in his head is mine, and I have tried to keep it as flat as he would have.*

I wake at 06:30 without the alarm. The alarm is a travel clock with a luminous dial, a Swiss movement in a brushed steel case, battery type AA, and it has not been wound or replaced since the second flood. I do not need it. I wake at 06:30 because the light in the east-facing window reaches the third tile from the left of the bathroom wall at 06:30, and I have noticed this, and it is a fact I am happy to have.

*\[Mine. The clock, the tile and the hour. The ledger does not record when a man wakes, and the neighbour was never in his bathroom. She told me only that he was a man of order, and that you could set a watch by his door. I have given him a morning to match.\]*

The flat is on the fourth floor of Block C, number 41, two rooms and a kitchen, sixty-one square metres. The stairwell has a tide line at the height of the second step, a brown band, perfectly level, edged with a thin white crust of salt. Beneath it the paint is a green that was chosen in 1974 by a committee. Above it the paint is cream. I count the stairs every morning. There are twenty-two between the landings. I count them going down and I count them coming up, and the numbers agree, and this is quite satisfying.

I wash at 06:40. The water comes from the standpipe in the yard in a twelve-litre plastic container with a screw cap and a handle moulded in the shape of a hand. It is the temperature of the yard, which this morning is 17 degrees. I use one litre. I heat none of it. I shave with a safety razor, two-blade, a German product, a chromed head with a rubberised grip, and I have had the same cartridge since the thirtieth of June. It is not as sharp as it was. I note this. It is within tolerance.

I put on the grey trousers, the white shirt, the grey cardigan. I had eleven white shirts. I have nine. I gave two to the warden for bandages. I would have given more. She said that two was enough. I said that I was quite happy to give more. She looked at me for a time and wrote something in the ledger.

*\[Ledger. The two shirts are in it, entered against his name as bandage linen, and beside them, in the same pencil, the word enough. The look that comes before it is mine.\]*

At 07:00 I look in on my mother.

She is in the back room, in the bed with the high rails, on the mattress with the alternating air cells, which has not alternated since the power went. She is eighty-three. She has been in that bed for three years and four months. The room has a smell that I will describe because it is a fact: it is the smell of a pharmacy that has been left open to the damp, with something sweeter beneath it, like fruit in a bowl at the point just before it is no longer fruit. There is a sore at the base of her spine, the size of a saucer, with an edge the colour of a plum and a floor that is yellow and grey and has, in places, the dull white of something that is no longer flesh. It was a Stage 2 sore in May. It is a Stage 4 sore now. I know the staging. I used to turn her every two hours. I turned her at 02:00 and 04:00 and 06:00 and so on for three years, and I did it, I will say, quite well.

*\[Neighbour, and mine. She never saw the sore. She knew the bed, the rails and the three years, and she knew the smell, which she said came under his door and along the corridor. The staging is the ordinary staging. The sore itself is mine. I have described it because a man who says he is quite all right beside it is the whole of this day.\]*

I have not turned her since Thursday.

I do not know why. I have thought about it. I have the clearest sense that I could turn her, that there is nothing in the way, that the mechanics are all as they were. I stand by the rail with my hand on it, a cold hand on cold tubular steel, powder-coated, white, and I wait for something to follow the thought, and nothing does, and after a time I find that I have gone to the kitchen.

She looks at me. Her eyes are very blue. She says my name, Wiktor, and then she says it again, a little differently. I say that I am quite all right, and that she is not to worry.

At 07:20 I eat. Ration, today: 180 grams of bread, grey, with a crust like a plank; 20 grams of margarine, white; one tin of mackerel in tomato sauce shared between three days, which makes a third of a tin. The tin is 125 grams, oval, with a pull ring that has been tied with wire, and a label in three colours, red, cream and a blue that I think of as a swimming-pool blue. The label shows a fish. The fish is not a mackerel. It is some other fish, a sleeker one, rendered by a designer who had not, I believe, seen a mackerel. I like the label. I have looked at it every morning for fifty-one days.

I cut the bread into nine slices of equal thickness. I eat three. I put one by for the girl. I put one by for my mother, though she has not been taking it, and the slice from yesterday is on the plate, curled at the corners like a dry leaf.

The girl is Marta. She is nine. She is my sister's child and she came to me in June, when her mother went across the river, and she sits at the kitchen table under the window with her hands flat on the oilcloth. The oilcloth has a pattern of lemons. There are thirty-two lemons in each repeat. For the first two weeks she asked me a great many questions. Where was her mother. Why was the tap dry. Why did I not go out. Why did the lady next door not answer. On the sixth day she asked whether it was all right that she was not hungry, and I said that it was quite all right. She has not asked me anything since. It is now the fifty-first day and she has eaten, by my own record, eleven slices of bread in the last nine days. I note it in pencil on the back of the electricity bill. It is within tolerance.

*\[Ledger and neighbour. The count in his flat goes from two persons to three in June, in the warden's pencil, with a child's age beside the new line. The questions she asked are the neighbour's, who heard them through the wall. The lemons are hers, though the number is his. She remembered the oilcloth better than she remembered the child.\]*

At 08:00 the loudspeaker begins on the west bank. It is two and a half kilometres to the Palace and the sound is thin by the time it reaches the window, but the voice is clear, a woman's voice, warm in the lower register, with a very slight roughness at the edge of the vowels, as of someone who has been awake for a long time and is not minding. She speaks in Polish. She says: *Good morning. The shelters on the left bank are open. There are beds. There is hot water. There is no need to be afraid.* Then there is a pause that I time at four seconds, and she says it again.

I have heard it four hundred and thirty times. I find that I know where she breathes.

*\[Transcript. Magda Wiśniewska's. The words of the morning and evening broadcasts are hers, and I have not altered them. The pause is on her page at four seconds. The count of four hundred and thirty is mine, and so is the breathing. A man who counts stairs would count this.\]*

At 09:00 I go to the yard.

The yard has forty-one people in it, which is the number the warden recorded, because I have kept a count of the numbers she recorded, and it has been forty-one, forty-three, forty-one, forty for a month. They are standing in the queue for the standpipe. They do not talk. They stand at the interval that has become customary, about eighty centimetres, which is a little more than an arm. A man in a blue anorak is holding his container in front of him in both hands, like a priest with a vessel. A woman is looking at the wall. The wall is a gable end of grey render with a faded mural of a sailing ship, in white and ochre, and a slogan I cannot read, and she has been looking at it, I am told, since Saturday.

There is a green growth on the lower courses of the gable. It is a pale green, almost grey, the colour of the underside of a sage leaf, and it grows in a pattern that is not quite a pattern. It runs along the drainpipe in a vine the width of a finger and goes in at the grating at the base of the wall, and from the grating, very faintly, in the warm air, there comes a sound.

It is a hum. It is lower than the generator and slower than the refrigerator I used to own. It has a beat to it, about once every six seconds, a slow swelling and an ebbing, like a breath. I do not mind it. It is the kind of sound that you are only aware of when it stops, and it does not stop. Every resident of the block has been asked by a young woman with a notebook whether they can hear it, and every one of them has said no.

*\[Neighbour. Every resident was asked whether they could hear it, and every one said no. She counted herself among them, and then, in the same breath, told me that she could. I have kept the hum because, in the end, she admitted it.\]*

I could say no too. I can hear it perfectly well. It would not seem to matter either way.

The young woman with the notebook came on the twelfth. She was twenty-eight, small, dark, in a waterproof jacket the colour of a traffic cone, with a satchel on a strap and a face that was too attentive. She spoke Polish with a foreign accent and she spoke it very carefully. She asked if she might sit. I said that she might. She sat on the kitchen chair that is not Marta's and she held the notebook shut on her knee, and she did not ask anything for a long time. I made tea. I used the good cups. There are six of them, white bone china, with a gold line at the rim and a pattern of small blue flowers, a service for six which I bought on a wet Saturday in 2019 from a shop on Targowa that has since closed, and I gave her the cup with the least wear.

She said: *How are you, Pan Wiktor?*

I said that I was quite all right.

She looked at me. I had the impression that it was the answer she had been told to expect, and that she had hoped not to be given it. She looked at Marta, who was at the table, and at the plate of curled bread, and at the door of the back room, which was shut, and from behind it came my mother's voice, saying my name. She said nothing about any of this. She drank the tea. She said that it was very good. It was. I had used the last of the Assam.

When she stood to go she opened the notebook, and I watched her not write in it. She held a pencil over the page for what I should estimate to be eleven seconds. Then she closed it. I have thought about this since, in so far as I think about anything. It was an odd thing to do. It was a courtesy, I believe. I would not have been able to say what it was a courtesy to.

*\[Tape and neighbour. This is the one place I have used Diana's tape. It gives the visit, the tea, and the page she did not write on, and the eleven seconds are hers. The jacket, the satchel and the care with her Polish are the neighbour's, who watched her go up the stairs. What the courtesy was to him, he could not have said, and I cannot say it for him.\]*

At 11:00 the warden comes. Pani Ostrowska, 64, a retired school secretary, with a clipboard in a plastic wallet and a ballpoint on a string. She stops at each door in the corridor. She knocks twice. I have the door open before the second knock. She asks the standard questions in the standard order and I give the standard answers. Number of persons: three, one of them non-ambulant. Water: adequate. Food: adequate. Fuel: adequate. Health: quite all right. She writes. I watch the ballpoint. It is a blue ballpoint, a cheap one, with a clear barrel, and the ink is running low, and the last two lines of each entry are fainter than the first.

*\[Ledger. The clipboard sheet does not survive. What survives is the ledger, into which she copied each day's sheet in the evening, in pencil, in a hand that does not change all summer. The questions are printed at the head of every page, and the answers are as she wrote them. I have not altered one. The ballpoint is the neighbour's: she remembers that it was running out.\]*

She says that the leaflet has been dropped again. I say that I have seen it. She says that it is very clear, and quite generous, and that several people in the block have been thinking about it. I say that I have been thinking about it too.

The leaflet is on the table. It is a single sheet of A5, printed on both sides on a matt coated stock, in a clean sans-serif. On the front there is a photograph of a bed. It is a single bed with white linen, and a blue blanket folded at the foot, and beside it a small table with a glass of water and a lamp. The light in the photograph is the light of eleven in the morning in a room with a north window. On the back there is a list of seven items, which I have by heart. Shelter. Warm food. Medical screening. Clean water. Family accommodation. Transport. Registration, which takes four minutes.

I have read it forty times. I have tried to find the thing in it that is wrong. The paper is good. The wording is clear. The offer is, so far as I am able to tell, exactly what it says. There is nothing wrong with it.

*\[Neighbour. She kept one of the leaflets, and showed it to me in Lublin. The seven items are on it, and the photograph of the bed. The forty readings are his. I have read it a good many times myself, and I can tell you that she was right to keep it. There is nothing wrong with it.\]*

I think that I will go tomorrow. I have not decided. It simply seems to be the case that I will.

At 12:30 I sit. I sit in the armchair by the window with my hands on my knees and I do not read, and I do not look at the clock, and I do not think about the kitchen equipment, though for nineteen years I sold it, and sold it well, and could tell you the gauge of any stainless steel worktop in the region at a glance. Eighteen gauge. Sixteen for the heavy duty. I find I can still say it. I say it, silently. Eighteen. Sixteen. It is a pleasant thing, to have a fact that stays where you put it.

At 15:00 Marta says something. It is the first thing she has said since the sixth day. She says, *Wujek.* Uncle. I say, yes. She says nothing more. I wait. I wait for four minutes, and I find that I am watching her mouth, and that there is a small movement at the corner of it, as of a word that has been started and withdrawn. Then she looks at the lemons on the oilcloth.

I say that it is quite all right.

At 18:00 I go to the back room. My mother is awake. She says: *Wiktor, is it the girl?* I say that it is. She says: *Wiktor, there is something wrong with you.* She says it quite clearly, in the voice she used when I was nine and had said I had done my homework.

I stand by the rail. I notice the colour of the light on the wall, a yellow with some grey in it, and the position of the shadow of the curtain rail, which lies across the bed in two parallel bars and across her hand. Her hand is on the blanket. It is a small hand, with the knuckles swollen, and a ring, a thin gold band, loose on the finger. I look at it for a long time.

I am aware, in some part of me, of an enormous quantity of something, behind a glass, a long way down. I have the clearest sense of its size. I have no sense whatever of how to open the glass. And the hum goes on, from the grating, from the yard, a slow swelling and an ebbing, and the glass seems very thick, and the thing behind it grows, in the unhurried way of something that has been told that it has all the time in the world.

I tell her that I am quite all right.

*\[Mine. The glass, and what is behind it, and the sentence his mother says. The neighbour heard the old woman call his name through the wall most evenings, and heard nothing else. The rest I have supplied, knowing I may be wrong. I would rather he had a mother who said something to him than none.\]*

At 20:00 the loudspeaker. *Good evening. The shelters on the left bank are open. There are beds.*

At 21:15 I wind the travel clock, which does not need winding. I set the alarm for 06:30. I lay out the grey trousers, the white shirt, the grey cardigan. I turn down the blanket on the girl's bed, and I turn down my own. I check the door. It is locked. I check it again. It is locked.

I sleep at 22:00 and I sleep very well.

§&#32;

The day above is the fourteenth of August. What follows is the fifteenth, which the ledger records, and then what I have supposed. From here the voice is mine, not his.

*Wiktor Szeliga crossed the Vistula on the fifteenth. The warden's ledger records it in the left-hand column in pencil: one adult male, ambulant; one female child, nine, ambulant. The line for the non-ambulant person has been ruled through. There is no entry beside it. I asked the neighbour what became of the old woman, and she said that someone had gone up on the Thursday, and that it was very quiet, and that the room had been tidied. I asked who had tidied it. She said that she did not know. She said that the bed had been made.*

I chose the fourteenth because it is the last whole day of his that the documents hold, and because the neighbour remembers it better than any other. She says that is because nothing happened. I have thought about that more than about anything he does. The man between the ledger and the neighbour is mine. She is kind, and she told me the bed had been made, and I have written it down, and I do not know that it is true. I would not have him mistaken for a witness.

## Chapter 2: The Photographer

**ALEX** · Berlin, November 2035

Fritha would not let me photograph her.

I should say at once that this was not vanity. She was twenty, perhaps twenty-one by the time I found her, and she had the kind of face that photographs well and knows it and has decided to do something about it. She worked in the basement of a museum in Berlin, in a long room kept at eighteen degrees and fifty per cent humidity, with a steel table down the middle and a lamp on a jointed arm, and she spent her days with the testimonies of the people who had survived the last occupation of Europe but one. Reels. Wire recordings. A shoebox of glass negatives from a village that no longer had a name on any map. Her job was to make them last. She wore white cotton gloves to handle the paper, and she took them off to shake my hand, which I noticed, and which I think she meant me to notice.

I put my camera on the table between us. She looked at it the way you look at a dog that has been known to bite.

"Not today," she said. "If you want to keep it in the bag, that's fine. If you want to take it out, I'll leave the room."

I kept it in the bag. We spoke for most of three afternoons, and on the third she went to a drawer and came back with a single print, a small square thing on proper paper, and I saw that she had photographed me while I was not looking, at the steel table, with my hands round a paper cup and my mouth a little open, in the act of being about to ask something. It was the best likeness anyone has taken of me. I still have it&#32;&#32;

"That's yours," she said. "So you can stop trying to remember what you looked like when you were listening. I'll keep the negative. No. Actually, you keep the negative as well."

I understood later that she had thought about what it would cost her to give me both.

I should describe the room, since I spent so long in it and since it explains her better than anything she said.

It was a long low basement, a former storage vault of the museum, with a barrel ceiling of white-painted brick and a floor of grey linoleum that had been laid in the 1960s and polished into a kind of shallow lake. The temperature and the humidity were displayed on two small digital panels by the door, side by side, in red figures, and a fan in the ceiling turned a quarter of a revolution every few seconds, with a sigh. The steel table ran down the middle. On it, in the three afternoons, I saw the following: a reel-to-reel machine under a perspex hood; a tray of cotton buds; a bottle of ethanol, a bottle of distilled water, a bottle of something with a skull on the label; a magnifier on a goose-neck; a sheet of blotting paper with a single glass negative on it, face up, an inch from the edge; a tin of white cotton gloves, from which she drew a fresh pair every time she moved from one object to another, so that the bin by her foot filled up over the afternoon with soft pale hands.

The negative was of a woman in a doorway. It was a small plate, the size of a playing card, and the woman in it was holding a child, and the child was looking away. Fritha was cleaning it with a swab on a stick, working across the emulsion in one direction only, from the left, and rolling the swab as she went so that it never touched the same patch twice. I watched her for forty minutes. She did not speak, nor did she seem to breathe, and when she lifted the swab at last and looked at the cotton, it was grey. She held it up to me. "That's eighty years," she said. "That's what it looks like."

I asked how she decided what to keep.

"We don't decide," she said. "The museum decides. I make it last."

It was a very good answer, and I wrote it down, and on the second afternoon I found out that it was not true.

She had gone upstairs, to take a call from the director, and she had left the lamp on and the drawer open. I do not make a practice of looking into things. I looked. It was a flat steel drawer in a plan chest, the deep sort, and in it, laid out in rows, each in its own sleeve of glassine, were photographs. Not the museum's. Not archival. They were prints of every size, snapshots and passport booth strips and cut-out pages from magazines, and they were arranged with great care by some principle I could not at first see. A boy on a bicycle. A wedding in front of a car. A woman in a headscarf holding up a fish. Two men at a table, laughing, with a bottle between them. A baby in a bath. They had been found, I realised, and kept. Some had been taken from albums in the museum's own waste, some from skips, some from the dust of a house clearance; one still had the corner of a sticky label on the back that said *Lot 41*. I counted a hundred and ninety before I stopped. In the drawer below there were more.

There was a card in the front of each row. The cards were in her handwriting, and each had a word on it. *Unclaimed. Unclaimed. Unclaimed. Unclaimed.*

I shut the drawer, as quietly as I could. I was standing at the other side of the table, with my back to it and the hood of the tape deck in my hand, when she came down the stairs.

She looked at me. She looked at the drawer. It was shut, to within a finger's width, as it had been left. I have thought since that she would have known if I had moved it a hair, the way a person knows that their coat has been touched.

"You saw," she said.

I said that I had.

"Then I'll tell you the rule," she said, without any change in her voice. "It isn't a rule of the museum. It's mine. A photograph that isn't claimed isn't safe. It's a thing without a name that anyone may throw away. So I take it. I give it a sleeve. I put it in a drawer at eighteen degrees, with a card, and no one may throw it away, because it is mine. That is all."

I said that it sounded like a kind of adoption.

"It isn't a kind of anything," she said. "It's a drawer." She took off her gloves. She put on a fresh pair. She sat down, with her back very straight, and for a time she said nothing at all; and then she said, in a voice with more in it than she had intended, "Nobody has ever looked, you know. In two years."

I waited.

"I thought somebody would. I thought it would be the director. I used to have a dream that he would come in, one night, and open it, and say, what is this, and I would have to say. And I would say, they are people. And he would say, they are pictures of people. And I would say, no." She stopped. "That's as far as the dream goes."

I said that it was none of my business.

"It's exactly your business," said Fritha. "It's why you're here." She tipped her head at my camera, which sat zipped in its bag on the chair beside me. "You want to make a picture of someone who has lost something. And I'm telling you that I won't be one. I won't be a picture. I'd rather be a drawer."

In the silence the fan turned its quarter revolution. I heard the little sigh of it, and then a creak from the pipes, and then, a long way off, a tram.

"Come back tomorrow," she said. "I'll tell you the rest."

There was a thing she did on the stairs, as I left, which I did not see her do on the first afternoon, or on the third. At the turn of the stair there was a framed print on the wall, a large one, a copy of an old photograph of the museum's founding, with ranks of men in black coats standing in front of the building. She stopped in front of it. She did not look at it. She put out her left hand, with the white glove on it, and touched the glass in the lower corner, at the place where there was a child in the front row, a boy of perhaps five, in a sailor suit, who was the only one in the picture not looking at the camera.

She took her hand away. The glove left no mark on the glass. But I saw, because I was a step below her and could see the light across it, a faint round smear, the exact size of a fingertip, that was there already, and had been there for a long time, and had been renewed each day.

Diana had been in the zone five weeks when she first met her. I have set her entry here, as I found it, in the pencil and the italic it was written in, and the testimony after it, which Fritha gave me in her own time over the three afternoons and which I have cut by a third. She asked me to cut it by half. I did not.

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 9 August 2034

**SY/FC/0011\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date:** 9 August 2034, 06:15*

***Location:** the Praga shoreline, vicinity St Michael's Cathedral*

***Conditions:** overcast, low light, the water flat and the colour of pewter*

***Temperature: **19°C*

***Pressure:** 1009 hPa, falling*

***Flood: **rising. Waterline up six centimetres on the last reading.*

*I went to St Michael's this morning on a rumour, and the rumour was true, which I did not expect.*

*The cathedral stands on the Praga side of the water and the water has been in it. There is a tide line on the nave pillars at the height of my hip, a brown band, very level, as though someone had measured it. The roof is open in two places over the north aisle. Pigeons, and under the pigeons a smell of wet plaster and old incense that has not been burned for years and has not left. I came in by the west door, which hangs from one hinge, and I stood still for a full minute to let my eyes find the dark.*

*She is nineteen. She is called Fritha. I was told her name by a Runner at the door who would not come in, and who said only that the girl in the church was not to be hurried.*

*I will set down what I saw. Photographs. Several thousand, I would estimate, though I did not count and I regret that. They are laid in rows on the altar steps and along the lowest courses of the pews, each one flat, each one weighted at the corners with a pebble or a spent cartridge. More hang from lines strung between the pillars, pegged out like washing. They are sorted. I could see that at once. By year, I think, and within the year by something I could not read, a logic that belonged to her. Weddings in one aisle. Holidays in another. School photographs in a long grey rank of identical children, all of them squinting at the same sun.*

*She was sitting in the middle of it on a folding stool, with a photograph in each hand, and she was talking to one of them. Not reciting. Talking. The way you talk to a person who has just come into a room. I heard her say: you've done something with your hair. I stood quite still. She did not know I was there.*

*Beside her, on the floor, a child of perhaps six sat with her knees drawn up and her arms round them. She did not touch a single photograph. She looked at them, and then at the girl, and then at them, and when I came further into the nave she looked up at me and then down again, with no expression I could name, and I understood that I had been assessed and found unremarkable. I have since learned the younger girl **is called Nikita. I have also learned that she does not speak.*

*I introduced myself. Fritha did not look round. She said, "Is it raining? I can hear it." It was not raining. It was the pigeons.*

*I stayed an hour. She let me sit on the step. She showed me nothing and explained nothing and I asked her no direct question, because there are people you spend a week not asking. When I stood to go she said, without turning, "Don't take any."*

*I said I would not.*

*I'm writing this on the shore with my feet in the cold, and I have to put down what I felt, because the work is useless to me if I leave it out. I knew her. Not her face. The stacking. The sorting by year, the pebbles on the corners, the refusal to let a single frame be lost or out of order. I grew up in a house where a man did exactly this with chemical formulae, at night, with a lamp, and I thought it was the most loving thing I had ever seen anyone do. I stood in that church and I thought: she is not hoarding them. She is guarding them. And then, immediately, in the same breath: that is what I do.*

*I did not say it to her. I did not say the other thing either, the thing that came after, that a person can guard something until it is the only thing she is guarding. I wrote it here instead, and I will see whether it is true.*

*\[in pencil, later: Nikita followed me to the door. Stopped at the threshold. Went back to her.\]*

\* \* \*

**FRITHA** · recording, Berlin, November 2035

**SY/FC/0001\_GR** · *Felicyta Cielinski, recorded in Berlin, November 2035*

I'll tell you what I tell everyone who wants to know about the photographs, which is that they weren't the point. Everyone thinks they were the point. A girl in a church with five thousand pictures of dead people, you can write that story in your sleep. I could write it in mine. I did, for about a year.

My father was a photographer. A real one, not a wedding man, though he did weddings to pay for the other thing. He had a studio in Praga above a shop that sold car parts, and I grew up in a smell of fixer and hot dust. My mother worked at the National Museum, in conservation, so I was raised between two people whose whole lives were about keeping things from going. You can see where this is going. I could see it too, later. Not then.

When the dam went, they had maybe forty minutes. I was at a friend's, on the high side. I want to say that, because people always make it sound as if I was there. I wasn't. I got a call, and then I didn't get a call. They went back for the albums. Not for the passports. Not for the money in the biscuit tin. For the albums. Eleven of them, in the bottom of the wardrobe, in the room that was the first to go. I know it was eleven because I've counted the empty places in the photographs of the shelf.

That's a joke. Or it was. Sorry.

What I took out of the water was forty-one prints. They had been in my father's jacket, which I found on a railing six days later, caught by the sleeve. Forty-one, and every one of them wet through, and I dried them on the radiator in the stairwell of a block that had no power, in front of a stove somebody had made from a bucket, and when they came up out of the water, they weren't what they had been. The emulsion had lifted. My mother's face had slid a little to the left. That's the first thing I'd say to you if you wanted one true sentence. Photographs don't keep people. They keep a version of the light that fell on someone, and the light moves, and the paper goes, and what you're left with is a thing you've made into a god because you haven't got anything else to pray to.

I'm sorry. That's not what I thought then. I thought, I'll save them. All of them. Everyone's. Because if my parents' faces could slide, then so could anyone's, and the only thing I could do about it was go round the district and find every picture I could and put it somewhere dry.

So that's the church. People were leaving them behind. In flats, in cars. In the hallway of a school, three hundred identical class photographs in a cardboard box that someone had left on a radiator. I took them. All of them. I'd go out in the boat with a bag and come back with a bag and I'd put them down on the steps and I'd talk to them, because someone had to. I knew it wasn't normal. I knew it the way you know you're drunk. I did it anyway.

Diana came in on a Wednesday. I'd heard her at the door, and I'd decided not to look. She sat on the step for an hour and said nothing, and I remember I was furious, because nobody had ever just sat. They asked what I was doing, or they said they were sorry, or they wanted something. She wanted nothing. Her hands were in her lap. After a while I realised she was looking at the pictures the way I looked at them, which is to say, in order. She'd worked out the order. I watched her work it out. She never said so.

When she got up she said she wouldn't take any, and I almost laughed, because no one had asked her. And then I thought: she's the first person who understood that was the thing I was afraid of.

She came back every second day for three weeks. She brought bread, and later a camera. A proper one, with a lens that she cleaned with her sleeve. I wouldn't let her use it in the church. I said it was a rule. She said all right, and she kept it in its case and put the case under her arm and she never once took it out.

On the twenty-second day she said, "Would you take one of me?"

I said no. Then I said why not. Then I took it.

That's the whole story. I took a picture of a woman on a shoreline with her hair in her eyes, and I looked at her through the glass, and I saw that she was, right then, in the middle of doing something. Not posing. Looking at the water. And I understood that I had been taking pictures of the wrong thing for a year. I'd taken pictures of the things that had finished. I'd never once taken a picture of someone while they were still deciding what to do next.

I gave her the print. It would have been in the boat, or the houseboat. I kept the negative. I'm not going to pretend I didn't.

There's a thing she said that I wrote on the inside of my arm that night, in biro, and I'll give it to you now so you can put it in. She said: a photograph is a promise that you'll remember. It's not the memory. If you want to keep someone, you have to do the remembering yourself, and you have to do it while they are in the room.

I was nineteen. I thought that was the most unfair thing anyone had ever said to me.

I still think so. But I do it now. I look at people while they're in the room.

Nikita stayed with me most of that autumn. She'd sit and watch me take the pictures down off the lines and put them in boxes, one at a time, and she never helped, and she never left. I used to say, you could say something, and she'd look at me. And I'd say, no. You're right. You don't have to.

I gave her one. The only one I never put in a box. It's me at four, on my father's shoulders, in a field, and you can't see his face, because he's holding the camera out in front of us both at arm's length and it's pointing the wrong way, at the sky. A bad photograph. The worst one he ever took. I thought, she should have the one where nothing's wrong with the person but everything's wrong with the picture.

I think she still has it. You'd have to ask Anna.

*(end of recording)*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · Berlin, November 2035

I took the lift back to street level and stood for a while on the pavement outside the museum with the print of myself in my coat pocket. It was the fourth week of November, and the sun was almost set by four. Diana's note against Fritha's transcript was short. *The best of them at being alive. Make sure she knows.* I have not been able to find any way of telling a person that without it sounding like a verdict.

I wrote to Fritha the following week. I told her what Diana had written. She did not answer for a month, and then a parcel came, and in it a single print, the same small square on proper paper, with a typed label on the back: *SY/FC/0001. Twenty-second day.*

It was Diana. Not a face I had seen by one shielded lamp in a farmhouse, but the face of a woman on a shoreline in August with her hair in her eyes, looking at the water, in the middle of deciding. She had kept the negative. Of course she had kept the negative. Underneath the label, in pencil, in the careful hand of someone who has learned to write for the archive, one line.

*Not a promise. It is what she looked like, once, while she was still in the room.*

## Chapter 3: The Zone (Protocol 001)

**ALEX** · 2036

*Binder one. Reproduced as found.*

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · Protocol 001

**SYNPTCL/PROTOCOL/001 · THE ZONE**

**Compiled: D. Antonoff · Status: ACTIVE · Reliability: PROB\_VAL\_0.9**

**1. SCOPE**

This document describes the survivor corridor of Warsaw as it stood on 30 June 2034. It records geography, water levels, designated positions and routes. It does not record opinion. Where a figure is estimated, it is marked E. Where a figure was measured, it is marked M and the instrument is named.

**2. DATUM**

All levels are given against the Warsaw pre-flood datum of 77 m above sea level (M, published survey).

| **Level** | **Condition** | **Date established** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 77 m | Warsaw before the flood | n/a |
| 78 m | Dam failure. Flood begins | 2028, the first flood |
| 80 m | The city divides along the Vistula | 2028, the first flood |
| 84 m | Warsaw falls. The occupation takes hold | 2028 to 2029, the first flood |

The operating level of this document is 84 m. At the time of writing the corridor sits at 84 m, plus or minus 0.4 m depending on rainfall (M, staff gauge at the Żerań dock, read daily at 07:00).

**3. CAUSE OF THE FLOOD**

The flood was not one event. It was a cascade. Five failures upstream, each making the next worse. They are listed in order of effect on Warsaw.

| **No.** | **Location** | **Date** | **Failure** | Effect on the Vistula |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Solina Dam, River San | 11 Oct 2028 | Gates damaged. Uncontrolled release | Surge into the San, then into the Vistula |
| 2 | Czorsztyn Reservoir, River Dunajec | 11 Oct 2028 | Forced release or structural damage | Mountain surge adds volume |
| 3 | Sandomierz, middle Vistula | 13 Oct 2028 | Dike breach, 40 m or more | Additional wave on the crest |
| 4 | Włocławek Dam | 14 Oct 2028 | Gates destroyed. No regulation possible | River runs unregulated toward Warsaw |
| 5 | Narew retention works, near Siedlce | 16 Oct 2028 | Collapse or breach | Water pushed onto the east bank directly |

The water has not receded because the causes have not been corrected. Reservoirs are damaged and cannot absorb rainfall. Włocławek is non-functional. Breached dikes cannot be repaired under occupation. Pumping stations have no power. The occupiers prioritise their own routes and show no interest in civilian drainage (E). It plateaus. It does not pass.

**4. DIVISION OF THE CITY**

The Vistula flows north. The west bank (Old Town, Śródmieście, Wola, Ochota, Mokotów) stands on slightly higher ground. It is drier and occupied. The east bank (Praga, Białołęka, Targówek, Wawer) is lower, flooded in parts, and held by the survivors.

No route leads west. The airport and the occupied west bank prevent it (M, three attempts, all abandoned).

**5. THE CORRIDOR**

The corridor runs north to south: Żerań, the Białołęka and Targówek pockets, Wawer, and the southern end at Wilanów. It is not a territory. It is a chain of dry places connected by rail embankments, overpasses, concrete roofs and shallow margins that can be crossed by boat or on foot if the crossing is known.

Length of corridor, Żerań to Wilanów: approximately 24 km by the usable route (E). Straight-line distance: 19 km (M).

Four classes of ground are used in this document.

| **Class** | **Description** |
| --- | --- |
| A | Deep, persistent flood. Boat only |
| B | Shallow, seasonal flood. Passable with knowledge |
| C | Occasionally waterlogged. Unpredictable |
| D | Dry but cut off. Survivor routes and bases |

**6. DESIGNATED POSITIONS (SURVIVOR)**

Each position is an operating point (OP). The designations below are those in use on the field map.

| **Code** | **Name** | **Ground** | **Function** | Grid Ref | Image Ref |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Z1 | Żerań | D | The hub. Power, radio, water access | 52.2926 N, 20.9964 E | OP-Z1 |
| Z2 | Zoo | D | Community gathering. Large grounds, good cover | 52.2573 N, 21.0255 E | OP-Z2 |
| SZ | Szpital (Brodno) | D | Medical. Defensible. Multiple buildings | 52.2984 N, 21.1071 E | OP-SZ |
| P1 | Praski | D | Market, trade, exchange | 52.2483 N, 21.0310 E | OP-P1 |
| R1 | Rynek (Grochów) | D | Gathering point. Mid-corridor | 52.2553 N, 21.0491 E | OP-R1 |
| G1 | Gocławek | C | Eastern edge. Exposed. Intelligence value high | 52.2357 N, 21.1246 E | OP-G1 |
| A1 | Mała Afryka | D | River access. Supply | 52.2234 N, 21.0660 E | OP-A1 |
| C1 | Czerniaków | D | River access. Escape route south | 52.2221 N, 21.0478 E | OP-C1 |
| SK | Siekierki | B | Southern riverside. Industrial. Exposed | 52.2072 N, 21.0870 E | OP-SK |
| L1 | Las | D | Forest. Concealed. Runner waypoint | 52.1982 N, 21.1145 E | OP-L1 |
| P2 | Przyloty | B | Deep in the margin. Accessibility seasonal | 52.1456 N, 21.1711 E | OP-P2 |
| P3 | Pałac (Wilanów) | D | Southernmost. Grounds large. Escape route link | 52.1653 N, 21.0897 E | OP-P3 |
| F1 | Filtry | D | Water filtration. Critical. Near Russian R0 | 52.2206 N, 21.0080 E | OP-F1 |

Note: the field map carries the code S1 twice (Szpital and Siekierki). This document resolves the collision by using SZ and SK. The field map has not been corrected. It should be.

**7. KONTAKT POINTS**

Twelve forward observation points. Each reports to the nearest OP. When a Kontakt point stops reporting, something has changed.

| **Code** | **Location** | **Watch** | **Grid Ref** | **Image Ref** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| KB | Michałów-Grabina | Northern corridor | 52.3572 N, 21.0335 E | KON-KB |
| KK | Marki | Northeast. Watches RK at Kobyłka | 52.3129 N, 21.1406 E | KON-KK |
| KH | Henryków | Northwest. Near the flood boundary | 52.3354 N, 20.9618 E | KON-KH |
| KZ | Ząbki | East. Watches the Rembertów approach | 52.3265 N, 21.0954 E | KON-KZ |
| KR | Rembertów | East. Deep grey zone | 52.2860 N, 21.2842 E | KON-KR |
| KS | Wesoła | Far east. Exposed | 52.2027 N, 21.3836 E | KON-KS |
| KA | Anin / Wawer | Southeast grey zone | 52.1625 N, 21.3538 E | KON-KA |
| KJ | Józefów | Southern grey zone. Near the escape route | 52.0984 N, 21.2369 E | KON-KJ |
| KW | Michalin | South. Watches R4 at Otwock | 52.0985 N, 21.1639 E | KON-KW |
| KC | Mokotów / Czerniaków | South. Near the flood margin | 52.1919 N, 21.0451 E | KON-KC |
| KF | Central, near F1 | Dangerous. Close to the west bank | 52.2190 N, 20.9893 E | KON-KF |
| KP | Wilanów | Southern. Near P3 | 52.1282 N, 21.1042 E | KON-KP |

**8. ESCAPE ROUTES**

| **Route** | **Direction** | Distance (approx) | **Character** | **Duration** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| PÓŁ (north) | Through the gap between RN and R2, past KB, toward Nieporęt | 25 km | Most direct. Passes close to RN. High risk. The winter route when the water is frozen | One night, 7 to 9 hours on foot. About 4 hours across ice in deep winter |
| WSC | Northeast, wide around RK at Kobyłka, toward Pustelnik | 45 km | Longest. Woodland cover. The safest. Used to move high-value persons | Two nights with a day lying up, 14 to 18 hours moving |
| PÓŁ (east) | East of Wawer, past KJ and KW, south through Józefów | 30 km | Closest to the bases. The everyday route. Most monitored | One long night, 9 to 10 hours, longer when patrols are out |
| ZAC | From P3 south past the palace grounds, through Bielawa, toward Konstancin-Jeziorna, then east | 50 km | Longest. Rural once clear of the city. Used when others are compromised | Two nights with a day lying up, 16 to 20 hours moving |

**9. SEASONAL VARIATION**

What is passable in June is not passable in January. This is the principal planning difficulty of the corridor. Readings below are provisional and will be revised with each season.

| **Condition** | **Summer** | **Winter** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Żerań canal | Open. Lily pads, reed, a beaver | Frozen. Walkable in a hard frost (E) |
| Boat movement | Free except in Class A | Impossible on the canal. Limited elsewhere |
| PÓŁ (north) | High risk | The preferred route over ice |
| ZAC | Affected by southern flood levels | To be recorded |
| Vegetation | Dense. Conceals movement and conceals the Dendron | Dies back. Reveals both |

The engineer at Termika (L. Kowalski) keeps a table of water levels by day. It is the only reliable series in the corridor. He has agreed to let it be copied.

**10. STREETS**

Many streets in the corridor are passable only under names that the municipal authority withdrew before 1944. Those are the names in use among the survivors. They are the names that will be understood. A list of forty-one streets, with both names, is given in Annex A (not reproduced here; see binder two).

**11. LIMITATIONS**

This document records what was measured. It does not record what was seen but not measured. It does not record what was heard.

**12. ITEMS NOT IN SCOPE**

12.1. The sound under the water at Żerań at night.

12.2. The condition of the survivors' faces.

12.3. The colour of the water at 06:00, which is not the colour of the water at 07:00.

12.4. The condition of the Dendron (see Protocol 003).

12.5. Item 14 (reserved).

**13. NOTES TO FILE**

13.1. The Żerań canal has a beaver. It has been observed at the same stretch on eleven consecutive mornings. It is not a hazard.

13.2. Dragonflies are present in the reeds at the dock. Four species have been counted: brown hawker (Aeshna grandis), four-spotted chaser (Libellula quadrimaculata), ruddy darter (Sympetrum sanguineum) and emperor (Anax imperator). The counting was not required by this document.

13.3. The compiler's father taught the compiler to count them.

**14.** RESERVED.

*End of Protocol 001.*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · 2036

I have changed nothing in it. People who have seen it in draft ask me why there is so much detail about the water, and so little about the people, and I tell them that is the wrong way round. Every person in this book is somewhere in those tables. Goya is in the column headed *Watch.* The Runners are in the line about the winter route. Fritha's church is in Class D, under the name of a place that was a cathedral. You only have to know where to look.

I did not understand the last three lines, the ones about the beaver and the dragonflies and the father, until I had read the twelfth Protocol. Diana wrote them, I think, on the evening she finished the document, and I think she thought no one would read as far as the notes.

She was right. For most of a year I did not.

## Chapter 4: The Engineer

**ALEX** · Gdańsk

At four o'clock on a Friday, Lucasz Kowalski stopped.

I was standing next to him on the crest of a new embankment on the Martwa Wisła, outside Gdańsk, with a hard hat that did not fit and a clipboard I had been given so that I would look as if I belonged. A hundred and forty people were working below us on the sluice. Cranes. A pile driver somewhere out of sight, setting a slow iron beat. He had been talking to me about tolerances, and the difference between the load a gate is rated for and the load a gate will meet, and he did not finish the sentence he was in. He looked at his watch. He took off his hard hat and held it against his chest, and without raising his voice he said, "That's the day."

The pile driver stopped. The cranes came down. Within five minutes the site was an arrangement of machines left where they stood, and a hundred and forty people were walking up the embankment past us in the light with their bags, and several of them nodded to him, and he nodded back, as if he were the head of a school.

"I put it in the contract," he said. "Not as a courtesy. As a load calculation. A crew that doesn't stop is a crew that hasn't been rated."

I asked how long it had taken him to learn to do that.

He thought about it with the seriousness of a man who is not accustomed to being asked a question about himself. "I started in the second winter," he said. "I'm still learning. If you come back in the spring you'll find me here at half past five, and I'll tell you it's a special case."

I came back. He was there at half past five.

What he gave me over four days, in a rented office with a space heater and a drawing board that had been used as a table, I have set against the entry Diana made the first time she watched him work, in the second month of her time in the zone. She noted nothing of what she thought of him. She noted instead, to the hour, what he did. I believe that was the kindest thing available to her.

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 26 July 2034

**SY/LK/0008\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date:** 26 July 2034, 20:30*

***Location:** OP-Termika, the Docks*

***Conditions: **clear, hot, still, haze over the canal*

***Temperature:** 24°C*

***Pressure: **1016 hPa, steady*

***Flood: **stable. Staff gauge 84.1 m. Water low and clear.*

*I spent the day at the Docks and I will set down the hours, because the hours here **are the finding.*

*05:10. He was already on the gauge. A man of about thirty-six, narrow, in a canvas jacket gone white at the seams, kneeling on the lowest step with a clipboard on his thigh. He wrote the reading, stood, looked at the sky, and wrote something else. He did not greet me. He said, "You're the journalist. The gate sticks on the ninth rung, don't use it."*

*06:30. Control room. A concrete cube with a steel door and the look of something that was meant to be a pumping station and has been forced into other work. Valves, handwheels, a hand-drawn schematic taped to the wall, and beneath it a second schematic, newer, with the first one's mistakes corrected in red. A boy of about eleven, working on a quadcopter on the floor. Lucasz did not look at him. He said, without turning, "Check the third motor." The boy checked the third motor. It was the fault. Neither of them said anything. I have seldom seen so much affection conducted entirely in the imperative.*

*09:00 to 14:00. He walked the Docks. I walked behind. He examined, by my count, forty-three objects: a sluice leaf, the seal on a culvert, a drain, a retaining wall, a cable run, a boat hull, an iron ladder. At each he laid the flat of his hand on it for about two seconds before he spoke. Then he spoke the figure. He did not tell me what the figure meant. I understood within an hour that the figures were not the point. The hand was the point.*

*14:30. Water. He gave me a tin cup and a piece of bread and stood while I ate. I asked him what the project had been. He said, "The canal." I said which canal. He said, "The one that was built to stop this," and went back out.*

*17:00. I found out the rest from a woman at the Docks who asked me not to name her. I record it as hearsay, unconfirmed (PROB\_VAL\_0.6). Before the war he was one of the engineers of the Vistula Canal project: a relief channel, intended to take the peak off a flood at Warsaw and lead it harmlessly round the city. The gates were his. The logic that opened and closed them was his. When the dam went, the occupiers took the control room, and ran the logic in reverse. The channel did not take water away from the city. It brought it in. He knows this. He has known it since the first week.*

*20:00. The light goes gold at the end of the canal and the chimney stack takes it first. At this hour, south **across the water, a violin.
