It comes from the Zoo, perhaps two kilometres, and it carries because the air is still. A single line. I do not know the piece. I saw him stop. Not slowly. He stopped as a machine stops when the power is cut: mid-gesture, with a coil of cable in his hands. He stood for the whole of it. When it ended he looked at the cable and began, at once, to coil it, faster than before.*

*I have not seen him sit down. I have not seen him eat sitting. I asked him, at the end of the day, when he had last slept for more than four hours, and he said, "Calculate it yourself," and I did, and I will not write the figure.*

*I know this. I know it from the inside, in my body, and I don't want to say how. A man who cannot stop is not working. He is holding something at arm's length so that it will not touch him. My father did it with formulae. I do it with a notebook. He does it with a river.*

*I asked the boy to tell me if Lucasz falls asleep anywhere. He said: "He doesn't. He just switches off. Like a lamp. Then he switches on."*

*\[in pencil, margin: the violin. who? ask.\]*

\* \* \*

**LUCASZ** · recording, Gdańsk, December 2035

**SY/LK/0001\_GR** · *Lucasz Kowalski, recorded in Gdańsk, December 2035*

I'll tell it in order, because that's the only way I know how to tell anything.

Before the war I was a civil engineer. Hydraulic works. I was good, which is not a boast, it is a classification. I was on the Vistula Canal, the relief scheme, from the first survey to the last gate. We were going to take two hundred cubic metres a second off a flood crest and put it round the east of the city, in a channel, under control. It was a beautiful piece of work. I say that as a man who has since had to look at it for a long time.

Its logic was mine. I wrote the gate sequence. The sequence was clean: open this, then this, then close this when the downstream gauge reaches that. I was proud of it the way you are proud of a joke that works. When the dam went at Włocławek and the water came down, I wasn't in the control room. I was in a cellar on Targowa with a torch, trying to find my mother's cat. And the people who took the control room, the occupiers, they weren't stupid. They looked at the logic and they saw that if you swap two lines it does the opposite of what it was built to do.

It doesn't take the water away from the city. It brings it in.

That's the whole story. People want it to be bigger. It's a sequence of gates, and one of them is wrong because it was right.

She asked me about it on the day she came to the Docks. I told her she didn't want to know. She said she would only write it down if I wanted it written, and I looked at her, and I thought, all right, you, and I told her. I think she was the second person I had said it to.

You want to know why I worked the way I did. Eighteen hours. Twenty, some days. I'll give you the engineering answer and then I'll give you the other one.

The engineering answer is that the corridor needed a man who knew what every gate did, and there was one of him. If I wasn't at the Docks, the level wasn't read. If the level wasn't read, nobody knew when to move the boats. It was true, and it was enough, and I used it for ten months.

The other answer is that if I stopped, I could hear it. The water. Not at the gate. In me. I don't mean as a figure of speech. If I put my hands down I would hear the sequence. Open this, then this. Close this when the gauge reaches that. It plays, you understand. It plays like a tune you can't get out of your head. The only thing that I had found that drowned it was another job.

Diana never lectured me. I want to say that. Several people had a go. A man from the Brigade explained that I would be of more use if I rested, which is the kind of thing that makes you work for another day to prove him wrong. Diana only counted. She'd come to the Docks at five and she'd write down what I had done, and at the end of the day she'd read it back, not as a reproach. As a record. "Twenty-two hours since you last sat down," she'd say with a pale grin. It was the first time I had been shown myself.

Then there's Tanja.

I'm going to be careful here, because she might read this, and because I owe her more than I know how to say. We had met that spring. She played at the Zoo most evenings for the ones who would come. I would hear it from the Docks and I would stop. I couldn't help that. I'd hear the first note, and for the whole of the piece the sequence would go quiet. All of it. For the length of a piece, I was a man standing on a dock.

I didn't tell anyone. It was the one thing I kept.

Artur told her. You know, the boy. He had worked it out in about a week, with the instinct of a person who spends his life looking at what makes a machine stall, and one evening he went to the Zoo and said, I think the engineer has a fault, and the only thing that fixes it is you.

I'm told she laughed. I wasn't there.

What happened next was a conspiracy. I know now that Goya was in it, and Artur, and Diana, who didn't take part and didn't stop it. They waited until I was in the control room on a Thursday in late October. Artur got the key from my belt. I still don't know how. They locked me in. From the outside. Goya stood at the door with his arms folded and when I banged on it he said, in the voice he uses for children and drones, "Sit down, Lucasz." And the door didn't open for seven hours.

I should say that you can't be locked in a control room for seven hours without something happening to the way you listen. At first I hammered. Then I worked on the lock. Then I sat down on the floor, because I was tired, and because there was nothing else, and I put my head on my knees.

And Tanja started to play. On the step outside, with her back to the door. I could hear the whole of it. It was an hour, perhaps more. And the sequence didn't play. For an hour, there was only the violin, and the concrete, and the pipe above me ticking as it cooled.

I'm not going to tell you I wept. I'll tell you I slept, which is rather worse. I woke on the floor at nine at night with a coat over me that I didn't recognise, and the door was open, and Tanja was sitting on the step with the violin across her knees, looking at the water.

She said, "That's three days' worth."

I said three days' worth of what. She said, "Whatever it is you were going to do instead."

I should have been angry. I wasn't. I've thought about what I was. I believe I was a structure that had been rated for a certain load and had been carrying a larger one for so long that I had forgotten what it was to be unloaded. You can't be unloaded all at once. It takes a very particular sort of care. She has it.

We're not married. I say that so you don't write it. We're something that I don't have a word for and she doesn't wish to have one. But on Fridays at four o'clock, I take off my hat.

*(end of recording)*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · Gdańsk, December 2035

I asked him, at the end of the fourth day, what he had felt when Diana read back the hours.

He took a long time to answer. He had put his hand flat on the drawing board, as he had at forty-three objects in a day, and he was not looking at me.

"Counted," he said. "Not judged. I could stand counted."

Her note against his transcript was the shortest she wrote. *Lucasz. Rated for more than he knew.* Under it, in the same pencil, smaller, and so faint that I did not see it until I held the page to a window: *Who was it on the violin?*

I did not tell him that. I had not yet been to see Tanja. She had a condition of her own, and I will come to it.

## Interlude II: Sasha Lebedev, Observation Post R0

*This is a reconstruction, and I want to be plain about what kind. Sasha Lebedev left no account of himself. He left these: eleven rota sheets, photocopied, in a clear plastic sleeve; the post log, in a green hardback notebook with a red spine; forty-four text messages from his mother, recovered from a handset that had been left in a drawer; the wrapper of a chocolate bar; and a receipt. They give times, numbers and a handwriting. They do not give the man, and I never met him. For some of what follows I also have the voices of men who knew him, or who did the same work, spoken to me across a table after the war. I do not take such men at their word, and I say so where I do not. So I have written one day of his life in his voice, in the present tense, because a list of figures is not a person and he was not a list. Whenever I step out of his voice and into my own, it is in italics, in brackets, and I name the source: log, rota, message, receipt, wrapper, interview, or mine, for anything I have supplied myself. Where the documents are silent I have kept silent too. Where they are not, I have copied.*

The room is four metres by three. Cinder block, painted to shoulder height in a green that the paint company calls Sage Whisper and the army calls Interior B, and above that in white. There is a window with a steel shutter, raised twenty centimetres, so that I have a strip of the city of the width of my forearm. There is a metal desk. On the desk are the following: one radio, set R0, with a handset on a curly cord; one laptop, grey, with a scratch on the lid in the shape of a seven; one green notebook; one pencil, HB, sharpened to a length of five centimetres; one electric kettle, 1.7 litres, white, with a limescale line at the one-litre mark; one cup, enamel, chipped at the rim at the position of four o'clock; one jar of tea, loose, black; one wrapped bar of chocolate, 90 grams, milk, with a picture of a cow.

I wake at 05:10. I have been at Observation Post R0 for ninety-four days.

*(Log. Every page of it begins at 05:10. Ninety-four days is my count, from the first page to the last.)*

I make the tea first. I put in the kettle one litre of water from the blue jerrycan, the one with the white cap. I switch it on. While it heats I put on my boots, the left and then the right, the laces tied with a double knot and the ends tucked in. The kettle takes four minutes and ten seconds. I have timed it on my watch, which is steel, with a green face, on a canvas strap that I have replaced once. I do not look at the window.

At 05:14 the kettle clicks. I pour. I leave the leaves in the cup. I do not drink yet.

*(Mine. The kettle, the jerrycan and the double knot are in no document. I have given him the objects of a morning so that he can have one.)*

At 05:20 I take the pencil and I open the notebook to the page with the date. I write the date. I write the temperature, which I read from the thermometer clipped outside the shutter, a plain red-spirit thermometer in a plastic case: 9 degrees. I write the wind, which is nothing. I write the water level, which I read against the post painted on the wall of the building opposite, a pole with white bands every ten centimetres, and which this morning stands at the fourth band, as it did yesterday, and as it did the day before that. I write: *Unchanged.*

*(Log. The date, the temperature, the wind, the level of the water, and the word Unchanged, which stands at the foot of almost every page.)*

At 05:30 I switch on the laptop. It takes a minute and eleven seconds to boot. There are four windows. I open them in order.

The first window is the grid. It is a map of the district in white lines on grey, and over the map there are green squares, one for each block, and the squares are numbered. At the top there is a bar that reads *COHORT STATUS* and a figure, which is 71.2 per cent. The figure is in a font I recognise, because I have looked at it for ninety-four days, a thin clean font with round letters. I know that the percentage is the proportion of the population of the district who have been assessed at Stage 3 or above. I know that Stage 3 is the point at which a person will give up a name when asked. I know that Stage 4 is the point at which they will give up one without being asked. I have been told that Stage 5 is not something that I need to concern myself with.

*(Mine, from the headings of the rota and one line in the log. I do not know what the screen looked like. The figure of 71.2 per cent is in the log.)*

The second window is the rota.

I will describe the rota, because it is what I am for.

It is a table. It is a single sheet of paper in the printed form and a single screen in the other. It has seven columns and, on an ordinary day, thirty-one rows. The columns are: *Block. Time. Crew. Task. Duration. Authorised by. Status.* Each row is a movement of some kind. A crew of four with a flatbed goes into Block 17 at 07:30 and removes seventeen persons who have been assessed at Stage 4 and are non-ambulant. A truck with a tank goes to the standpipe at Block 22 and applies a treatment. A man with a clipboard goes into a courtyard to count. The rota is issued every sixth day, at 06:00, by Major Budanov's office, and I read it aloud on R0 to the six field commanders, one by one, in the order in which they are listed, and each of them says *Received* and a number, and I write the number in the notebook beside their name.

That is the whole of my job. I read. I am the voice on the radio. In the post log of my predecessor, who left at the end of May, there is an entry in his handwriting, a small upright hand, in blue ink, that says: *Do not add anything. Do not leave anything out. Do not pause.* It is underlined twice.

*(Log. The first page, in the hand of the man who left in May. The line is underlined twice.)*

At 05:55 I drink the tea. It is at the temperature that I prefer, which is 62 degrees, because I do not let it cool, I drink it with the leaves still moving. It is a small pleasure and I am aware that it is a small pleasure. I am twenty-three. I enlisted in the autumn of the first year of the war, in a town on a river, and I was posted to signals because I had a clear voice and no accent that anyone could place. My mother says that I have the voice of a news presenter. This is what she says in her messages. There are forty-four of them, and I have counted them, and the last one was received on the fourth of October at 19:12.

I do not read them any more. I know where the sentences are.

*(Messages. His age, his enlistment and the news presenter's voice are his mother's, from the forty-four. I have kept her words and left him the silence.)*

At 05:58 I put on the headset. I sit up. I place my left hand flat on the desk, with the fingers spread, as I was taught on the course, and I lay the rota sheet under my right hand with the edge square to the edge of the desk. I take three breaths. In for four, out for six. I check the clock on the laptop against my watch. They agree.

At 06:00 I key the handset and I say: *R0 to all stations. Rota for the six days commencing today. Stand by.*

I read.

*Block 3. Oh-seven-hundred. Crew Alpha. Task, door-to-door count and tagging. Duration, three hours. Authorised, B.* I do not say *Major Budanov*. I say *B*. *Block 9. Oh-seven-thirty. Crew Bravo. Task, collection, non-ambulant, Stage 4. Estimated eleven. Duration, two hours. Authorised, B.* I say it in the same voice, at the same speed, at one syllable per two-fifths of a second. I know the speed because I have recorded myself, and played the recording back, and measured it with the cursor on the waveform.

*Block 14. Oh-nine-hundred. Crew Echo. Task, application, standpipe. Duration, forty minutes. Authorised, B.*

*Block 17. Thirteen-hundred. Crew Bravo. Task, collection. Estimated six. Duration, ninety minutes. Authorised, B.*

*Block 22. Fourteen-thirty. Crew Delta. Task, interview, Stage 3 and above. Duration, open. Authorised, B.*

*(Rota. These are lines from the sheet for the fourth of October. I have not invented a row. The speed of his voice is mine; the log says only that he read clean.)*

The word *collection* means what it means. The word *application* means a thing that is sprayed from a tank on a trolley: a solution of a compound in water, 4 per cent, colourless, with a smell that I have been told is like boiled cabbage and which I have never smelt. It is applied to the standpipe and to the grating below the standpipe, and the purpose of it is to encourage the growth, or to feed it, I have not been told which, and it is not my place to ask.

The word *interview* is a word that I have, once, been told the meaning of. It was explained to me by the man who trained me, a captain, in a room with a flip chart, over four minutes. He said that it was a conversation. He said that the subject was seated, and offered tea, and that the interviewer had a list of questions, and that the questions were few and quite courteous. He said that at Stage 3 the subject would answer. He said that the list was of names.

*(Interview. The captain who trained him, now in custody, gave me this explanation in his own words. He still calls it a conversation. I have kept it short, as he did.)*

I read the rota. The commanders say *Received*. Alpha, six, four. Bravo, six, five. Echo, one, nine. Delta, one, one. I write each number in pencil in the margin, in a column, in figures that do not vary in height. My hand is quite steady. It has been quite steady for ninety-four days.

At 06:09 I say: *End of rota. R0 out.* I put down the handset. I take off the headset and I fold it, once, and I place it on the left of the laptop, with the foam pads upwards.

I do not think about what I have said. I want that to be plain. I do not think about it, because it is not mine to think about. I have said a list of times and numbers, as a clock says them. A man who reads a timetable at a railway station is not responsible for the trains.

At 06:30 I eat. A tin of stewed beef, 338 grams, with a key on the lid. A sleeve of crackers, seven in the sleeve. I count them. I eat five and I keep two for the evening. I wash the spoon in a plastic bowl with a quarter of a litre of water and a drop of detergent and I dry it on the towel, which is green, and thin, and has the stamp of the quartermaster's store in one corner.

At 07:00 the first report comes in on the second window. It is a feed from the crews, a line of text for each event, and the lines arrive in the order in which they happen. *07:04 BLK 3 ENTERED. 07:11 BLK 3 DOOR 1 OPEN, 2 PERS. 07:12 TAG 1 STAGE 3. 07:12 TAG 2 STAGE 2.* I do not read them. I see them, in the way that a man sees a fence from a train.

I will tell you what I do see, because I have the leisure to see it, and the strip of window is wide enough.

The building opposite is a post-war block of eleven storeys, concrete panels, painted at some point in the seventies in a cream, with a balcony for each flat. On the sixth floor there is a balcony with a rotary washing line. There is a shirt on it. It is a man's shirt, blue, with a white collar, and it has been there for the whole of the ninety-four days, and it has not moved in any wind, because it has been stiffened by the green. It is an extraordinary thing to see a shirt that does not move. On the balcony of the fifth floor, beneath it, there is a bicycle, a woman's, with a basket. There is a flowerpot with something brown in it.

The green is on the lower four floors. It is the pale colour of the underside of a leaf. It is growing on the drainpipes and on the balcony rails and in the gaps between the panels, in vines, in a texture that, close up, I am told is finely ribbed. It has been growing since the second flood. It is advancing at between four and nine centimetres per day. This is in the notebook. I have a column for it.

At 09:40 on the sixth day a figure appears in the window.

I note this because it is irregular. It is a woman. She is on the street, at the base of the building opposite, on the near kerb, in a jacket the colour of a traffic cone, and she is carrying a satchel on a strap. She is looking up. She is looking at the window, at the strip, at me. I know that she cannot see me. The shutter is down to a level at which the room is a darkness. But she is looking directly at the strip, and she holds the look for nine seconds.

I write: *09:40. One civilian, female, orange jacket. Non-compliant posture. Observed nine seconds. Departed east.* I do not report it. It is not in the instructions that I should.

*(Log. That entry is his, word for word. Everything around it is mine.)*

I should say that I thought about it afterwards. I should say that it was the first time in the ninety-four days that I thought about a person on the street as a person, in the way you might think about a particular bird.

At 12:00 I eat the chocolate. I have decided this, in advance, in the way that I decide everything. I take the bar out of the drawer and I look at it. It is a 90 gram bar of milk chocolate, in a purple wrapper with a gold border, and a picture of a cow, a brown and white one, standing in a meadow, in front of a mountain that is perhaps the Alps, perhaps not. I break off one square, and then a second. I place them on the tongue and do not chew. I time it. It takes one minute and fifty seconds for them to dissolve entirely. The taste is of milk and sugar and a faint flavour that I would call vanilla, though my mother says it is not vanilla, it is a chemical that is like vanilla. I eat two squares. I wrap the rest and put it back in the drawer.

My mother sends the chocolate. She sends it in the parcel, every month, with the socks and the soap. There is a card in each. The card says: *Sashenka, are you eating? Are you warm?* I have kept all the cards. They are in the drawer under the chocolate, in a rubber band.

*(Wrapper and cards. The cow is on the wrapper and the words are on the cards. How he eats the chocolate is mine.)*

At 15:00 I read the log of the day. It is not an instruction. It is a habit. I sit with the notebook open, and I read what I have written, from the first line, and I see that it is a plain thing. A page of figures. A temperature. A water level. A column of numbers received. I look at the handwriting. It is not mine. It has been mine for ninety-four days, but it is the hand of the man who trained me, and of the man who trained him, a small upright hand, with the sevens crossed.

At 19:12 the handset on the desk lights with a message. I do not open it. I know where the sentences are. I put the notebook in the drawer. I put the pencil on the notebook. I fill the kettle with one litre of water from the blue jerrycan and switch it on, and while it heats I take off the left boot, and then the right.

*(Message. Received on the fourth of October at 19:12. I give its words below. Whether he opened it, I do not know.)*

I drink the tea at 62 degrees, with the leaves still moving. I eat the two crackers. I do not eat the rest of the chocolate. I look at the strip of the window until it goes dark, and the shirt on the sixth floor of the building opposite goes with it, and I am aware, for the length of a breath, of the whole district in the dark, forty thousand lights that are not lit, and the sound that comes up from the grating at the foot of the block, slow, and patient, and in no hurry.

I do not mind it. It is quite all right.

§&#32;

The day above is the fourth of October. What follows is the second, which the documents describe less well, and then what I have supposed. I am speaking now, and not him.

I haven't got the log for the second of October. I have the rota sheet and I have the log of the third, and there is a gap. The gap is in the radio record, which is kept on a card in the laptop, and which I found, in the end, on a card in the plastic sleeve with the sheets: there is a transmission at 06:00 on the second, which begins, *R0 to all stations. Rota for the six days commencing today. Stand by.* There is a silence. The silence is of four minutes and four seconds. Then the transmission resumes. *Block 3. Oh-seven-hundred.*

I do not know what he did in those four minutes. I know what the log of the third says. It says: *Equipment fault. Radio. Cleared 06:04.* In the small upright hand, but not, I think, quite upright. The hand slopes a little to the right, in a way that it has not before.

I know what was in the rota for the second. It is in the sleeve. It was a long one, forty-four rows. The first of them was a collection, Block 9, Crew Bravo, 07:30, estimated nine. The ninth was an application. The twentieth was a collection from a place that is not described by a block number but by the name of a school.

I know what is on the receipt. It is from a petrol station on the road to Radzymin, dated the second of October at 16:41. It is for one litre of milk, one packet of sugar and one lighter. It is in the sleeve with the card and the wrapper of the chocolate bar.

I have made some assumptions. I have assumed that he did not leave the post. I have assumed, from the receipt, that on the afternoon of the second he left it, for forty minutes, and came back. I do not know why he bought sugar. I do not know why he bought a lighter. He did not smoke. There was no stove that needed lighting. The kettle was electric.

I have assumed that he read the rota in the end. The log says that the commanders said *Received*. Alpha, six, four. Bravo, six, five.

I have assumed that on the fourth of October, at 19:12, he received the last of his mother's messages, and that it said what the others said. I have the message. It says: *Sashenka, I hope you are eating. I put in the good chocolate. Do you remember the blue cow? I think that this is the same.*

I do not know what a blue cow is. It is not in the other messages. I have looked for it.

I have set down the fourth because it is the day on which the documents agree with one another most closely, and the second because it is the day on which they do not. The man between the two days is mine. I would rather say so than let him pass for a witness.

*Sasha Lebedev was relieved at R0 on the eleventh of October. The post log passes, in the same notebook, to a hand that I do not know. It is blocky, and it presses hard. On the first page of that hand, under the date, is the old line, copied out entire: Do not add anything. Do not leave anything out. Do not pause. Beneath it, in pencil, in a style that is not that of either hand, there are two words: He paused.*

## Chapter 5: The Pilgrim

**ALEX** · Przemyśl, January 2036

Janek made tea.

That was the first thing and for the first twenty minutes it was the only thing. A refugee centre outside Przemyśl, a converted barracks with a laundry smell and a noticeboard in four languages, and at the end of a long corridor a small kitchen where a man of fifty-seven in a worn tweed coat was waiting for water to boil. He had the glasses ready, the Russian kind, in their metal holders, and he set one in front of me on the oilcloth and did not ask a single question until I had drunk half of it.

"Goya sent you," he said.

I said that Goya had not exactly sent me. That he had told me to come.

"Then he sent you." He smiled. It changed his whole face, which was long and creased and not, until then, a face made for it. "Is he alright?"

I thought of the veterans' facility, and the swearing that had stopped in the middle of a word. I said that I thought he would be. I said that he had asked after him in a voice I had not heard from him before.

Janek nodded, and looked at his tea, and said nothing for such a long time that I began to wonder whether I had said something wrong. Then he said, "Good," very quietly. And poured me another.

We talked for three hours. I have tried since to say what he did, because it is difficult to explain, but I have settled on this. He listened to my questions the way you listen to rain on a roof. As a thing that is going on above you and does not require you to leave.

Diana had met him in the first summer, on a dock. I have put her entry before the testimony because I think she would have wanted it that way. She was always careful to let a person be seen by someone else before they were heard.

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 19 August 2034

**SY/JD/0013\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date:** 19 August 2034, 21:00*

***Location: **the dock, OP-Żerań*

***Conditions:** dusk, warm, a little wind from the south, smell of cut reed*

***Temperature: **22°C*

***Pressure:** 1011 hPa, falling slowly*

***Flood:** rising. Waterline up nine centimetres on the last reading.*

*They were sitting at the far end of the dock when I came down from the control room, and I will set down exactly how, because I believe it is a finding.*

*Two men. The larger was Goya, on the edge, his boots a hand's breadth from the water, a fishing line in his lap that he was not watching. The smaller was the man everyone calls The Pilgrim**, in a long trench **coat, with a small book on his knee. They were about a metre apart. They had not been speaking. It was not that sort of silence. It was the other sort, the sort that two people build together and take turns keeping up.*

*I have now heard Goya for two months and I have not once heard him go a full minute without a profanity. He has sworn at the weather, the Russians, the flood, the engineers, the dead and the living, his boots, and, in one memorable instance, a heron. He did not swear at Janek. He did not swear at the line. He sat with his shoulders down in a way I have not seen in him, and I understood that for the length of that silence he was off duty from himself.*

*I stood there longer than was polite.*

*Janek looked round. "Come and sit," he said. "It's the best place on the water to watch nothing happen."*

*I sat. It was low, and the planks were warm. The canal had that quality it takes on before dark, when the surface gives up all pretence of depth and becomes a sheet of something you could put your hand through. He had a flask. He poured a cup of tea and gave it to me without asking and I held it. The tea was too sweet. I did not say so.*

*We talked, or rather he did, in short sentences with long gaps. I will record what I can, though I do not think the words are the content.*

*He is fifty-five. He studied for the priesthood and left it to marry. His wife died of cancer five years before the invasion. He has owned, since then, a coat, a pair of boots, and the book on his knee. It is a prayer book. The spine is taped. The tape has been replaced several times, and I could see the older layers, yellow under the new. He let me hold it. I cannot** say what I felt. I held it for perhaps a minute and gave it back.*

*He asked me nothing about myself. I noticed that. He did not ask who I had lost or why I had come. After half an hour I heard myself say, to the water, "I haven't prayed in two years." I hadn't** meant to say it. It came out in the voice I use for something I have kept in a pocket so long that I've stopped feeling its weight.*

*Goya didn't** look round. Janek didn't** either. He simply **said, after a moment: "No. I didn't think you had."*

*That was all. He did not ask why. He did not tell me it was all right. He left the sentence on the planks between us and let it lie there like a coin someone has dropped, and after a while I realised that I was not afraid of it.*

*I asked him whether he believed God was in the city. I phrased it as a question of observation, because I did not know how else to ask. He said, "Which city?" I said this one. He said: "I don't think God is in the city. I think the city is in God. It's a smaller thing than you imagine, and it makes no difference to where you stand."*

*I didn't know what that meant**. I'm writing it down because it troubled me, and my** work is to be troubled in order.*

*At 22:15 Goya got up, said something obscene about the line, and walked up the dock. I watched Janek watch him go. He lifted two fingers in a kind of benediction that didn't** claim to be one. Goya didn't turn. But his shoulders, in the dusk, went up and down once, as if someone had told a joke.*

*I am not a believer. I want to record that. I think I **was one, once, and I would like to be exact about the tense.*

*\[in pencil, margin: he did not ask. why did he not ask?\]*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · Przemyśl, January 2036

He would not be recorded yet. I had put the machine on the oilcloth between us, and he looked at it with the mild courtesy of a man being shown a baby, and said that it was a quarter to four and that at four he did the round, and that if I wanted the rest I had better come.

So I went with him.

The round began in the kitchen with the flask. It was a steel one, dented on the shoulder, and he filled it from the urn, not the kettle, because the urn was hotter and held its heat to midnight. Then he took four paper cups from a sleeve and put them in the pocket of the tweed coat, where they were crushed flat, and he opened them again one at a time as we went, with his thumb, like a man opening envelopes. He did not tell me where we were going. He walked at the pace of someone who has walked a long way and is in no danger of arriving.

The centre had been a barracks and still behaved like one. A corridor sixty metres long, painted to shoulder height in a green that had gone grey, and above it a cream that had gone yellow. Doors on both sides, numbered in black stencil, each with a card in a plastic sleeve and, below the card, a second sleeve for the names. The floor was grey linoleum, worn to the weave in a path down the middle, and along that path there were, as we walked, a perambulator with a bucket in it, a pair of child's wellingtons set neatly side by side, and a bicycle with no front wheel. The smell was what I have said, laundry, but there was something under it that I had not expected, which was onions. Somewhere a pan was being used at four in the afternoon for something that would be eaten at six.

At the first noticeboard he stopped. It was a cork board two metres wide, and it was in four languages, in four columns, each headed by a small flag drawn in felt tip by a child. Polish, Ukrainian, English, Arabic. The notices were the same in each. *Showers: Tuesday, Thursday, Saturday. Doctor: Wednesday, 10:00, ground floor. Please do not keep food in rooms. Lost: one grey glove, left hand.* He read the whole board, top to bottom, left to right, as I have seen a priest read a missal he knows by heart, and when he reached the end he took a drawing pin from the cork, pushed it back in a quarter of an inch to the left, and went on.

The first door was a woman of about seventy who did not open it. He knocked twice, then once, and said through the wood, "Pani Wanda, it's only me," and a chain was drawn back and the door opened eleven centimetres. A hand came out. It was an old hand, spotted and very clean, with the nails cut square. He put a cup in it and filled the cup, and it went back in, and the door closed, and he waited, and after a short time the chain went on again. That was all. He said to me in the corridor, in a normal voice, "She counts the spoons. There are four in the room. She counts them in the morning and at night, and if I say it's all right she doesn't believe me, so I don't say it. I bring her tea at four. It's a spoon she doesn't have to count."

The second door was a boy of eleven with a laminated map. It was a map of the whole of Europe, the kind that is sold to schools, and he had it taped to the wall and was doing something to it with a ruler and a green pen. Janek did not offer him tea. He sat down on the end of the bed, which I noticed he did not do in any other room, and looked at the map, and said, "Where today?" The boy said, "Lisbon." Janek said, "That's a long way." The boy said, "Four thousand and ten kilometres by road." He drew a line. It went from Przemyśl to Lisbon through towns I could not read, and it was straight, and perfectly neat, and I counted nine other green lines on the same map, all from the same point, all ending at the sea. "Tomorrow," the boy said, "Faro." Janek said that Faro sounded warm. And that was the entire conversation, and when we left the boy had not looked up from the ruler once.

The third door was a man who slept under the bed. I do not mean that he was found there. He had made it his place. There was a mattress laid on the linoleum beneath the iron frame, with a blanket and a folded coat for a pillow, and he lay on his side with his knees drawn up, awake, with his eyes open, in a space that was perhaps sixty centimetres high. Janek got down on his own knees on the floor, which took him some time, and held out a cup, and the man took it from under the frame and drank without sitting up. They said nothing. Janek stayed on the floor for the time it took the cup to be finished. When he got up he put a hand on the iron rail, the way a man steadies himself on the side of a boat, and I saw his lips move, but whether in prayer or in the counting of a pulse I could not tell.

At the fourth door he did not knock. He took a key from his own pocket.

"This is mine," he said. "It's the one thing I've kept." And he nodded at the room, which was a single bed, made, with the grey blanket squared at the corners, a chair, a table the size of a tea tray, and a window. The room was clean in the way of a room lived in by a person with very little. "It's two doors from the kitchen. Sometimes there's a person who can't sleep with anyone else breathing, and they come at two in the morning and knock, and I give them the bed and sit up in the kitchen. In the morning I strip it."

I asked how often.

"Last night," he said. "And Tuesday. And the Sunday before."

I drank the tea. It was too sweet. I did not say so.

We went down a staircase and out through a back door into a yard where a line of washing had been hung between two posts and a woman was taking it in, sheet by sheet, folding each against her chest. There were thirty-one pegs on the line. I counted them because I count things when I have been in a room with a great deal of other people's trouble. The sheets were the grey of the blankets, with the same red stripe. She nodded to Janek, and he to her, and she did not say a word, and I understood that in this building the exchange of a nod was a fully articulated conversation that took place twice a day.

It was starting to get dark. He stopped at the corner of the yard, where there was a bench against the wall and a drain, and from the drain, in the cold, a thin white breath of steam rose and spread. He looked at it for a time. I thought that he was resting. Then he said, quite quietly, "Do you hear it?"

I did not. I said so.

"Good," he said. "I thought you might. People who have been in the city sometimes do." He picked a leaf off the bench and turned it over, and put it down. "There's nothing in the pipe. It's only the boiler. But half of the people here can't walk past that drain after dark. Pani Wanda goes the long way round, past the bins. I've thought about asking the caretaker to move it. And then I thought, no. If I move the drain they will think the thing they were afraid of has gone, and it won't have. It'll only have changed its address."

He screwed the cap on the flask. The round was over. It had taken fifty minutes, and he had said perhaps four hundred words in them, and I had the feeling that I had been given a lesson in something, and had not yet learned what.

Back in the kitchen he washed the four cups in the sink and set them upside down on the rack to dry, and then he sat down opposite me, and folded his hands on the oilcloth, and nodded at the recorder.

"Now," he said. "I can talk. I was only waiting until the people had been seen to."

\* \* \*

**JANEK** · recording, Przemyśl, January 2036

**SY/JD/0001\_GR** · *Janek Dąbrowski, recorded in Przemyśl, January 2036*

You'll want me to say something wise. People do. They look at the coat and the book, and they think, here is a man who's worked something out. I've worked out how to boil water on one ring. That's the extent of it.

I'll tell you the order.

I was in the seminary at twenty. I believed. I want to say that, because people expect a man who leaves to have stopped believing, and that's not what happened. I believed so much that I couldn't stand the distance between what I believed and what I did with my afternoons. There was a woman who came to the choir. Hanna. She sang alto, badly, with enormous conviction, and one evening she said to me, in the vestry, "You look at the floor as if it owed you money." And I laughed, which I hadn't done in the building for a year, and I knew.

I left. They were kind about it. That was the worst. I would have preferred a scene.

We were married twenty-three years. She had a small flat in Praga with a kitchen the size of a confessional, and I used to say that I'd exchanged one for the other and got the better bargain. We had no children. We had a table. It was green, painted by her father, and it had a leg that was shorter than the others, so that every meal for twenty-three years had a folded beer mat under one corner. I don't know where that table is. I think the water has it.

She died in the spring, five years before the war. It took eleven months. I'm not going to tell you about it. I'll say that during those months I discovered that I'd never in my life wanted anything for myself, and that this wasn't holiness, as I had thought, but a kind of cowardice. I wanted her to live. I wanted it so badly that it frightened me, and I prayed, and I understood in the middle of the praying that I wasn't asking God for anything. I was bargaining. I was a man at a market.

After she died I gave the flat away. I gave away the furniture. I kept the coat she had bought me and the book, and I took to the road. A man who owns nothing can't be taken from. I said that to myself. I thought it was freedom.

I walked for eleven years. I won't list the places. I was fed in kitchens. I slept in barns. A sort of professional guest. I did odd jobs, I sat with the dying, I buried one or two. And the war came, and I kept walking, and when I came to Warsaw it was under water, and I thought: here's a place that has nothing left to take.

It was a good place for me. That's a terrible thing to say. I was useful. Goya, for instance. You've heard about Goya. He sat down next to me on a dock one evening, a man in such a state of fury that it came off him like heat, and he cursed at God for ten minutes. I let him. When he'd finished I said it was the most honest prayer I'd heard in a year. I meant it. And he looked at me as if I had hit him.

We sat there. That was the whole friendship. We've been sitting ever since.

Now. What you want to know is about the room.

There were two old people in the zone, Rosa and Marek. They had come through the last war as children and the one before it as parents and this one as old people, and they had a flat on the third floor of a block in Białołęka that had stayed above the water. It had a back room. A window. A bed with a real mattress, and a wool blanket, and a stove, and one evening in September Rosa said, "Janek. We have a room. It's been empty since the winter. Do you want it?"

I said, no. Of course I said no. I said I was only passing through.

She said, "You've been passing through for eleven years."

I've thought since about what I felt. It wasn't gratitude. I want to be accurate. It was a want. It rose in me so fast and so complete that I had to put my hand on the wall. I wanted the bed. I wanted the window. I wanted the kitchen smell of old people's soup and the sound of a radio through a wall. I wanted, God forgive me, the folded beer mat. I wanted a place with a leg that was shorter than the others.

And I had spent eleven years teaching myself that this was exactly what I must not have.

It was Diana who saw it. She had come with Marek to carry a table. She stood in the doorway of that room and she looked at me, and she said, in a very ordinary voice, "Janek. Is it that you can't have it? Or that you don't think you're allowed?"

Nobody had asked me that. Not in eleven years. I looked at the floor. As if it owed me money.

I took the room. I'd like you to put that in. I took it in October, and I lived in it through the second winter, and I was warm for the first time since Hanna. I used to lie there at night and think: you've been proud of owning nothing. That's a kind of owning. You owned your poverty. You hugged it. And what Hanna wanted, if you want my view, what she would have wanted from the green table and the shorter leg and twenty-three years, was that you would let somebody keep you.

It isn't a sin to want to be kept. It's the least sinful thing I know. I think it might be the whole of it. I think the rule, if there is one, isn't that you mustn't want. It's that you mustn't want what is somebody else's and call it yours. And Rosa gave me the room. Do you see? It wasn't hers to keep. It was hers to give.

I'm still here, you know. At the centre. People think I'm doing something noble. I'm doing the washing up. I'm the one who goes round at night with a flask. And I have a room. Two doors from the kitchen. It's nothing. It has a window. I keep a folded beer mat in the drawer, though I haven't got a table, and I won't tell you why.

You've been very patient. You haven't asked me whether I still believe.

I do. But it's a smaller thing than you might imagine.

*(end of recording)*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · Przemyśl, January 2036

I drank the last of the tea. It was cold. Janek had said nothing for a minute. Then he reached into the pocket of the tweed coat and put the prayer book on the oilcloth between us, spine up, the tape yellow under the new.

"She sat where you are," he said. "On the dock. Diana. She held it for about a minute. I've thought since that it was the first time anyone had held it who didn't want it to be anything."

I did not ask him what he meant. I asked whether he would let me photograph it. He thought, and said no, and I understood why, and I was glad he had said so, having met Fritha.

Diana's note against his transcript is the only one in the whole archive that is not in pencil. It is in ink. It is two words, and she has gone over them until the paper is nearly through.

*I asked.*

She had written *He did not* in front of it, and crossed that out.

## Interlude: The Second Flood

*Reproduced as found. Notebook two.*

**DIANA** · diary, 28 August 2034

**SY/DA/0014\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date:** 28 August 2034, 23:40*

***Location:** the houseboat, OP-Żerań*

***Conditions:** heavy cloud, gusting, the reeds flat then upright then flat*

***Temperature: **16°C*

***Pressure: **998 hPa, falling fast*

***Flood:** rising. Waterline up fourteen centimetres on the last reading.*

*Something is coming.*

*I have written that three times in the last hour on three different pages and crossed it out each time as unscientific. I have now written it in ink, in the proper place, under the readings, because the readings are what they are and the sentence is also true.*

*Pressure at 21:00: 1004. At 22:00: 1001. At 23:00: 999. At 23:40: 998. The fall is steady and it is accelerating. I checked the barometer against the second instrument, the one in the cabin, and they agree to within a hectopascal. The wind has backed to the south and has been in the south for nine hours. The gauge at the dock was read by Lucasz at 18:00, and the figure was relayed to me by Artur, who ran. It was up fourteen centimetres on the morning. I do not have the earlier readings in front of me. I have written to ask.*

*Observations. The birds went quiet at about 19:30. The heron that has stood on the pontoon every evening for three weeks has not come. The water is higher against the hull than I have seen it, and it has the look that water takes on before it moves: the surface is heavy, as if it were thicker than it should be. A smell of iron. And the sound.*

*I will set it down as exactly as I can. At night, from the far side of the canal, a low sound that I have recorded on eleven previous evenings. A sustained note. Not machinery. Too broad, too uneven. It has the quality, I wrote on the first night, of a great many people groaning together a long way off. Tonight it is not lower. It is nearer, and it is not groaning. It is one note, held. I would say a warning that has been muffled before it reached me. I would say a siren with its mouth stopped.*

*I do not believe it is the wind in the structures. I'm no longer saying that.*

*I'm afraid. I have not written that in this notebook since the day I came. I'd like to say it is the fear of someone who has been in a flood before and knows what it does. It is not. It is the fear of someone who has been here for twelve weeks and has begun to love the place.*

*I have packed the notebooks, the cassettes and the memory cards in the oilcloth, in the order I would want to lose them in. I have put the oilcloth on the high shelf in the cabin. If the water comes in it will be a long time before it reaches the shelf.*

*I do not intend to sleep.*

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 2 September 2034

**SY/DA/0015\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date:** 2 September 2034, 04:00*

***Location:** OP-Termika, the Docks*

***Conditions: **driving rain, south wind, visibility under fifty metres*

***Temperature:** 13°C*

***Pressure: **986 hPa, falling fast*

***Flood: **SECOND FLOOD. Waterline up fifty-eight centimetres in the last twenty-four hours. The Docks overtopped at 02:50.*

*I am writing this in a wet notebook on the roof of the control room with my back against a ventilation stack. I will keep it short because I am cold, and because there are two things I must put down while they are exact.*

*The first is the water.*

*At 01:30 the canal was at the third step of the dock. At 02:10 it was at the fifth. At 02:50 it went over the top, not in a wave, which is what I had expected, and what the first flood is said to have been, but as a slow and total rising, like something being poured into a bath. By 03:15 the lower quay was gone. By 03:30 the Docks were an island. The rain was so heavy that the surface of the water seemed to boil. Lucasz was at the gate from 01:00. He did not leave it. I saw him from the roof, in a yellow coat, one hand flat on the main sluice leaf, and I understood that he was reading it through his palm, as he reads everything. At 03:40 he opened the second gate. I do not know what the sequence was. I know that when he did it his lips moved.*

*Goya had the Runners out by 01:45. Twenty-three children, in four boats, the youngest nine. I counted them out and I counted them in. I counted twenty-three. I will repeat that line, because I wrote it and then read it as though it were someone else's: twenty-three out, twenty-three in. He stood at the bottom of the ladder and handed each one up like a parcel and for once he did not swear. His mouth was moving. I could not hear what he was saying. It may have been the count.*

*The second is the three.*

*I have been asked to keep a record and I will do it correctly. Three people were lost at the Docks between 02:50 and 03:30.*

*Wacław Pietrzak, 71, who kept the ferry and would not leave the ferry. He was seen on the pontoon at 02:55. The pontoon is no longer there.*

*Iga, 23, a Runner, who went back for a boat. Her surname is not known to me. I did not ask. I should have asked in June.*

*A man whose name I did not get. He was brought to the quay by two others from the lower street at about 03:10 and he did not speak. He was breathing. At 03:20 he was not. I have his boots. I mean that they were handed to me because I had a notebook and someone thought the notebook was where such things went.*

*I'm writing their names in ink. I wish it did not seem important to me that the ink should be good.*

*It is 04:25. The rain has not eased. The level is still rising but more slowly, and Lucasz has come up on the roof and sat down beside me, which he does not do, and said nothing, and I have not asked him to go. The note from across the water is much louder than it was. It has not stopped. It is as though the flood has taken the cloth from its mouth.*

*I know now what I am here to do. I knew it in June. I have not said it. A flood that nobody records is a weather event. A flood that is recorded, in order, with the names of the people it took, is a charge. I have a recorder. I have forty cassettes. I have a community that has told me nothing about itself except under duress, and tonight I saw twenty-three children come in out of the dark one at a time, and a man read a gate with his hand, and I thought: no one will believe that they were this careful.*

*I will begin tomorrow. Not with the dead. With the living, while they are in the room. I'll ask each one the same first question. What do you carry? I do not know yet what the answers will be, and I have found that this is the only way to begin.*

*I'm shaking. It is the cold.*

*\[in pencil, below: Tape D-001. 3 Sept. Goya, if he will.\]*

## Chapter 6: The Occupation (Protocol 002)

**ALEX** · 2036

*Binder one. Reproduced as found.*

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · Protocol 002

**SYNPTCL/PROTOCOL/002 · THE OCCUPATION**

**Compiled: D. Antonoff · Status: ACTIVE · Reliability: PROB\_VAL\_0.7**

**1. SCOPE**

This document describes the occupying force as observed from the east bank between 3 September and 20 October 2034. It records positions, routines, named individuals and methods. It does not record intent. Where a statement rests on one source it is marked U (unconfirmed). Where it rests on two or more it is marked C (confirmed).

It is a companion to Protocol 001. Read that first. The occupier is not a separate subject from the water. Each is the other's instrument.

**2. THE MACRO PICTURE**

Warsaw is a dot. The force that surrounds it holds Belarus, the Baltic states, most of Ukraine and a great swathe of Poland running east from the Vistula. On the map that circulates in the zone, which is a hand copy of a hand copy, the occupied territory is coloured pink. Warsaw is a white speck in it, smaller than a thumbnail.

There are other specks. Gdańsk, on the coast, holds because the sea is open to it. Lviv holds in the mountains. Kyiv holds. Between those places and this one there is no consistent land route. No relief is coming overland. The western powers watch, drop what they can by air, and issue statements, which reach us by radio and are read aloud at the Zoo in the evenings, to general indifference.

I put this first because everything else in this document is detail, and the detail is easier to bear if the shape is understood. We are not simply under pressure. We are enclosed. The difference governs what it is reasonable to hope for.

**3. THE OUTER RING**

The posts are designated R. Codes are those on the field map. Two codes appear twice (RK, RL). This document resolves the collisions by using RKS (south of Wilanów) and RA (the airport). The field map has not been corrected. It should be.

| **Code** | **Location** | **Purpose** | **Location** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| RN | Michałów-Reginów (north) | Northern approach control | 52.421, 20.972 |
| R2 | Chotomów / Legionowo (northwest) | Vistula north, supply route | 52.417, 20.883 (Chotomów); 52.402, 20.927 (Legionowo) |
| RL | Łomianki (west) | Western Vistula bank | 52.335, 20.889 |
| RB | Bemowo (west) | Western perimeter | 52.240, 20.910 |
| R3 | Izabelin (west) | Forest western block | 52.297, 20.811 |
| RK | Kobyłka (northeast) | S8 highway control | 52.343, 21.190 |
| RH | Halinów (east) | A2 motorway control | 52.233, 21.350 |
| R4 | Otwock (southeast) | Southern river approach | 52.105, 21.261 |
| R1 | Konstancin-Jeziorna (south) | Southern Vistula control | 52.085, 21.115 |
| RKS | South of Wilanów | Southern exit block | 52.140, 21.090 (approx.) |
| RA | Chopin Airport | Supply hub, air control | 52.166, 20.967 |

No post is held in strength greater than a company (E). The ring is not a wall. It is a series of eyes with gaps between them, and the gaps are the escape routes (Protocol 001, section 8). The occupier knows where the gaps are. I believe it has left them open because it prefers to watch what passes through.

**4. THE INTERIOR**

Three positions stand inside the perimeter. They are the most dangerous facts in this document.

| **Code** | **Location** | **Purpose** | **Location** | **Coordinates** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| RM | Tarchomin | North of Żerań. Direct pressure on Z1 |  | 52.318, 20.980 (approx.) |
| RW | Wola | West bank. Inner city control |  | 52.236, 20.965 |
| R0 | Ochota | West bank, adjacent to the Filtry water station (F1) |  | 52.213, 20.985 |

RM is two kilometres from the Żerań dock. It can be seen from the roof of the control room on a clear morning. It has not fired. It has not needed to. R0 is the post that matters to the water: F1 stands within sight of it, and every litre that reaches the corridor passes under its eye (C).

The grey zone between the survivor line and the ring is mostly water. It is crossed by boat or by knowing where the water is shallow. The occupier patrols the main flooded thoroughfares in gunboats, at irregular hours, without lights (C, three sources).

**5. THE WEST BANK**

The west bank is drier. It is the occupier's ground and it is held with ease. Five places are of consequence.

**5.1. The Citadel.** The nineteenth-century fortress on the Vistula in the north of the city. Command centre. It controls river access from the north.

**5.2. The Palace of Culture and Science.** A gift from Moscow in 1955, designed to be seen from every part of the city. It is seen from every part of the city. It is now a logistics and supply hub, and it carries a mast from which broadcasts are made. The broadcasts are in Russian and in Polish. They are discussed in section 8.

**5.3. The Old Town and the Royal Castle.** Rebuilt after 1944 from photographs and paintings. Occupied for its symbolism. Loudspeakers on the ramparts.

**5.4. The Saxon Garden and Łazienki.** Open ground turned to air defence. Missile batteries have been seen between the trees (U, one source).

**5.5. Szucha and Pawiak.** The German security police took the building at Aleja Szucha 25 in 1939 and used its cellars for interrogation. The Russian force has reoccupied it. A tunnel is reported to run from the cellars to the old prison at Pawiak (U, one source), which has been reactivated. Persons are taken in. Some are returned. A source who walked past Pawiak on 14 September counted seven lit windows.

I record these together because they have a feature in common, and it is the only feature in this document I am permitted to call a finding. In each case the occupier has built nothing. It has taken what was already there. The Palace was built to be looked at. Szucha was built to be afraid of. Pawiak was built to hold. It has not needed a new instrument for any of them.

**6. THE SCREENING CAMPS**

There are at least four, all west of the Vistula. Persons who cross the river, or who accept the offer (section 9.3), are taken to them. They are screened. I use the word as it is used on the leaflets. What the screening consists of is not known to me, and I have no source who has been through one and returned willing to say (U).

The occupier numbers what it holds. The points are marked SP on the leaflets and on the buses, in black stencil. I record the three I can place.

**6.1. Marymont (SP-1).** The sports halls and halls of residence of the physical education academy on Marymoncka, on the left bank opposite Białołęka. It receives persons who cross in the north (E). A Runner who works the northern reach counts two buses a day on the Marymoncka road, one at about 07:00 and one at about 16:00. They arrive with the blinds down and they leave with the blinds up (E, one source). I do not know what is done in the halls. I know that the lights are on.

**6.2. Torwar (SP-2).** The ice arena and sports hall on Łazienkowska, near the river, in the centre. It receives persons who cross at the central bridges (E). The lights are on all night. A source who worked the south shore before the second flood says that the ice is still kept frozen, and that the power for it was the first thing restored in that district (U, one source). I note that this is a large use of power for a place that offers warm beds.

**6.3. Służewiec (SP-3).** The racecourse in Mokotów: stables, a grandstand and a very large open ground. It receives persons who cross from Czerniaków and Wilanów, in the south (E). It is the only point I know of that has a fence of its own. The fence is new, two and a half metres high, in pale galvanised wire that shows against the grass in photographs taken from the flood margin (C, two photographs). Persons are queued on the open ground. I am told that the queue is orderly (U).

**6.4. SP-4.** A fourth is known to me by its number only, which I have from the destination board of a bus seen on the embankment road. I cannot place it (U). It is the reason I wrote at least four.

**6.5. Okęcie.** The airport is not a screening point. It is the supply hub, and it is where the aircraft come (C). I record it here because of what is believed. Among survivors it is believed, and I set this down as a belief and not as a finding, that certain persons are not sent on to the shelters at all but are flown out from Okęcie, and that they are the persons the occupier most wishes to keep. The word I have heard used is rendered. I have heard it in three places that do not speak to one another. I have no source who has seen it (U).

**6.6. The shelters.** Persons who pass through a point are sent on to shelters, which are numbered also. I have heard the numbers one to six spoken, by two persons who were not together (U). The numbers rise (E).

| **Code** | **Location** | **Receives from** | **Coordinates** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| SP-1 | Marymont, Marymoncka | The northern crossings | 52.272, 20.962 (approx.) |
| SP-2 | Torwar, Łazienkowska | The central bridges | 52.216, 21.040 (approx.) |
| SP-3 | Służewiec racecourse | Czerniaków and Wilanów | 52.185, 20.970 (approx.) |
| SP-4 | Not placed | Not known | Not known |

**7. NAMED INDIVIDUALS**

Three names are used here. They are given in full, with patronymic, because that is how they appear on the one set of orders recovered. In the zone they have other names, which I give in the notes.

**7.1. Colonel Viktor Nikolaevich Sorokin.** Commanding officer. Age approximately 55 (E). Based at the Citadel. His record, so far as it is known, is that of a professional: no excesses are attributed to him, and none to his staff in his name. He does not appear to hate the city. He appears to have orders, and the competence to carry them out. It is reported that he reads. A source who delivered food to his office in July says the desk was clear except for a single paperback and a glass of water. The title was not seen.

**7.2. Major Alexei Dmitrievich Budanov.** Head of screening and intelligence. Age approximately 44 (E). Operates from Szucha. He is the active officer. It is his name that appears on the orders. He is thorough. He is courteous to those who are brought in and is said to speak good Polish, with an accent that reminds the listener of Kraków (C, two returned persons). I am aware of him before I am aware of the colonel. I believe he is looking for someone. I do not know whom.

**7.3. Sasha Igorevich Lebedev.** Contracted operator, observation net. Age approximately 24 (E). Not a regular officer. Underpaid, by every sign. He is attached to R0 and he reads the post's rota on the radio net every sixth day at 06:00. The voice is young. Two intercepts have the speaker reading the new rota twice, slowly, as a person would who was not sure of it.

On 2 October the rota was announced with a four-minute gap in the sequence of posts. The gap was not repeated. It was not noted by anyone else on the net (U, one source, my own intercept).

I do not know what a four-minute gap is. I have written it down because that is what I do.

**8. ROUTINES AND BROADCASTS**

| **Event** | **Time** | **Source quality** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Helicopter flight, west to east | 23:00 to 03:00, irregular | C |
| Gunboat patrol, main thoroughfares | Irregular, unlit | C |
| Radio check, all posts | Every four hours, on the hour | C |
| Rota change, R0 | Every sixth day, 06:00 | C |
| Searchlight, Citadel to the river | Sunset | C |
| Loudspeaker, Palace and Old Town | 08:00 and 20:00 | C |

The searchlight at sunset is the most reliable event in the corridor. It sweeps across the east bank once, slowly, for forty seconds, and goes dark. It does not appear to be looking for anything. It is, I believe, a statement.

The broadcasts are propaganda of an elementary kind. They speak of order, restored services, warm beds. They are made by a woman with a very pleasant voice. They do not mention the water.

**9. METHODS**

**9.1. Starvation by indifference.** The water cut the supply routes. The occupier does not restore them. It does not need to refuse. It only needs not to help (C).

**9.2. Selective pressure.** Individuals who cross the river are taken. Individuals who do not are left alone. The line is the river (C).

**9.3. The offer.** Posts at the edge of the corridor broadcast, by loudspeaker and by leaflet, an offer of shelter, food and warm beds in the western camps. It is made without force. It is made every day (C). By my count it has been accepted by approximately 340 persons since June (E).

**9.4. The silence.** After the second flood no force was sent in. No aid was offered. The occupier watched the water come in and did not move (C, four sources). I have no instrument for recording this.

**10. LIMITATIONS**

This document does not record what the occupier believes. I have a name for the man who reads, and a name for the man who looks for someone, and I do not know whether either name describes anything.

**11. NOTES TO FILE**

11.1. Survivors in the zone have their own names for the three men. The colonel is the Iron One. The major is the Spaniel, not for loyalty but for the way he hunts, nose down, patient. The boy on the radio is the Watcher. These names are not used in this document and I have recorded them here because a community that gives names to its enemy is still alive.

11.2. Reading the orders in the original Russian, I noticed that Budanov signs the Latin letter D with a small flourish, as a man does who was once taught calligraphy.

11.3. The loudspeaker at the northern post has a recorded female voice, which says the same sentence in Polish every four minutes. It is not a hostile sentence. It is the one about the beds.

11.4. On 4 October a child at the Zoo asked me whether the Russians were bad. I said it was not my job to say. She said, "What's your job, then?" I said, to write down what they do. She thought about it and said, "That sounds like the worst job."

*End of Protocol 002.*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · 2036

I have put this document in the book where it falls, between the Interlude and the Musician, because that is where Diana put it, and because I have found in interviews that people do not remember the occupation as an event. They remember it as a light. A beam across the water at dusk, forty seconds long.

I asked Goya what the occupation had felt like from the Docks. He said, "Like being looked at by someone who isn't angry." He said it was worse than being shot at, and then he said that he had been shot at, and was in a position to compare.

I have not found the paperback. I have written to three people who might know. Diana's notes leave a line under section 7.1 where a title would go, and the line is blank.

## Chapter 7: The Musician

**ALEX** · Wrocław, January 2036

Tanja had one condition, and she put it in writing.

*No questions about the soldier.*

I read it standing in a corridor in Wrocław, in a former school that had been given over to the city's reconstruction fund and now smelled of paint, and I thought, I do not know of a soldier. That was, I think, the point. She wanted me to arrive knowing there was a door and not know what was behind it.

She was thirty-six. She ran a room on the second floor with a piano, a carpet, and a row of small plastic chairs, and on the Saturday I came there were eight people in it, aged between nine and seventy, and all of them were holding instruments. A boy of eleven had a violin that was too big for him. A woman in a headscarf held a hand drum in her lap like something she had been asked to mind. Tanja was moving among them, small and square-shouldered, in a man's cardigan, correcting a grip here, lowering a shoulder there, and she did not look up when I came in.

I sat at the back. For an hour I watched a woman who had been, by every account I had been given, one of the best violinists in Warsaw, teach a roomful of damaged people to make a very bad noise together. She was extraordinarily patient. She said, "Again." She said, "Listen to her, not to yourself." And once, when the boy with the large violin dropped his bow and it clattered on the boards, she did something I noticed and did not understand. She went still. Not startled. Still, the way a person is when they have heard their name in another room. She looked at the bow where it lay. It was a perfectly ordinary carbon fibre bow, black, with a white hair. She looked at it for about three seconds. Then she bent and picked it up and put it back in the boy's hand, and said, "Again."

I have since been told that her students notice it. They don't know what they are noticing. They tell each other that Tanja doesn't like bows.

Diana first heard her from across the water. I have put the entry here because it answers a question that I found written in pencil in the margin of another transcript, and I think it is fair to the reader that it should be answered.

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 17 November 2034

**SY/TD/0033\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date:** 17 November 2034, 18:40*

***Location:** OP-Zoo, the old lion house*

***Conditions: **clear, still, the first hard frost, breath visible*

***Temperature:** 3°C*

***Pressure: **1022 hPa, rising*

***Flood: **stable. Skin of ice on the shallows.*

*I have found out who plays the violin.*

*I had been told it was a woman at the Zoo. That told me nothing, because the Zoo is large and has, at the last count, two hundred and ten people living in it, in enclosures which have been made into houses by the unembarrassed ingenuity of people who need a roof. The lion house is the biggest and the best. It has a barred front, which makes a good wall for a stove, and a long dim hall where the cages used to be, and at about six every evening the people come in out of the cold and sit on the floor.*

*She plays at the end, on an upturned feed bin, with her back to the old viewing window. No stage. No announcement. She tunes. She waits until the hall has found its quiet, which takes a minute or two. Then she plays.*

*I came because of Catalina. I will set that down first. Catalina has been keeping a list of her patients at stage 2, the ones whose affect has gone flat but who are not yet obedient, and she told me, in the dry way she tells me everything, that on Tuesday three of them had been in the lion house when the violin played, and that all three had cried. She said it as one would report a fever. "I don't know what it means," she said. "Come and watch."*

*So I watched. There were about sixty. I counted the children, because I count the children: nineteen. And I counted the three. They were sitting apart from one another, one by the stove and two near the door, and I would not have known them from the others except that I knew their faces. A man of about fifty, a retired tram driver, who in two weeks has spoken a total of eleven words to anyone. A girl of seventeen. A woman I could not place.*

*It was the tram driver first. Perhaps four minutes into the piece. His face did not change. His hands were open on his knees. And water began to run down his cheeks, quite steadily, as if from a tap that someone else had turned on, and he did not wipe it, and he did not seem to know. It went on for the rest of the piece. When she stopped he sat with it on his face and looked at the floor. Then he said something to the man beside him, and the man beside him laughed, which I took to be the first laugh I have heard in that hall.*

*I will write down what I think and I will mark it as what I think. I think the compound does not remove feeling. I think it removes the route to it. And I think that music is a route that does not go through the place the compound has closed.*

*It is a hypothesis. It is based on three people**. I have asked Catalina to count again on Friday.*

*I noticed two other things.*

*She does not play to the room. She plays to one person. I do not mean that she looks at them. Her eyes are shut. I mean that there is a place at the back, left of the door, where a person is sitting, and the sound goes there, like a ball thrown to a particular pair of hands. Tonight it was the girl of seventeen.*

*And the bow. I could not see it at first, because she held it low. When she lifted it into the light of the stove I saw that it is not a bow. It is a stick of green willow, stripped, about the length of a bow, with horsehair strung along it and tied off with wire. There is a rasp in the upstroke because the hair is not evenly set. It should sound wrong. It does not. It sounds like someone breathing.*

*At the very end I looked round. There was a man standing at the back, against the wall, in a canvas jacket gone white at the seams. I do not know how long he had been there. He had a coil of cable over his shoulder. It was Lucasz. He had come two kilometres in the frost, in the dark, with the Docks unattended. He did not move. He did not look at me. When she lowered the willow stick he turned and went out, and a minute later I heard the cable hit the ground outside, as if he had put it down in order to be able to stand.*

*I did not follow. I have asked a boy to tell me if he comes again.*

*I am a little afraid of her. I do not know why. I will find the reason and write it down.*

*\[in pencil, margin: not a bow. ask her what happened to the bow. no. do not ask her what happened to the bow.\]*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · Wrocław, January 2036

When the class was over she did not sit down. She collected the instruments one by one, from the hands that had held them, and said each person's name as she did it, and each person said hers back. The boy with the large violin was last. She looked at the scroll of it, where a small crack ran in the varnish, and said, "This one needs a visit," and took it under her arm, and beckoned me with her chin.

The store was at the end of the corridor, behind a door with a hand-lettered card that said **INSTRUMENTS. KNOCK.** Inside there was a long room that had been the school's chemistry laboratory, and the black benches were still there, with the gas taps still standing in rows like the necks of birds. Along the north wall, where the windows were, she had put up shelves, and on the shelves, laid on their sides on folded towels, were perhaps ninety instruments. Violins. A cello with a tide mark across the belly. Three accordions. A trumpet that had been run over. A harp with eleven strings. A plastic recorder in a plastic bag. Each had a luggage label tied to it with string, and on each label, in her small square hand, a name, a date and a few words.

I read some. *Oskar, 9. Brought in a pram. Wants to play it standing.* *Pani Irena. Her husband's. Does not want it repaired. Wants it to stay as it is.* *Left on the steps. No note. Cleaned.* *Found in the water, Orunia. Dried over six weeks. Do not play.* I asked about the last. She said that she had not played it and did not intend to, and that it was kept in the room for the reason that a person who sees a thing thrown away can also see it taken in.

She put the boy's violin on the bench under a lamp and pulled a stool up to it and sat. This was the first time I had watched her hands. They were not what I had expected. I had thought they would be long, and fine. They were square, with broad flat fingertips and nails cut straight across, and a callus on the left index finger so old that it had gone the colour of horn. She worked a thin blade into the crack and did not speak. She breathed through her nose. The lamp was a clip-on, with a daylight bulb, and it made the varnish glow like honey in a jar, and there was a smell of hide glue and spirit and of something sharp and clean that I later learned was the oil she used on the fingerboards.

I looked at the shelves, because I could not look at her while she worked. And I noticed something. I do not think it is the thing she would have wanted me to notice, but it is what I saw. There was a rack at the far end, a wooden one, like a rack for billiard cues, and in it were the bows. Thirty or so. There was a bow of cane, and a bow of bamboo, and a bow that was plainly a length of curtain rail with a row of horsehair, and a bow made out of a ski pole, and bows of ash, and of hazel, and of green willow, peeled and pale, with the hair tied off in wire. They had all been made in that room, I think, by the people who used them, with her guidance, and none of them was straight. There was not a single bow in the rack that had been bought in a shop. There was not a single bow of carbon fibre. Not one.

I said nothing. I had a letter in my bag that said I should not ask.

She finished with the crack. She took a small brush and a pot of something amber and painted a seam of it into the fault, very slowly, the way one paints the edge of an eye, and set a clamp, and wiped her hands on her cardigan. Then she looked up at me for the first time since the class had begun, and said, with no inflection at all, "You noticed the rack."

I said that I had.

"Everyone does," she said. "Not everyone says."

I said that I had been asked not to.

"You were. That's why I like you." She took the lamp off the bench and turned it to the wall. "The boy will want it on Tuesday. It'll take three days to set. I'll tell him it's nothing. I always say it's nothing. And then I stay up, the night before, in case."

I asked in case of what. It was permitted. It was not about the soldier.

"In case he drops it," she said. "In case it breaks. In case I'm wrong about the glue." She laughed. It was a short, dry noise, with no pleasure in it and no unkindness either. "People believe that a teacher is someone who knows how things will go. I'm a person who sits up at night to be ready for how they might."

There was a kettle on the end of the bench, an electric one, with a frayed flex, and she filled it from a plastic bottle and switched it on. We waited. The room was cold. The radiators were of the old kind, ribbed, painted silver, and they ticked as they warmed, and over the ticking there was another sound, which I had been hearing for some minutes without placing it. Somewhere in the building a child was playing the recorder, one note, over and over. A B-flat. It was very slightly flat. Each time it was a little less so.

"That's Oskar," she said. "He's nine. He's been doing it since eleven this morning. I haven't told him to stop." She poured. She gave me the cup with the handle and took the one without. "I'll tell you a thing now, and it's the last thing I'll say before you press the button. I teach because it's the only act I know that's entirely in the future. You can't teach a person anything they've already done. You can only be somewhere in front of where they are going and hold something out."

I said that it sounded like a description of a vocation.
