"No," she said. "It's a description of a hiding place." And she picked up her cup, and looked at the clamp on the violin, and the B-flat from the other room went on and on, coming closer and closer to true, and when it arrived, at about twenty to two, she shut her eyes for exactly as long as the note lasted. Then she nodded at the recorder in my bag.

"All right," she said. "Now."

\* \* \*

**TANJA** · recording, Wrocław, January 2036

**SY/TD/0001\_GR** · *Tanja Dopolewka, recorded in Wrocław, January 2036*

I'll tell you what I can. You've seen the condition. I'm going to be honest about what it covers. It covers one night. It does not cover the rest of me, and I'd like it if you would let me tell you the rest first.

I grew up in a flat on Mokotowska, in a building with a piano in every third window. My grandparents raised me, mostly. They had been children in the ghetto, and then in a cellar in Praga, and then, for the rest of their lives, people who said very little at table. It was my grandmother who taught me. Not the violin, she couldn't, but the other thing. She said it to me when I was six and I was crying because a boy had hit me. She said: "Whatever you do to him, it will still be done." I didn't understand her. I understood it later. It's the only sentence of hers I carry. She never said it again.

I was a pacifist. I'm going to use the past tense and I'd like you to leave it there. I didn't choose it. I was raised in it, the way you are raised in a language, and then I chose it, in my twenties, which is a different thing. I went to the protests. In the year before the invasion of Poland I stood in Plac Zamkowy with sixty other people and a violin. We didn't shout. We stood. I played a single line from a Bach partita, again and again, for four hours. A man spat at me. A girl held my coat. I thought, this is what I am for.

I taught at the Academy. Principal lecturer. I was twenty-eight when Warsaw fell. We kept going. That's the thing nobody will believe. For years after the occupation began, a handful of us kept the Academy open, in the basement, for the students who had nowhere else to go. We had eleven students. We had three pianos. We had a stove that burned the legs of chairs. It was the happiest I've been, and it lasted four years.

I'm going to stop here. I'm going to take a breath, and then I'm going to tell you about what came after, and not the night. I came to the east bank because there was nothing left on the west. I carried my violin. I didn't carry the bow. I came across the water in a rowing boat with six other people and a dog, and the dog was the only one who wasn't afraid, and I thought, I will play for him. I played for the dog.

In the zone they asked me what I could do. I said, play. They gave me the lion house here in the zoo. For the first year I played in the lion house I didn't look at the faces. I looked at the back wall. I played to a particular patch of plaster. Then Diana came, in the autumn, and she stood at the side, and she didn't write. I saw her not write. She had a notebook in her hand, and she kept it closed. Nobody had ever done that. I'd had a hundred journalists in my life. She closed the book.

I'll say one more thing, about the music, because you'll have been waiting for it. Someone told me that I was healing the people in the hall. I said, I'm not healing anyone. I'm playing a violin. If it does a thing, it does it without my permission. That's the only way it can work. If I was trying to, they would hear it.

That's all. That's what I'm willing to put on the tape.

*(end of recording)*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · Wrocław, January 2036

When she had said it I switched off the recorder, because she asked me to. She had stood up, and gone to the window, and I thought that was the end of it. The room was empty. Outside, in the school yard, someone was dragging a bin across the frozen ground.

"You didn't ask," she said, without turning.

I said that she had asked me not to.

"I know. I was waiting to see if you would." She was quiet. "Nobody has not asked. In two years. It's very tiring, being a person who isn't asked."

I said I could ask now.

"No," she said. "I'll tell you. It isn't the same."

I'm going to put down what she told me as she told me, with nothing added. I have thought a great deal about whether to put it in. She said I could. She said she would rather it was somewhere than nowhere.

It was in the Academy, in her own studio, in a winter four years into the occupation, at about nine at night. She was alone. A soldier came in. He was drunk, or he was something like it, and he was very young, and he had a rifle that he put down on the piano in order to have both hands free. She said, "I won't tell you what he said. I'll tell you he didn't say it in anger." She had the violin in one hand and the bow in the other. She had been practising. She put the violin down on the floor, carefully, because it was the only thing she could think of that she did not want broken.

She said that the bow broke in the struggle. A carbon bow does not break cleanly. It splits. She said she remembers the sound. And she said that she remembers, very clearly, that she did not decide anything. Her hands did it. She was watching it, from somewhere behind her own eyes, with a sort of horror, and with interest, as one watches a thing in a window. She said that she had always assumed that if it ever came to it she would have a choice. It had not occurred to her that there might not be one. It had not occurred to her that the part of her that would act was a part she had never met.

When it was over he was not breathing. She did not look for a long time. She picked up the violin. She did not pick up the bow. She went out through the back, down the service stair, and walked to the river.

"I don't know what happened to it," she said. "To the bow. I never went back. Someone must have found him. Someone must have found it. I don't know if it's still there. I don't want to."

She turned from the window.

"My grandmother said, whatever you do to him, it will still be done. She was right. I'd like to tell her she was right. I would like to tell her that I understand it now, and that I'd rather I had not."

I did not say anything. I had learned from watching her with the boy and the dropped bow that there are moments when the right thing is to wait. She stood, looking at me, with her arms folded across the cardigan, and I understood that she was not looking for absolution. She was looking to see whether anything in my face changed.

I do not think it did. I have tried to be certain. She nodded, once, as if I had passed something, and went to the piano and began to put the plastic chairs away.

Diana's note against the transcript was the last she wrote about Tanja, and it is the only one I haven't been able to place. It is in pencil. It reads: *I did not ask. She did not tell me. I think she was grateful for both.* Under it, so faint that I did not see it until I held the page to the window: *Lucasz knows. I have not decided whether that is a kindness.*

## Interlude III: Major Budanov, Aleja Szucha

*This day is a reconstruction, and I owe the reader an account of how it was made. Major Budanov wrote nothing about himself. These are what he left: a procedures binder of 212 pages, ring-bound, in Russian and in a translation into Polish that is good and has been proofread; an appointments diary, leather, with a ribbon; the stationery drawer of a desk, inventoried by a clerk and photographed by a member of the resistance who was, for six weeks, employed to dust the room; a menu card from the officers' canteen; and the screening record, in 4,100 numbered forms, of which I have read some three hundred. The papers give hours, procedures and a hand. The man himself is not in them, and I never met him. I asked those who served under him, and a few would speak of him, and I say below what they said. I have therefore written one day of his life in his voice, in the present tense, since a binder is not a man. I have not invented his manners. They are on every page. I have invented the order of the day, and where I have had to guess I have guessed in his favour, because the record gives me no reason to do otherwise. When I speak for myself instead of for him, it is in brackets and in italics, and it begins with the name of its source: binder, diary, drawer, card, record, interview, or mine, for what I have supplied.*

I rise at 05:45. I do not use an alarm. I have, since the Academy, risen at 05:45, and the body is a better instrument than any clock that can be bought.

*\[Diary. The first entry on any page is breakfast, at 06:40. The hour of waking is mine, and so is the clock he does not use. The diary gives me a man who is never late, and I have built the rest to suit.\]*

The room I sleep in is on the third floor of the north wing. It was a room belonging to a magistrate. The bed is a single, iron, with a mattress of horsehair that I had changed for a firmer one in the first week. On the wall there is a picture of the Vistula at Kazimierz in oils, in a gilt frame, which I have left. It is a competent picture. The river is blue in it, and there are barges.

I shower. The water is heated by a boiler in the basement that the engineers have restored, and it comes at 41 degrees, and I allow it to run for four minutes. I use a bar of soap, white, unscented, of a kind that is made in a factory in the east of the country that I understand is now my own. I do not use a flannel. I use my hands. I wash in an order, which is the scalp, the face, the neck, the left arm and the right, the chest, the back, the legs, the feet, and I do it without hurry, because a man who is hurried in the morning is a man who is hurried at noon.

I shave with a straight razor. It was my grandfather's. It has an ivory handle and a blade of Solingen steel, and I strop it on a strap of horse leather that hangs from a hook beside the mirror, twenty strokes on each side. I use a badger brush and a soap in a wooden bowl. I begin at the left sideburn and I shave downward, with the grain, then I lather and shave across it, and then I do the upper lip, and the chin, and the throat. I rinse with cold water. I apply an astringent of witch hazel and a balm that has no scent. I do not apply cologne. A man who is to speak to the frightened should not smell of anything.

I dress. The shirt is white, cotton, ironed by the orderly, and has a collar of exactly the height that the tailor in Kyiv determined, over three fittings, to be correct. The tunic is dark green and has no medals on it, because the medals are in a drawer, and I do not think a medal is a courtesy. The boots are black, and are polished by me, with a cloth, with a tin of wax. I do this at 06:20 and it takes eleven minutes. It is the one thing in the day that I will not give to an orderly.

*\[Mine. The bedroom, the bed, the picture of the river and its barges, the water, the razor, the strop, the shirt and the boots. The record does not say how he kept his body, and no one I asked would say. I have guessed in his favour, as I said at the start.\]*

At 06:40 I take breakfast alone in the small dining room on the ground floor, at a table by the window. It is a hard roll, a boiled egg, a cup of black tea with a slice of lemon, and a dish of plum jam. I eat the egg with a spoon from the shell. I crack the top with the back of the spoon, three taps, and I take the top off in one movement. I do not read at breakfast. I look at the courtyard. There is a plane tree in it, and under the plane tree there is a bench, and on the bench there is, every morning, a pigeon.

*\[Card. The canteen card is dated, and gives the roll, the egg, the tea with lemon and the plum jam. It does not say that he ate alone, or that he ate with a spoon. The diary mentions a pigeon once, in his hand, in the margin of a page. I have given him one every morning.\]*

At 07:15 I walk the corridor of the second floor. I do not have to. I do it so that the clerks see me. I say good morning to each of them by name. I know each name, and the name of the clerk's wife, or husband, or mother, and I ask after them. *Good morning, Sergeant Kovalenko. How is your mother's hip?* A man does his work better when the major knows his mother's hip.

*\[Diary. The names and the questions are in the margins of the diary, in his hand: Kovalenko, mother, hip. He wrote them down so that he could ask. I have not decided whether that makes it better or worse.\]*

At 07:30 I sit. My office is room 214. It is a large room with two tall windows and a ceiling of plaster with a rose in the centre, and a desk of oak, which was the desk of the magistrate, and on the desk are the following: a blotter of green leather, a lamp with a brass base and a green glass shade, a pen stand of black marble with two pens, a bottle of ink, a tray with three folders, a glass paperweight with a thistle inside it, a telephone in cream bakelite, and a card in a frame. The card says, in my own hand: *Courtesy is the cheapest instrument we have.*

*\[Interview. The desk is from the photographs taken by the one who dusted the room, and the clerk's inventory agrees with them item by item. The card in the frame is in a photograph, and the hand on it is his. I spoke to that person once. I was told that nothing on the desk was ever moved, and that this was why the work was easy. I believe the photographs more than I believe the account.\]*

The pen is a fountain pen with a gold nib, a 14 carat, medium. The ink is black. I write with a particular hand, and I will say, because it has been remarked upon by those who have received letters from me, that my capital D is something that I am proud of. It is a thing I learned in a school with a very strict teacher. The upright of the letter is perfectly straight, and the bowl is a half-circle, drawn in one stroke, without lifting, and at the top it has a small flourish that curls left before it meets the stem. It is a letter that looks like an invitation. I write it, on average, nine times in a day. I write it at the head of each form that I authorise, in the box that says *Decision*.

*\[Record. The D is on every form I have read, and the hand is the same on all of them. The upright, the half-circle bowl and the flourish that curls left are as I found them. The pen, the school and the pride are mine.\]*

At 08:00 I read the overnight reports. There are fourteen. I read each of them twice. The first time I read for fact, and the second for what has been left out. At 08:40 I have Captain Isaev in. He is the officer of the day at the checkpoint on the Poniatowski Bridge, and he is thirty-one, and sweats a little. I offer him tea. He declines. I offer it again, and he takes it. We discuss the night. There were 212 crossings, of which 190 were assessed on the bridge at Stage 2 or 3 and passed on to the reception hall on the left bank. There were 22 who did not pass. I ask him what became of the 22. He tells me. I thank him.

At 09:30 I go down.

I will describe the screening, because it is what I am for, and because I am proud of it, and because there are people who have seen it, and described it, who do not understand that it was designed.

The screening is a procedure of seven steps. It is in the binder, on pages 61 to 88, and it was written by a committee that I did not sit on, and revised by me, in the first week, to take out the unnecessary. I have a copy of the procedure at my desk. It begins: *The purpose of screening is to establish, in the shortest time and with the least distress, the stage of each subject.* The word *distress* is mine.

*\[Binder. Pages 61 to 88, in his order. The seven steps are theirs. The sentence about the purpose of screening is on page 61, in the revised text, and the revision is initialled with a D. The pride is mine.\]*

The screening room is on the lower ground floor. It was a cell. It is now a clean white room of three metres by four, with a floor of grey rubber, a drain in the centre of it, and a ceiling of acoustic tile. There is a table, and two chairs. There is a lamp on the table that gives a warm light of 2700 kelvin, because the cold light makes the subjects afraid. On the table there is a jug of water and two glasses, and a box of tissues, and a small dish of boiled sweets, lemon and strawberry, in cellophane. The room is kept at 21 degrees. It smells faintly of lavender, because I have it wiped down with a lavender solution at the end of each session.

I will list the steps.

*One. Reception.* The subject is brought in. He is not bound. He is not hooded. He is walked in by one guard, who stays at the door. I rise. I say, in Polish, *Good morning. Thank you for coming. Please, sit.* I have learned the Polish for this from a woman who was a teacher of the language at the university, and who was, I think, glad to be asked. I do not use the formal address *Pan* with those under the age of twenty, and I use the formal with everyone else. I pour the water.

*Two. Observation.* I sit. I say nothing for a time. The period is between ninety seconds and three minutes, and I time it from the second hand of my watch. I watch the hands. I watch the eyes. I watch the way that the subject sits in the chair, and I watch what they do with the glass. At Stage 1, the subject will refuse the water. At Stage 2, the subject drinks it quickly and with a tremor. At Stage 3, the subject drinks it steadily and then holds the glass in both hands. At Stage 4, the subject does not drink it, and does not decline. At Stage 4 the subject looks at the glass, as one looks at a clock, and says, *That is very kind.*

*Three. Baseline.* I ask five questions. They are in the procedure and I ask them in the order that is written. The name. The address. The date of birth. The occupation. The names of the nearest relations. I write the answers on the form with the pen, in my hand. I note the latency of each answer, in seconds, in the margin: *1.2. 0.8. 1.4.* A latency of more than four seconds on a question of this kind is a point of interest.

*Four. The offer.* I take a sweet from the dish. I say, *Would you like one?* This is the step that has been most remarked on, by the people who do not understand. It has a purpose, and the purpose is not what is thought. It is the test of the reflex of acceptance. At Stage 3 about half of subjects will take the sweet. At Stage 4 all of them will. I have never known a Stage 4 subject to refuse. I note the sweet that is taken.

*Five. Questions.* I ask the subject for the names of persons known to be hostile to the order of the district. I ask in a manner that is entirely courteous. I say, *I wonder if you can help me.* I do not raise my voice. I do not touch the subject. It is, I have found, extremely seldom necessary to do either.

I should say that I have been told by the engineers that for a subject in the first days of exposure, it is sometimes helpful if the room is quiet. A low sound is introduced through a speaker beneath the table. It is in the range of 180 hertz and has a slow beat, of six seconds, in the manner of a breath. It is not loud. The subject is not told of it. I have sat in the room with it for four hours without any effect that I could discern. I am told that I am not susceptible. I am told that this is a rare and useful thing in an interviewer.

*\[Binder. An annex of one page, headed Acoustic Conditioning, gives the frequency and the beat. It is unsigned. That he sat in it for four hours, and was told that he was not susceptible, I take from a line in the diary in his own hand. I do not know who told him.\]*

*Six. Recording.* The subject's answers are transcribed by the clerk, who is in the adjoining room, behind the glass. The transcription is typed on the form, in triplicate, and I initial each copy with a D.

*Seven. Disposition.* I write the stage. I write the decision. The decisions are of four kinds: *Release. Retain. Transfer. Treat.* I do not say what the last two mean. I do not say it on the form. A form should be clean.

*\[Record. The four words are printed on the form, in the box beside the D. What the last two meant is not printed on any form I have read, and I have read some three hundred of the 4,100. I say that here because it matters.\]*

At the end of the session I rise. I say, *Thank you. You have been very helpful.* I shake the hand. I walk them to the door myself. This is a courtesy that does not have to be shown, and I show it.

Between 09:30 and 13:00 I conduct nine. I will describe them in a single paragraph, because it is the manner in which I think of them. A man of fifty-eight, a bus driver, Stage 4, who gives eleven names without being asked. A girl of sixteen with a plaster on her wrist, Stage 2, who says nothing. She is *Retained.* A woman of thirty-three, a nurse, Stage 3, who looks at me for a long time and says, *You have a very good voice.* A man of seventy with a hearing aid, who is *Released.* A boy of twelve who has a drawing of a bird. A teacher. A priest. I take the sweet from the dish nine times and offer it nine times, and I am aware, at each, of the small dry sound of the cellophane.

*\[Record. Nine forms of one morning survive in sequence, and I have taken the ages, stages and decisions from them. The nurse's remark about his voice is in the margin, in his hand. The gestures, and the boy's bird, are mine.\]*

At 13:00 I wash my hands. I wash them at the basin in the corridor with the white soap for forty seconds, and I dry them with a clean towel, and I walk upstairs to the dining room.

Lunch is soup, a plate of boiled beef with horseradish, and a glass of kvass. I sit at the table by the window and I eat everything. It is a rule of mine. At 13:40 there is a pause that I fill with the daily letter, which is the thing that I would like, in particular, to explain.

Each day I write a letter. It is to a woman who is not my wife. She is, in so far as I can describe her, a colleague of my late father's, who taught me my letters and who is now in a home outside Poltava, eighty-nine years old, and unable to read. A nurse reads them to her. I write a page, in my best hand, in blue-black ink on cream paper that comes from a stationer in Lviv. I tell her what the weather has been. I tell her what I have eaten. I tell her about the plane tree and the pigeon. I do not tell her what I do. I have the sense that she knows. I sign the letter with a *D*, and the D is perfect.

*\[Drawer. The inventory gives the cream paper, a stationer's wrapper from Lviv, and a bottle of blue-black ink. It gives no letters. The woman, the home outside Poltava and the nurse are mine. I wanted one person in his day whom he did not frighten, and the record would not give me one.\]*

At 15:00 I have a visitor. I will not describe the visitor, because it is not my business, except to say that there are two of them, and that they carry, between them, a case of grey plastic with brass corners, and that they have been in the building for three weeks. They do not give their names. They sit on the sofa and they ask me questions about my numbers. I answer them as I answer everyone. I give them the figures. I am courteous.

*\[Diary. The 15:00 entry is a closed bracket in his hand, repeated on every page for three weeks. The one who dusted the room saw two men in the corridor, once, with a grey case, and nothing more. The sofa and the questions about his numbers are mine.\]*

At 17:00 I walk in the courtyard. I walk under the plane tree. I walk ten laps, clockwise. The pigeon is on the bench. It is a grey pigeon, a common one, with an iridescent patch on the neck and a left foot that has no toes. I have named it, in my own mind, and I will not say what. It looks at me with an orange eye. I am aware that it is not afraid.

At 18:30 I dine. At 19:30 I take the evening papers. At 20:00 I hear, from across the river, thin, borne on the wind, the voice of the woman on the loudspeaker. *There are beds. There is hot water.* I stop with the fork in my hand. I listen for as long as it takes. Then I put the fork down, and I take out a handkerchief, and I fold it into four, and I touch it to my lips.

*\[Transcript. The words are Magda Wiśniewska's, as before. That he stopped to listen, and what he did with the handkerchief, are mine, and I have no authority for either.\]*

At 21:30 I write the day's last form. It is the form for the file. I write at the head of it the date, and the word *Decision*, and I write in the box a *D*, the best of the day, an upright with a bowl and a small flourish at the top. I hold the pen above the page for a moment longer than I need to.

At 22:00 I go to bed. I lie on the horsehair mattress under the picture of the river with its barges. I do not dream. I have been told this is one of the signs, and I have been told that it is not a sign in my case. I am not susceptible.

I should like it to be recorded, if anyone should ever read this, that I was always polite.

*\[Interview. I asked those who served under him. A few would speak, and each said one thing only, and said it in the same words: that he was always polite. He did not say it himself. I have put it in his mouth because it is all that anyone was willing to give me.\]*

§&#32;

Everything above was made. What follows was found, and after it comes what I have supposed. The voice is mine again, and not his.

*I found the screening records in the end in a basement in Praga, under a tarpaulin, in eleven boxes. They are in the order in which they were made. I have counted the sweets. I have counted, in the margins, the note that Major Budanov made, in the same hand, of the sweet that was taken. The strawberry is taken by the young. The lemon by the old. Of 4,100 forms, the box for the sweet is blank on one: a woman of forty-one, an interpreter, Stage 0. In the margin, in the best capital D that I have ever seen, there is a single line. It is not in the procedure. It says: She did not take it. I am curious about her.*

I kept this day because the forms are the only part of him that did not need me. The politeness is in the record. The man who is polite is mine, and he should not be taken for a witness. I have read that last line more often than any other in the eleven boxes.

## Chapter 8: The Medic

**ALEX** · Kraków, February 2036

It took three months to get Catalina Chmielewska to speak to me, and I want to say what it took, because it is the only interview in this book that I had to earn by doing something.

I wrote four letters. The first was answered by a young man at her organisation, who said that Dr Chmielewska was not a doctor and was not available. The second was not answered. The third came back with a single line in a firm hand across the bottom: *Why?* I wrote four pages. The fourth letter was an address in Kraków and a date, with no salutation, and under it: *Come at seven. Wear shoes you can stand in.*

I came at seven. The organisation ran a clinic out of a former bank, with the vault used for stores, and she put me on the water. For nine hours I carried bottles, wheeled a trolley, held a lamp for a man with a gashed shin and, for twenty minutes, a baby. She did not speak to me except to give instructions. She was forty-one now. She was lean and quick, with grey in her hair that she had not troubled to remove, and the same trick I have since seen in nurses in four countries of seeing a whole room and a single patient at once.

At four o'clock she said, "That will do." She took me to a back office with two chairs and a kettle, and sat me down, and said, "Now you know what it is that you're asking. Do you want to ask it?"

I said that I did. I understood that she had needed to see whether I could be of use before she could bear to be of interest. It is a distinction she has built a life on.

I will set down the nine hours, because she asked me, afterwards, whether I had noticed anything, and I said no, and she said, "You will," and she was right.

The bank had been built in 1911 for a firm of grain merchants, with a banking hall forty feet high and a counter of black marble, and the marble was still there, with the grilles removed. A queue ran along it, and a woman in a green tabard stood at the head with a roll of numbered tickets, the kind used at a delicatessen counter, tearing them off one by one and handing them down. The numbers were in red. I was given forty-seven. "You're not a patient," she said, "but you're in the queue, and you'll wait like one." I waited like one for twelve minutes, in a line of people who stood close together and spoke very little, and who had, each of them, the appearance of bringing a thing in a bag.

Then Catalina came out through a swing door in her shirtsleeves, looked down the hall, and said, in a voice pitched exactly to be heard at the far end without being raised, "Forty-seven. Water." I went. She did not turn to see that I had followed.

The vault was at the back. A round steel door, a metre thick, standing open on its hinge like the lid of a very large pot, and behind it a room that had held the grain firm's deeds and now held the clinic's store. The shelves were steel, to the ceiling, with a ladder on a rail. Everything on them was in a box, and every box was labelled in the same marker, in capitals, and under each label was a number in a second colour. PARACETAMOL 500. 3,100. AMOXICILLIN 500. 212. ORAL REHYDRATION. 1,640. SUTURE, 3-0. 96. At the end of the room stood a small desk with a lamp and a ledger bound in black cloth, and I saw her glance at it as we passed, the way a person glances at a sleeping child.

"Everything that goes out is written in the book," she said. "Everything that comes in is written in the book. At the end of the day the book and the shelves must say the same thing. When they don't, I find out why. It hasn't happened since March."

I asked what had happened in March.

"Eleven tablets," she said. "A volunteer. He had a mother." She put a hand on the ladder. "I did not report him. I gave him the eleven tablets. I wrote them in the book, with his name beside them, as a loan, and he repaid them in April. It is the only entry in the ledger in two colours."

The water was in a corner, in plastic bottles of one and a half litres, stacked in crates of six. She showed me the trolley, which had one bad wheel, and the route, which ran down the length of the hall behind the marble counter, and the rule, which was that I was to put a bottle into the hand of anyone who was standing and to watch their face while I did it. "If they take it and drink, it's nothing. If they take it and hold it, I want to know. If they don't take it, tell me at once." I asked why. "Because the ones who don't take it are the ones who aren't thirsty," she said. "And the ones who aren't thirsty are the ones I'm afraid for."

I pushed the trolley. It squealed on the left front. I gave out bottles to a hundred and ten people, I think, in a morning, though I did not count exactly, and I watched the faces as she had said, and mostly they were what I have described, hands, a drink, a nod. A man of seventy took his bottle and said, with perfect courtesy, "Thank you, young man," and drank, and I felt absurdly proud. A girl of ten took hers and held it against her chest like a doll, and did not drink, and did not look up. I stopped. I looked down the hall. Catalina, at the far end, with her back to me and a stethoscope round her neck, did not turn, but she lifted two fingers of her right hand and let them fall. I understood that she had been watching all along, in the glass of a cabinet, or the reflection on the marble, or simply with the part of the mind that notices what a room is doing. I knelt down. I asked the girl her name. She said, "Weronika," and her mother, behind her, said, "She doesn't drink at the clinic. She thinks it's medicine." I took the bottle, opened it, drank some, and handed it back. She watched me. She drank.

"Good," said Catalina, behind me. She had crossed the hall without my hearing. "It's the commonest thing I do. Showing someone that it's safe."

At noon there was soup, in the kitchen of the old manager's flat above the hall, at a table covered in oilcloth, with six people eating in turn, one of whom was a man asleep with his head on his arm and a spoon in his hand. She ate standing. She had a method for it, which I observed and could not quite describe, in which she took the spoon in her left hand, held a pencil in her right, and made small marks on the back of her wrist, a stroke for each patient, a cross for each referral, and now and again a ring, which I learned was for a death. There was one ring that morning. She did not look at it.

In the afternoon I held a lamp. A man had come in from a farm outside the town with a shin laid open to the bone by a piece of sheet metal, and she cleaned it, and stitched it, with her head very close to the wound and her glasses pushed up on her forehead, and talked to him all the while about his dog. The dog was called Bruno. Bruno, she gathered, had eaten a pair of his boots. She asked how many. The man said, "Both, Pani doktor, in the one night." She said, "Then he must be very fond of you," and tied off the last suture, and I saw the man's shoulders go down by an inch and a half. He was not looking at the wound at all. She had made sure he would not.

I was told not to call her Doctor. She wasn't one. I was told that she had trained on the job and in the dark and in the back of vans, and had, in the years since the first invasion, closed more wounds than most surgeons in their careers, and that if I wanted to see her angry, I should call her Doctor in front of a patient. I did not try.

At half past one there was a lull, and she sat on an upturned crate in the vault with her back against the steel shelves and her eyes shut. I did not know whether I was permitted to speak. I put down the lamp. After a minute she said, without opening her eyes, "I used to write software for hospitals. Did you know? Scheduling. To make sure the right doctor was in the right room at the right time." She let this lie for a moment. "It never once occurred to me that the right doctor might not exist."

At half past two I held a baby. It was small, hot and heavy, with a cap of black hair stuck to its skull, and the mother had gone to be sick in the yard. Catalina handed it to me without ceremony and said, "Hold his head," and returned to the cough she was listening to. I held its head. I don't think I have ever been so afraid of anything so light. It looked at me with the dark unfocused regard of the very young, and then it put its fist in my collar and held on, and I stood in the vault under a single bulb with a stranger's child, for twenty minutes, not daring to shift my feet, and heard, in the hall beyond the open door, the whole sound of the place: the squeal of the trolley with someone else behind it, the murmur of the queue, the click of her pencil. It was the longest twenty minutes of my year.

When she took him back she looked at me properly for the first time. "Hm," she said. It was all. But I saw something go through her face, an adjustment, like a person moving a weight from one hand to the other.

The last patient left at ten to four. She locked the vault. She put the key into her breast pocket and buttoned the pocket, and she read the ledger and ran a finger down the shelves, and then she stood for a moment with both hands on the black marble of the counter and her head down, and I saw how tired she was, and how she held it.

"That will do," she said.

Diana met her at Brodno in the first week of October. I have set the entry first because it is the only account in the archive of her at work, and everything she says afterwards is about what the work was for.

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 11 October 2034

**SY/CC/0022\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date:** 11 October 2034, 06:40*

***Location:** OP-Brodno, the old hospital*

***Conditions: **cold, grey, a light mist off the water*

***Temperature:** 6°C*

***Pressure: **1017 hPa, steady*

***Flood: **stable. Waterline unchanged on the last four readings.*

*I came to Brodno to ask about the clinic and I spent the morning carrying water.*

*The hospital stands on a low rise and has kept its feet dry. A long building in grey brick with four wings and a chapel. The ground floor is a ward. The first floor is a ward. Everything that is not a ward is a store, and everything that is not a store is a bed. It is not tidy. It is orderly, which is a different thing, and I recognised it at once: it is the order of a place where somebody is making a decision every ninety seconds, and has built the rest of the building around the decisions.*

*Catalina Chmielewska is forty. Before the war she was a software engineer, in Warsaw, in a firm that made scheduling systems for hospitals, which she says is a joke she did not intend. After the invasion she trained as a volunteer medic. She came to the zone eighteen months ago, from Kraków, when anyone sensible was going the other way. She did not say why. A boy who was mopping the floor told me what she is. "She's the one who tells you if it's a cough or if it's the cough." Everyone I asked used the same word for her. Selfless.*

*I introduced myself. She nodded, once, and said, "Can you lift?" and put a basin in my hands.*

*I will set down the morning in the order it occurred, because I don't want to inflate it.*

*07:10. Water, from the cistern in the yard to the ground-floor ward. Eleven trips.*

*08:00. A child with a fever of 39.4. She didn't look at the thermometer. She looked at the child's eyes, then the mother's. "It's the water, but it isn't the bad water. We have a day." The mother cried. Catalina did not touch her. She said, "I need you to hold his head up. Can you do that? That's the most important thing in the room."*

*09:30. The ledger. I want to record this carefully. In the dispensary there is a ledger, a school exercise book with a green cover, and in it she writes the stock of every drug in the hospital and, beside it, a number which is not a stock count. I asked what it was. She said, "That's the number of people who won't get it." She has a rule, and she has written it on the inside cover. It is a rule for who receives the antibiotics, and it is a good rule. It is the fairest I have seen anyone write in this city. It leaves, by her own arithmetic, nine people a week who will not be treated.*

*10:15. A man was brought in with a hand that had been through a window. She sewed it herself, with a curved needle, talking throughout about nothing.*

*It was then that I saw the other man. He had come in at about half past nine by the east door, a thin man in his mid-thirties with a good coat gone shabby, carrying a clipboard, and he went to the end of the ward and began to count bandages. He did not speak. He did not look at her. She passed through the ward twice and, the second time, as she went by, she put her hand on a cot rail about forty centimetres from where his hand was on the same rail. Neither moved. They stayed like that for as long as it takes to read a chart. Then she went on.*

*Pawel. I had the name from the boy. "He does the stores. He used to be a banker. He won't carry a gun."*

*At eleven Goya came in with a Runner of about thirteen, who had a bullet through the meat of the forearm and was behaving, with admirable stiffness, as if it were a bee sting. Goya put the child on a cot. He turned, saw Pawel at the end of the ward, and said, in a voice that carried the whole length of the room, "Oh. The banker. Count that." He pointed at the Runner's blood on the floor.*

*Pawel did not answer. He went on counting. I saw that his ears had gone red, and that his pencil had stopped, and that it began again. Goya looked at him for a long second with an expression I could not name and then, because he cannot help it, swore. Catalina did not look up. She said, quite pleasantly, "Goya, you are bleeding on my floor," and he looked down, and he was.*

*I do not know what I saw. I have written it down as what I saw.*

*I will add one thing. A person who does not rest is hiding something. I recognise the manner of it. For the first time in a while I did not want to know what it was.*

*\[in pencil, margin: forty centimetres. measure it again tomorrow. and the ledger: nine a week. who is the ninth.\]*

\* \* \*

**CATALINA** · recording, Kraków, February 2036

**SY/CC/0001\_GR** · *Catalina Chmielewska, recorded in Kraków, February 2036*

I'll tell you the reason first, because the reason is what people seem to want. They're polite enough not to ask.

I was a software engineer. I was good at it. I was married, to a man I won't name because he has a right not to be in this, and we divorced when I was thirty-two, and the thing that I took from that was a sentence. A friend said it. She said, "You were never there." She meant that I had been present at every dinner and absent from every one. I thought about that for two years. And then the war came and I decided what I would really be there for.

I trained. I was a good medic. It's the same trade, you know. It's scheduling. It's deciding what happens to whom, and in what order, with not enough of anything. I came to Warsaw because it was the worst place. I'm going to say that plainly. I wasn't brave. I thought that if I went to the place where I was most needed, and stayed there, and gave everything, then I would have paid for it. I believed in a kind of ledger. I thought there was one, somewhere, and that mine was in the red.

Diana saw the ledger first. I mean the green exercise book. She asked me about the number beside the stock. I said it was the number who wouldn't be treated. I said it as if it were a fact of nature. And she said, "Who decides who's in it?" And I said, I do. And she looked at me, and she wrote nothing down.

Now. Pawel.

He had been a banker. He was thirty-five. He was in Warsaw because his father was in a nursing home in Praga and couldn't be moved, and when the water came he wouldn't leave him. He stayed until the old man died. Then he stayed because he didn't know where else to go. He wouldn't carry a weapon. That was the whole of his reputation. He was called a coward by the men who had been in Mariupol, who had a right to a view. I never thought it. I thought, here is a man who has decided, at some cost, what he won't do.

It began in the stores. I'm not going to say that it was a great love affair. It began because I was tired, and he held a door. That's the true account. He held a door open with his foot and carried a crate through it, and he said, "You should sit," and I did. We sat in the stores for twenty minutes, on two crates, and didn't speak. I thought: this is the first time in two years that someone has told me what to do and been right.

You'll want to know what I felt guilty about. It wasn't the shame that people expect, because I wasn't married. It was something I can only describe by saying that I had come to the zone to be a person who needed nothing. And I needed him. I needed a man to hold a door. The community had decided what I was. They had built me out of the thing they needed, which was somebody who didn't tire, and I had let them, because it was easier than being a person. And a person who's in love isn't a saint. She's a woman who wants things.

It went on for eight months. And then he stole.

I'll say what it was. There were forty courses of an antibiotic in the dispensary. I had counted them. It was the last of the last delivery, and by my rule they were allocated: to the children, to the post-operative, to a man who had been shot. There was a woman in the Zoo, a Mrs Lewandowska, with pneumonia, who wasn't on the list. She was forty-six. She had a daughter, fourteen, who had been stealing food from half the district to keep her alive and who had come to the hospital door at night and stood there without knocking. I knew her. I had seen her at the door. I hadn't let her in, because she wasn't on the list.

Pawel took one course. He took it out of the dispensary at three in the morning, with the key I had left on a hook, and he walked to the Zoo, and he put it in the girl's hand. He told me in the morning. He said, "I've taken one. It's on the ledger. You can put me in the number."

I'll tell you what I did, because it's the thing I'm most ashamed of and also the thing I'm proudest of. I did not shout. I did not do anything. I sat there for about a minute. And then I opened the green book and I looked at the rule. It was the fairest rule I had ever seen. And I understood that it was also the way I had made myself safe. I had made a rule so I would never have to look at a particular face. And a man I loved had looked at a face.

There was a hearing. The Brigade has them. It sat in the chapel on a Thursday, thirty people on the pews. I was asked whether the theft should be reported. The question was put to me because it was my ledger. And I stood up and said, the rule was wrong. I said it was a good rule and it was wrong, and that I had written it so as not to be present, and that the theft was mine. I said that. And then, because I was already standing and it had gone so far, I said the other thing. I said that Pawel and I had been together for eight months.

There was a silence. Then someone at the back, an old man, said, "We know." And a woman said, "Everyone knows." And somebody laughed, kindly, and then a good many people did.

I hadn't expected that. What they had been waiting for, it turned out, was not for me to confess a love. For me, it was to need something. They had been waiting for eight months for me to ask. Nobody had been able to think how to give me anything. You can't give a thing to a person who has arranged her life so that she doesn't receive. It's a kind of tyranny. It looks like virtue.

The mother lived. Her daughter was named Alisia. She didn't say thank you. She looked at me with a very particular, flat expression, and the following week there was a tin of peaches on the dispensary shelf that hadn't been there before. I have never been repaid so exactly.

Pawel and I are still together. We haven't married. There's a certain pride in that. He does the accounts for the organisation, and he's, I may tell you, extremely good at them. I'd like it said that there's no one in Poland who is better at deciding what a crate is worth.

I'll tell you what I learned. I'm forty-one. I'm still learning it. It's that you can be loved, and also of use, and that these aren't in competition. And that when someone has decided what they need you to be, you owe them, sometimes, the courage to disappoint them.

*(end of recording)*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · Kraków, February 2036

When she had finished it was dark outside the office window and the kettle had gone cold. She hadn't once looked at the recorder.

There was a knock. A man came in with two mugs on a tray. He was in his mid-forties, thin, in a good coat that had lost some of its edge, and he put one mug in front of each of us and then stood, uncertain, at the end of the desk, as though awaiting a ruling.

"Pawel," she said. "Alex."

He shook my hand. His hand was dry and cool. He did not say what he did and I did not ask. He looked at her, and she looked at him, and the distance between them was, I would estimate, about forty centimetres.

"Did she tell you about the peaches?" he said.

I said that she had.

"They were stolen," he said. "I should say. I never told her. I know the shop."

Catalina closed her eyes. I thought she was going to laugh. She did, finally, a short tired surprised sound, and she said, "Of course they were." And Pawel, who was a coward, who had been a coward for four years in the eyes of every man who had a rifle, looked at her with the face of a man who had just been forgiven for something he was proud of.

Diana's note against the transcript is the only one in the archive addressed to someone other than herself. It reads, in pencil, *Catalina. You are not hiding. You are holding three things. It is allowed. It is very heavy. It is allowed.* And underneath, almost at the margin: *The ninth. It was never a number. It was Alisia's mother, and she was on the ledger all along.*

## Chapter 9: The Dendron (Protocol 003)

**ALEX** · 2036

*Binder one. Reproduced as found.*

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · Protocol 003

**SYNPTCL/PROTOCOL/003 · THE DENDRON**

**Compiled: D. Antonoff, with clinical notes by C. Chmielewska · Status: ACTIVE · Reliability: PROB\_VAL\_0.6**

**1. SCOPE**

This document describes the organism known in the corridor as the Dendron: its appearance, its spread, its effects on the human body and mind, and the means by which those effects can be reduced. It is the least reliable of the three Protocols compiled so far. The organism has not been clinically studied. It has been observed, and the observers were mostly afraid.

The method is my father's. He kept the archive of a chemical works in Kharkiv for thirty years, and he taught me that an unknown substance is first of all a filing problem: where does it go, what is it adjacent to, what would be missing from the shelf if it were removed. I have applied that method here. It does not make the subject less frightening. It makes the fear legible.

The name is a survivor's name. *Dendron* is Greek for tree. The organism is not a tree.

**2. WHAT IT IS (U)**

The following account rests on two sources. The first is secondhand. The second is the man described in section 10 (PROB\_VAL\_0.5).

The Dendron is reported to be an organism of great age, recovered from permafrost in northern Siberia before the war. It was taken to Soviet laboratories, studied for decades, and in the last years before the invasion made available for military use. It is not a plant. It is not a fungus. It is not an animal. It has been described to me, by the man who worked in a Russian laboratory and fled, as "the first thing that was alive before we decided what alive meant."

I record this and decline to assess it.

**3. APPEARANCE (M)**

The Dendron has been seen, photographed and handled by me. Its appearance is as follows.

It grows as a vine. The stem is the width of a finger and the colour of wet slate. It is flexible but not elastic. It has no leaves. Along its length, at intervals of about nine centimetres, it carries small paired structures that open when the temperature is above 8°C and close when it is lower. When open they are the grey-white of a mushroom gill. When closed they are not visible.

It climbs. Walls, pipes, railings, the pillars of overpasses, the iron of the tram wires. It prefers damp and shade. It prefers the dark inside things. A drain with no vegetation in it in June will, by September, have a mass of Dendron the size of a man's torso.

**3.1. The sound.** The Dendron makes a sound. It was first reported to me by a Runner as a hum and I did not believe her. I have since recorded it on eleven nights from the roof of the control room (M, instrument: the Zoom recorder, 44.1 kHz). It is a sustained tone at approximately 180 Hz with a slow amplitude beat of about once every six seconds. It carries over water. It is loudest at night and when the air is still. It has the quality, to the ear, of a siren heard through a wall: a warning with its edges taken off. The beat is not constant. It slows, and then, for a minute or two, it stops, and the silence that follows is more noticeable than the sound. I have no explanation for it. I note only that the sound resembles an alarm that has been muffled before it reached the listener, and that those exposed to the compound do not appear to hear it.

It absorbs water. At the Żerań cooling channel, where I measured it, a mature mass lowered the local water level by 11 centimetres in nine days (M). The water does not go anywhere. It goes into the organism, and the organism swells.

**4. SPREAD**

The Dendron enters the corridor with the flood and follows it. In the first flood it was carried on the water. In the second flood it appeared in places where it had not been recorded: the lower Zoo, the Docks, the sewers of Targówek (C, several sources). It has since been found in all four classes of ground.

It clogs. This is the characteristic with the greatest effect on the survivors and the one that is discussed least. A mass in a sewer does not remove the sewer's function. It blocks it. Drains back up, the water rises, the Dendron grows in the new water. The relation is a loop. I have not found a way to describe it that does not sound like intention.

In summer it is dense and it hides. In winter it dies back, but not far. The visible part retreats and the roots stay. In the cold it opens less, and its effect is reduced. This is the most important fact in this document.

**5. EFFECTS ON THE HUMAN BODY (M, with clinical confirmation)**

The Dendron emits a chemical. It is not a toxin. It does not kill. Catalina Chmielewska, who has treated eighty-one persons with documented exposure, calls it "a sedative with ambition."

Her description of the stages follows. Times are for continuous exposure at close range, in warm conditions.

| **Stage** | **Time** | **Effect** | **Oxygen saturation** | **Blood pressure** | **Pupil dilation** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 0 to 4 hours | Mild euphoria. Warmth in the hands. Unusual calm | 97 to 98 per cent | 118/76 | 4 mm, brisk |
| 2 | 4 to 24 hours | Loss of urgency. Affect flattens. Tasks are begun and not finished | 95 to 96 per cent | 108/68 | 5 mm, slow |
| 3 | 1 to 4 days | Apathy. Obedience to simple instructions. Loss of initiative. Normal speech continues | 92 to 94 per cent | 98/62 | 6 mm, sluggish |
| 4 | 4 days and longer | Compliance. The subject will follow any instruction from any source, without hostility, and without apparent memory of having refused | 88 to 91 per cent | 92/58 | 7 mm, fixed to light |

At stage 4 the person is not distressed. This is the thing that cannot be conveyed by a table. A person at stage 4 will tell you, courteously, that they are quite all right.

Catalina's clinical note, which I reproduce without alteration: *The distinguishing feature is not what the patient lacks. It is what they no longer need.*

**6. COUNTERMEASURES**

Four measures reduce exposure. None eliminates it.

**6.1. Distance.** Effects are reduced beyond approximately 40 metres from a mass (E).

**6.2. Cold.** Below 8°C the organism closes. Exposure in winter is much less dangerous. This is the reason for the seasonal variation given in Protocol 001, section 9.

**6.3. Wind.** Upwind is safe. Downwind is not. The wind has been recorded as the principal vector of exposure in the open (C).

**6.4. Filtration.** A cotton mask soaked in vinegar and worn over the nose reduces exposure by perhaps half (E, Chmielewska). At the Filtry water station (F1), which is closest of all the survivor positions to the western ring, the supply is treated by charcoal and a three-stage sand bed. It is the only supply in the corridor that is reliably free of the compound.

I have recorded that the filtration at F1 is the reason the Occupiers have not taken F1. This is not correct. They have not taken F1 because F1 is not worth taking. The relationship is the other way round.

**7. DENDRON DUST**

The surface of a mature mass carries a pale powder in dry conditions. It can be scraped off. It is known in the corridor as Dendron Dust. In the dry months of summer the wind lifts it from the surface and carries it some distance, and it is then more widely spread than in any other season. Wet, it does not keep. Where it falls into standing water it dissolves, and the water that takes it is afterwards, in its effect, no different from water that carries the compound (C, two sources; not confirmed clinically).

Raw, it carries a trace of the compound, and breathed in on the wind it dulls as the water does. Those who gather it know how it must be scraped, dried and cut, and what is left, in the amounts used, does the opposite. They gather it in the cold months, when the organism is slow, and not in summer. It produces vivid visual phenomena. It produces a sense of vast scale, and of being addressed. It does not make people obedient. Those who have taken it describe it as the first thing that has made them feel less alone.

It is used by a small number of persons in the resistance, in rituals that I have been permitted to describe in general terms and not to attend. They take a pinch and sit in a circle in a cellar. They do not speak. In the morning they speak of what they have seen. I record this without comment. It is not for the Protocol to say what it means that an organism produces both the thing that numbs and the thing that wakes.

I have been offered it. I have declined. I have made a note of the reason, and it is not an interesting one: I am afraid I would like it.

**8. THE HUMAN CRACK**

Four per cent of the persons exposed to the compound at stage 3 or beyond did not become compliant. Catalina's data: 3 of 81 (3.7 per cent). They are not distinguished by age, sex, diet, health, nationality or politics. They are distinguished by one thing, which she has noted without explanation: all three, on the first day of exposure, were doing something for someone else.

She was not able to say whether this is a cause or a coincidence. She said, "I'd like it to be a cause." It was the only unprofessional remark I heard her make.

I place the finding here, in the middle of the Protocol, and I wish to be clear that it is the only finding in this document that is hopeful, and that it is based on three persons.

**9. LIMITATIONS**

This document records what has been observed of the organism's effects. It does not record why it has them. It does not record what it is for.

**10. THE WESTERN THREAD (PROB\_VAL\_0.5)**

I record this section with the greatest reluctance, because it is the first in the Protocols that concerns the question of who is responsible.

On 23 October a boy brought me a plastic crate that he had pulled out of the water at Czerniaków. It had contained something that had not survived. On the lid there was a label, partly torn. It was not in Russian. It was in English, or French. I have the following words from it: *Consignee*, then a gap, then a name torn away, then *Project*, and then a line that read *for post-exposure assessment*. There was a reference number with two letters I did not recognise. I put it in the second binder, label uppermost, and told no one.

On 30 October, at night, a man came to the Docks. He asked for the engineer, and then, when the engineer had been fetched and had looked at him for a long time, he asked for me. He was in his fifties, a Russian, a biochemist, very thin, in a coat that was too light for the season. He had walked, he said, for four days. He did not give a name. I have put his initial in the second binder and nowhere else.

He is the second source named in section 2. He is no longer secondhand.

I will set down what he told me and I will set it down at the reliability I give it, which is 0.5, because he is one man and I have not seen a document. He said that the organism was found in Siberia and was Russian in the sense that a stolen horse is Russian. He said that the Russian laboratories understood what they had and did not have the biotechnology to use it. He said that the expertise came from elsewhere. He said it was a joint programme, running across at least two Western nations, with private biotechnology contractors, with intelligence agencies, with military research facilities. He said that the Russians supplied the organism and the others supplied the science: how to enhance the effect, how to stabilise it in a water system, how to refine it for something that the programme's papers call population management. He said the appeal on the Western side was that there was no visible delivery mechanism, no detectable residue, and an effect that looks, in a population, like collective depression, not like an attack.

He said that Warsaw was the test.

He gave the programme a name. I have not written it here. I have written it in the second binder, on a separate page, with a date.

*\[Alex. The name is given in Protocol 005 (Chapter 15), in the entry for 28 December. The brief itself is reproduced in the second binder, as the last item filed.\]*

I asked him why he had come. He said that he had worked in the laboratory for eleven years and had never once seen a person exposed to the compound. He had seen graphs. He had walked four days through the corridor to look at one. He asked if he could see a person at stage 3. I took him to Brodno. Catalina let him sit in the ward for an hour, at a distance of two metres, with a mask. He did not speak. When he came out he sat on the step of the chapel for some time and then said, in Polish, which I had not known he had, "They don't look unhappy."

I said that was correct. I said that was the feature.

I do not know where he is now. He asked to be sent west and I have arranged it with a Runner.

I have written to no one. The Suspilne bureau in Kyiv may or may not be receiving. I have this binder, a crate, a page, and a man's word. It is not enough. I am going to make it enough. That is what the Codex is for.

**11. NOTES TO FILE**

11.1. The Dendron does not grow on the bricks of the Żerań power station. I have no explanation. I have examined it on four days. It grows up to the edge of the old coal yard, and it stops.

11.2. At the Zoo there is an old woman, Mrs Kaczmarek, who believes the Dendron has been sent to teach us patience. She leaves a saucer of milk at the foot of the biggest mass, every morning, at seven. She is at stage 1. She is not at stage 2. She has been at stage 1 for three months. I have asked Catalina how that is possible. Catalina said that the saucer is for someone else.

11.3. The word for what the Dendron does, in the language that was spoken in the laboratory where it was studied, is also the word for peace.

*\[Alex. The language is Russian. The word is mir, which means peace and also world. The verb for pacifying, "umirotvorit'", is built on it. The people who named the work heard no difference between making someone peaceful and making them quiet.\]*

*End of Protocol 003.*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · 2036

I had meant to say nothing about this Protocol. It is the one I find hardest to put in a book. It does not have a person in it. It has, instead, a great many people who are not in it, and all of them are in the section about stage 4.

Catalina, when I told her I was going to publish it, asked me to add a sentence. She said it was the only change she wanted. I have added it, here, outside the Protocol, because it was not Diana's, and because it is the part I believe.

She said: "The cold isn't a defence. It's an interval. Use it."

## Interlude IV: The Voice, Studio Two

*Nothing here is testimony. It is a another **reconstruction, and I should say what it is built from. I have not found her name, and I have decided not to look further. She set down nothing about herself. What remains is this: a script, in forty-one revisions, typed on a machine with a dropped lower-case e; the log of a sound engineer who counted everything; a tin of throat lozenges, empty, with her teeth marks in the lid; a series of recordings of the same sentence, 212 of them, on a hard disk that had been dropped in a bin; and an eight-page memo to the broadcast office, on the subject of tone. I am told she is alive. It was said to me once, in passing, and the person who said it asked me not to ask, and I have not. Apart from that one sentence, nobody has spoken to me about her. I have only the paper, and I say so. I have written one day of her life in her voice, in the first person, in the present tense, because that is how she spoke, and because a script is not a woman. Where I leave her voice and speak in mine, I do it in bracketed italics and name where the words come from: script, log, memo, tin, recording, or mine, for what is my own.*

I wake at 04:50 in a room on the nineteenth floor of the Palace. It was an office. It has a sofa that has been made into a bed, a desk, a window of the kind that does not open, and a view to the south and east of the roofs of Śródmieście, which at this hour are grey and then, as the minutes go on, a kind of pewter. I have the room to myself. There is a lock on the door, on the outside.

*\[Mine. The hour she woke, the room, the sofa, the window and the lock on the outside. The engineer's log begins when she reaches the studio. Everything before that, and everything after the last reading, is mine, and I have made it quiet because the log is quiet.\]*

I lie for a minute with my eyes closed. I do the first of the exercises.

The first is the jaw. I open the mouth to the width of three fingers and I close it. I do this ten times. I place my fingertips at the hinge of the jaw, below the ears, where there is a hollow, and I press, and I move them in small circles for thirty seconds, until the muscle gives. Then the tongue. I put it out to its full length and I draw it back, and I put it out to the left, and the right, and I touch the tip to the nose and the chin, ten times each. I say *la la la la*, softly, on a falling scale. I say *ma me mi mo mu*. I say the one about the beetle in the reeds at Szczebrzeszyn, *w Szczebrzeszynie chrząszcz brzmi w trzcinie*, and I say it six times, each faster than the last, and I do not trip.

I get up. I put on the grey cardigan, the grey skirt, the flat shoes. I have six pairs of flat shoes, in a row, on the floor by the wall. They were brought to me. They are all in my size.

At 05:15 a man comes with the tray. He has a key. He is perhaps forty, in the uniform of the support staff, a dark blue overall, and he does not look at my face. On the tray are: a pot of tea; a glass of warm water with a slice of lemon in it; a spoon of honey, in a saucer; two slices of dry toast; a bowl of porridge. He puts the tray down. He says, *Good morning.* I say, *Good morning.* The lock turns.

I take the water first. The lemon is for the mucus, the honey is for the cords. I let the honey dissolve on the tongue and I swallow in two parts. I do not take milk or coffee, which dry the throat, and I do not take anything cold. I have the porridge. I eat a third of it. I will not eat more before the broadcast, because a full stomach limits the breath.

At 06:00 I do the breath. I lie on the floor, on my back, with my knees up and my feet flat, and I place a book on my stomach. It is a paperback dictionary. It has been there for eleven weeks, and the corner of it is dented. I breathe in through the nose for four. I hold for four. I breathe out through the mouth, on a hiss, for eight, and I watch the dictionary go up, and down. I do twelve. The point of it is to find the place in the body that is under the ribs and above the hips, where the voice is made. It is not in the throat. A voice made in the throat is a voice that is working.

*\[Mine. The cardigan, the shoes, the tray, the exercises and the dictionary on her stomach. I took them from what singers and announcers are taught, and from the tin, and I have no evidence that she did any of it.\]*

At 06:45 I am walked down. There is a corridor, with a carpet in a red and gold that has been there since 1955, and the walls are a cream of the kind that is called *magnolia*, and at the end of it is a steel door with a window of wired glass. I am walked by a young soldier. He is seventeen or eighteen. He walks half a pace behind me and to the left, and he carries a rifle, which he has been told, I think, to hold in a manner that is not alarming. He holds it against his chest, like a child.

*\[Log, and mine. Here the engineer's log begins, at 06:45, with the corridor and the door. He counted her steps. The soldier, the carpet and the rifle held against his chest are mine.\]*

Studio Two is on the eighth floor. It is a room of four metres by five, with walls of a grey foam in the pattern called egg-crate, and a floor of carpet, and a window onto the control room, in which there is a man at a desk. The man is the engineer. He is called Pavel. I know this because he has said it, once, on the intercom, in the first week, and not again.

In the middle of the room there is a stool, and in front of it a stand, and on the stand a microphone. It is a large-diaphragm condenser, in a cradle of elastic bands, with a pop filter in front of it, a circle of black nylon stretched on a hoop. I have measured the distance from the filter to my mouth. It is a hand's width, with the fingers together, about eleven centimetres. I sit with my back straight. I place my feet on the rung. On the stand there is a sheet of paper.

*\[Log. The room, the microphone and its distance from her mouth are in the engineer's writing, in the first week. He measured, as he measured everything. I have given the measuring to her. His name is the log's first line.\]*

At 06:55 I put on the headphones and say my name.

I will not say it here. It is the sound check. *Level, one two. Level, one two.* Pavel says, *Thank you.* The light goes red. I look at the paper.

There are three paragraphs.

*Good morning. The shelters on the left bank are open.*

*There are beds. There is hot water. There is no need to be afraid.*

*If you are able to walk, please come to the bridge. If you are not, please stay where you are. Someone will come.*

That is the whole of the script. It is read four times a day, at 08:00, at 12:00, at 18:00, and at 20:00, and on the half hour at night, in a shorter form, and it goes out over a network of two thousand speakers that were fitted to the lamp posts and the rooftops of the district in the first month, in a spidery grey cable, and I am told that it can be heard in every street.

*\[Script. Forty-one revisions, and the three paragraphs above are the last. The schedule is typed at the head of the final one. The earlier revisions have more words and fewer beds. None of them is signed.\]*

I have read it, at the last count, five hundred and forty times.

I would like to speak of the memo, now, because I wrote it, and because I am proud of it, and because it is the only thing in the whole of the eleven weeks that I have been permitted to write.

The memo is eight pages. It was submitted in the third week, in response to a note from the broadcast office that asked for *feedback on tone*. It is typed, double-spaced, and has a heading: *Notes on Delivery*. It begins: *The present reading is correct. It is also ineffective.* It goes on to say that the version of the script that was originally recorded, by an announcer, was read in what may be called an official voice: flat, neutral, with falling intonation at the end of each sentence. It explains that in a population that has been frightened for a long time, an official voice does not reassure. It says that such a population has learned that the official voice is the voice that precedes bad news. It says that a different voice is needed, and describes it. It is a warm voice, in the lower register, at around 180 hertz in the fundamental. It is slightly slower than the natural rate of speech, by about eleven per cent. It has a roughness at the edge of the vowels, of the kind that results from a person having been awake for a long time and not minding. It breathes audibly between the sentences, at a point that is calculated: after *open*, and after *water*. It says the phrase *no need to be afraid* in a particular way. It says it a little too kindly.

I put this in the memo. I said that the phrase should be said *a little too kindly*, and I explained why. I said that a person who is afraid, and who is told by a person who is not afraid that there is no need to be, will not believe it. But a person who is afraid, and who is told by a person who seems to know that he is afraid, and who seems to be sorry, will weep. I said that the way to make a person trust a voice is to let it sound as though it might be about to break.

*\[Memo. The quoted sentences, the figures and the places for the breath are the memo's, as I have them. So is the phrase said a little too kindly. The memo is unsigned. It was typed on the same machine as the forty-one revisions of the script, with the same dropped e, and I do not know whose machine that was. That she wrote it, and that she was proud of it, I have assumed.\]*

It was approved in four days. I have the reply. It is on the letterhead of the broadcast office, with a stamp, and it is two lines: *Approved. Please proceed with the proposed delivery.*

I am aware of what I did. I want that to be plain. I am not a person who has been misled. I wrote the memo. I read it again each morning, in the pocket of my cardigan, where it has been, folded in four, since the third week, and the folds have worn through.

*\[Mine. The pocket, the folds and the morning reading. The copy I have is flat and unworn, and was kept in a file by the broadcast office, with the reply clipped to the back. That she knew what she was doing is mine. I could find nothing that says so, and nothing that says otherwise.\]*

At 07:58 the light on the intercom goes amber. Pavel says, *Two minutes.* I swallow. I take a lozenge from the tin. It is a honey and sage lozenge, in a tin of the kind with a hinged lid, with a picture of a beehive in red and yellow. I hold it in my cheek. I breathe in for four, hold for four, out for eight.

*\[Tin. Honey and sage, a hinged lid, a beehive in red and yellow, as I have given it. The teeth marks are small and close together, on the inner edge of the lid. I have supposed that she bit it while she waited.\]*

At 08:00 the light goes red.

I breathe in. It is the breath that the listeners will hear. It is a small sound, a slight catch of air across the teeth. I have practised it. I have practised, on the floor, with the dictionary, to make it neither too loud nor too soft.

*Good morning.* I say it in the lower register. I let it fall a quarter-tone, and then I let it rise by a fraction at the end, as if it were a question, and as if I were a little unsure, and a little hopeful. *The shelters on the left bank are open.*

I breathe. After *open*.

*There are beds.* I take a pause of one second. *There is hot water.* I breathe. After *water*. *There is no need to be afraid.* And I say it as I have been saying it for eleven weeks, very softly, with a slight roughness in the vowel of *afraid*, and with just a little too much kindness, so that it sounds like the voice of someone who would rather not be saying it. It is a thing that can be done. I do not know how I know how to do it.

The light goes amber, and then off. I take off the headphones. Pavel says over the intercom, *Thank you. That was good.* He always says *Thank you.* And then, after a moment, in a voice that is not for the log, and with his finger, I think, off the key, *That was very good.*

I say nothing.

At 08:05 I am walked back up.

My day is not occupied between the readings. I have a window. I have a sofa. I have a shelf with eleven books, which are in four languages, which were selected, I suppose, by someone who thought they were harmless. I have read nine. I have the tenth open. I have a radio, which gets nothing. I have a pencil, and a pad. I have used the pad to keep a count. It is a tally, in fives, in a column, on each day's page, and it is the count of the number of times that I have said the sentence. It is not required.

At 12:00 I read it again. And at 18:00. And at 20:00.

And at 20:00 I do the thing that I do not tell the broadcast office.

It is not in the memo. It is a small thing. In the pause after *There is hot water*, which is a second, I do not breathe in. I let the second go by with the microphone open and nothing in it, and I listen. It is the only time in the day that I am not speaking. I listen for what is on the other side of the sound. The room is dead, and the microphone is very sensitive, and in the nothing, if I hold my breath, I can hear a thing. It is a hum. It is a low hum with a slow beat, once in about six seconds, which comes through the wall from somewhere under the building.

Pavel says that it is the ventilation. I have not asked him again.

*\[Recording and log. The pause and the hum are on the disk, and the log gives them one word, ventilation. They are on the take of the twelfth day at 08:00, and I have put them in the evening. I do not know why I did that, except that the evening is when I would have listened. That she left the pause open on purpose is mine. I cannot tell from the disk whether it was a choice.\]*

I do not know how many people in the district hear the pause. I have the sense that one or two do. I have the sense that, in a kitchen, in a flat, in a block on the east bank, there is a man or a woman, at a table, with their hands flat, who has noticed that the voice stops, and has felt, for the length of a second, a faint cold on the back of the neck.

*\[Mine. The kitchen, the table and the hands laid flat on it. I know whose kitchen I took them from.\]*

At 21:30 I do the last of the exercises. I do the jaw, and the tongue. I say *la la la la* on a rising scale, to bring the voice up. I drink the last of the warm water. I clean my teeth. I fold the cardigan over the chair. I lie down on the sofa that has been made into a bed, and I look at the ceiling, which is white, and I think of nothing in particular.

At 22:10 the light in the corridor goes out. The lock turns, from the outside, with a small, well-oiled sound.

*\[Mine. The sofa, the ceiling, the light in the corridor and the lock. The log ends with the last reading of the day. What a person thinks of when she thinks of nothing in particular is not in it.\]*

§&#32;

I made the day above. What follows is the disk, and after it what I have supposed. Here the voice is mine, and not hers.

*The disk of recordings had 212 files. They are named by date and time, and they are the same three paragraphs. I listened to all of them in a single night, in a kitchen in Lublin, with the headphones on and a glass of tea. I will say what I noticed, because I am a journalist and it is what I notice. For the first eleven days, there is a voice that reads the words like a reader of the news. On the twelfth, it changes. It does not change gradually. It changes in a single take, at 08:00, and it does not change back. In the same recording, at the pause after There is hot water, there is a silence of a second, and in it, very faintly, almost under the floor of the noise, there is a sound that the engineer's log does not name. I have played it forty times. It is a hum. And at the very end of the file, after the last word, after the click of the key, there is a short sound, a single breath, taken in and not let go.*

The disk was in a bin, and I do not know what was not on it. I have not found her name, and I have said that I will not look. I will say why. Whoever could tell me her name could tell me where she is, and I am a journalist, and I know what I would do next. The voice on the disk is hers. The woman in the room is mine, and I will not let her stand in for a witness.

## Chapter 10: The Listener

**ALEX** · Lublin, March 2036

Magda Wiśniewska interviewed me first.

The commission sat in a requisitioned courthouse in Lublin, in a corridor of closed doors, and she met me at the end of it. I had expected, from what I had been told, a woman of some age and some hardness. She was eighteen.
