She was small, with a straight dark fringe, a school-grey coat that was a little too good for the building, and the manner of a person who has been in rooms with adults since before she could see over the table. She shook my hand once. She did not ask my name. She asked how many stairs there were between the street door and the landing.

I said I did not know. She said, "Thirty-eight. You came up them at an even pace and you stopped on the second landing for four seconds. Was it your knee, or were you listening?"

I said that it was my knee.

She wrote something on a pad. "Good," she said. "I need to know how you tell the truth. Then I can tell what it is I'm hearing."

She asked me nine questions in a room with a very tall window. I answered, I think, eight honestly. She looked up when I answered the ninth and said, "Thank you. That's the one I wanted you to lie on." I have not been able to tell whether she meant it kindly.

They call her the Listener. She doesn't like it. She says it sounds like a job for a priest. She told me, on the second day, that she had been called a good many things and the only one that could have ever hurt was *the informer*, and that it had not hurt because it was untrue.

Diana spent a night with her in the third week of September. It is the only night in the diary on which she did not write until it was over, and I believe that is why the entry has the order that it has.

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 22 September 2034

**SY/MW/0020\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date:** 22 September 2034, 05:10*

***Location: **OP-Rynek, Grochów, the room above the pharmacy*

***Conditions:** dawn, damp, windless*

***Temperature:** 9°C*

***Pressure:** 1013 hPa, steady*

***Flood: **stable. Staff gauge 84.2 m.*

*I have not slept. I will write down why.*

*I was told not to come. Goya, in particular, was clear. "The girl is an** informer," he said. "Everyone knows what she did on the other side. She'll sell you for a packet of tea." The woman who runs the Rynek kitchen said, more kindly, that she was very clever and I should not let her know what I was thinking. A man at the Brigade said that she was a useful instrument and that I should be careful what I played on her. I have written these down in the order they were said. I notice that every one of them used her or feared her. I have not, so far, met anyone who was neither.*

*She is sixteen. I did not know that until I saw her. I had expected someone older, because of what I was told. She is thin and watchful, in a boy's anorak, and she asked me at the door whether I wanted tea before she decided whether I was welcome. I said I did. She said, "Good answer," and let me in.*

*The room above the pharmacy at Rynek is a long narrow loft with a sloping ceiling and, along one wall, a table with five radios on it, two of them military, one shortwave, one a field set of a kind I did not know, and one an ordinary kitchen radio with a cracked case. Headphones on a hook. A small lamp with a green shade. A notebook ruled into six columns: time, frequency, language, speaker, content, and a sixth that she has not given a heading.*

*She sat me on a stool behind her and told me not to speak. For eight hours she listened.*

*I do not know how to describe that. I had assumed it would be a kind of scanning. It is not. She sits with her eyes half closed and her hand on the dial and she waits, and the sound comes in and goes out, and for long stretches there is only a hiss. She does not become impatient. And then, without any change in her face, she picks up the pencil and writes a line.*

*At 23:00 she let me hear the posts check in. Five voices in Russian, one after another, at the top of the hour: R0, RM, RW, RN, R4. They are flat. They are the voices of men reading the time. And she said, very quietly, the first thing she had said all night: "The third one is lying."*

*I asked how she knew. She said, "He says all quiet in exactly the way that you say it when it isn't." She said that everyone has a way of being truthful and a way of being false, and that the difference is almost never in the words. It is in the breath. A man who is telling the truth takes the breath after he starts speaking. A man who is not takes it before.*

*I asked where she had learned that. She said, "From being very good at the other thing."*

*At 03:15 the kitchen radio, which she had not touched, gave out a woman's voice in Polish. It was the recording from the northern post, the one about the beds. Magda listened to it twice and said, without turning, "She isn't a recording." I said it must be. She said, "It's a recording of someone who is meant to sound like a recording. She's been asked to say it kindly, and she's chosen to say it a little too kindly. I think she's trying to tell us something."*

*I asked what. "I don't know yet. I'm going to listen to her for a month."*

*I have thought about this for two hours. I record it because it troubles me, and my **work is indeed **to be troubled in order.*

*The trouble is this. I believe her. And I have noticed that she says "I will tell you the truth" before almost every sentence that matters. She said it six times. I counted. It is the only habit I have found in her that is not required by the work.*

*I noticed something else, and I'd like to write it carefully. On the evening of 14 September I came to Rynek to ask her about the Kontakt rota. She was not there. A boy said she had gone to the Praga flats to check a relay, and she was not back by the 15th, and she was not back by the 16th. On the 17th she was at the table with her headphones on. I asked where she had been. She said: "The Praga flats. The relay was bad." And I said nothing, because the Praga flats are four kilometres away, and the relay, I happen to know, is a ten-minute job.*

*I don't** think she lied. I will write that down. But she did not take the breath before.*

*At the end of the night, at about five, I did a thing I have been turning over since. I did not ask her anything. I said, "Thank you for letting me sit." She did not answer for a while. Then she said, "Nobody has ever just sat." I said that I did not want anything. She turned round on the stool, for the first time, and looked at me, and I saw that she was not performing. And then I saw that I could not tell.*

*\[in pencil, margin: she told me the truth. she told me the truth. what was the question.\]*

\* \* \*

**MAGDA** · recording, Lublin, March 2036

**SY/MW/0001\_GR** · *Magda Wiśniewska, recorded in Lublin, March 2036*

You'll have decided by now that I'm going to tell you something that proves me either good or bad. I've been through this with several people. I'll tell you what I tell them.

My family had money. I want to say that at the start, because it's the part that people would like to forget and I don't intend to let them. We lived in Mokotów, in a house with a garden wall and a gate with a code. My father imported things. I never knew what. My mother was a lawyer who stopped working when I was born and was very good at being unhappy in a way that didn't show. I had a nanny, Galina, who was Russian, from Pskov, and who spoke to me in her own language until I was six. I thought of it as a game. It was my first trade.

When the Russians came into Mokotów, I was eleven. They took the house at the end of our street first. Then the one beside it. Then ours, for the ground floor, which they used as an office, and we were allowed to live on the first floor and the attic, and my parents learned very quickly to be invisible. They were very good at it. I wasn't.

I'm going to tell you the thing I did, and I'd like you to be honest about it.

I spoke to them. I spoke Russian. I'd come down the stairs and say good morning, in the voice of a small well-brought-up girl, and a lieutenant who was perhaps twenty gave me a bar of chocolate. I was eleven. I hadn't had chocolate in four months. I said thank you. I said it so well that he laughed. And I saw what it did. I watched it work, on his face, like a tap being turned. It was the first time I understood that a person can be made kind by being charmed, and that kindness can be made to carry a message.

I charmed all of them. I don't mean that I was anything but a child. I mean that I sat on the stairs and I learned their names, and their mothers' names, and which of them was homesick, and which of them was afraid of the major, and I gave them what they wanted, which was a small girl who thought they were nice. In return I was given chocolate, and a pass for the street, and, later, a way of being safe.

And they asked me questions. They asked in the nicest possible way. They asked who lived in the house with the green door. They asked whether the old doctor at number eleven had visitors. And I answered. I gave them names. Eleven of them. I know the number because I've kept the list.

I'd like to explain it and I can't. I wasn't threatened. Nobody put a gun to anything. I was eleven, and a man in uniform was pleased with me, and I wanted him to go on being pleased. It was as simple and as bad as that. It was a kind of hunger. I've thought about it for eight years and I don't think there's more to it, and I think that's the worst thing about it. People want it to be fear. It was appetite.

Four of the eleven were taken to Szucha. Two were returned. I'll say their names to the commission and I'll say them to you, though I'd rather you didn't print them: Dr Wawrzyniak, Pani Gorska, and the two Nowak brothers. And Jan, the boy at the green door, who was fifteen. He wasn't taken to Szucha. I don't know what became of him. That's on the list.

I wasn't sorry all at once. I'd like you to put that in. For two years I thought I had done well. I thought I was clever. It was the day I understood what Pani Gorska looked like when she came back that I began to be sorry, and I went on being sorry, and I'm still at it. I crossed the river at fourteen, with a rucksack and a false name. I thought no one would know me. Someone knew me inside a week.

That's what I am. I'm not going to ask you to forgive it. I want you to understand that I do not tell lies now. It's a position, if you like. It's an experiment. I decided at fifteen that I would see what happened if I stopped, and I've been watching it ever since.

You'll have noticed that I said, I do not tell lies now, and that I've said it before and that I say it often. You're right to notice. Someone who has lied in every sentence for three years doesn't wake up truthful. She wakes up saying that she is. I know the difference between the two. I know it because I can do both, and I can't always tell which I am doing.

You'll be asking whether I'm doing it now.

I've noticed you write something down when I cry. I'd like it on the record that I know when I'm crying on purpose. And that tonight I wasn't sure.

Diana said one thing to me. She said it in October, when she had asked once about the relay and had not asked again. She said, "I am not going to ask you where you were. I am going to ask you who you are listening for." I said, "Everyone." She said, "That is not a person."

I've thought about that for eighteen months. It's the only thing anyone has said to me in my life that I couldn't do anything with.

*(end of recording)*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · Lublin, March 2036

I stayed in Lublin for four days. On the fourth she walked me to the station, and at the barrier she said, with her eyes on the departures board, "You want to know whether I was lying."

I said that I did.

"I've told you the truth," she said. "That's all I can say that's true." She had taken, I noticed, the breath before. And then, as the train came in, she took another one, afterwards, and smiled, and I could not tell which of the two I had been given.

On the second day she had shown me the list. It was a school exercise book, and the names were written in a careful child's hand, in pencil, going back eight years, with a line through four of them and a question mark after one. The question mark was against Jan. She had not read it to the commission. She said she was going to. She said she would do it in April.

I asked about the fourteenth to the sixteenth of September. She said, "Diana wrote that down?" I said she had. She was silent for a moment. "Of course she did," she said. "I told her the truth. It's the best technique I know. If you want to say nothing and you can't lie, you tell the truth about everything but the one thing, and you stand very close to it, and the person is so busy checking the other things that they forget to look."

She said she had known Lebedev's voice. She said she had known it from the first time she heard it on the net, reading the rota twice. It was the voice of a young man who had once, in a stairwell, given her a bar of chocolate. She said she could not be certain. She said that his accent was the same, and that he read slowly, as one reads to a child. She said that on 2 October, when the gap came, she had understood that it was a message, and that she did not know whom it was for.

"It might have been for me," she said. "I should like to think so. That's a very dangerous thing to like."

Diana's note on the transcript is in ink, which is unusual, and is the longest she wrote about anyone. *She tells the truth the way other people lie: because it works, and because she is very good at it. I trust her entirely. I do not know what she is for.* Underneath it, in pencil, much later, in a hand that is not quite steady: *Whoever it was for, she did not give them the Zoo.*

I don't know what that means. I have asked three people. I have been given three answers, each of them true.

## Chapter 11: The Orphan

**ALEX** · Dęblin, February 2036

Captain Zoysa Zieliński received me at ten to the hour, precisely, in an office at the air academy at Dęblin with a map on one wall and nothing on the others.

She was nineteen. I have been told that she is the youngest officer of her rank in the Polish army, and I believe it, because she wore it as a person wears a coat that has been issued rather than chosen: correctly, without comment, as a thing that would be handed back. She was tall and narrow-shouldered with cropped brown hair, and she stood to shake my hand and sat only after I had, and then she put a notepad squarely in front of her, with a pen laid across it at right angles.

"You may record," she said. "I will answer what is asked. I would ask that the questions are put in order."

I put them in order. It was the most precise interview of my life. She did not pause. She did not repeat herself. She had the habit, which I have only seen in staff officers and the best translators, of answering the question as asked and stopping, so that a silence followed each answer that I found myself, absurdly, wanting to fill. She told me about her family in the voice of a person reading from a document she had written herself. She told me about Goya. She told me, without any apparent emotion, how many people she had killed. And when I reached the last question on my list, the one about her father, she looked at the recorder, and then at me, and said, "That will be last. And I'd prefer it wasn't on the tape."

I said that was her right.

Diana had been aware of her for months before she was able to put a name to what she saw. I have set the entry from the end of November here, because it is the first in which the unease appears, and because it is written in the careful, lightly contracted voice that Diana used when she was frightened and did not want to be.

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 30 November 2034

**SY/ZZ/0036\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date: **30 November 2034, 21:30*

***Location:** Rosa and Marek's flat, Białołęka, third floor*

***Conditions:** snow, still, very cold. The windows have ice on the inside.*

***Temperature:** −7°C*

***Pressure: **1026 hPa, steady*

***Flood:** partly frozen. Canal ice at Żerań 4 cm.*

*I'm going to write this slowly, because I don't want to write what I think.*

*I went to the flat to hear Marek. He tells the stories on Thursdays, at the table in the kitchen, to whoever comes, and Rosa makes tea, and the children sit under the table, because it is the warm place. There were eleven of us. It is the best hour in the week. I have decided that I will not record it. I have found that some things are for being in the room.*

*Tonight he told the one about the captain.*

*It is 1944. A unit of the Home Army in the sewers under Śródmieście, in August. A captain, a hard man, who had held the line through three weeks of the rising on nothing but the fact that his men believed he would never leave them. He did not leave them. And in the second week of September, when it was all but over, the men found a note in his coat that offered him terms. He had not accepted. He had kept it in his pocket for four days. Marek said: "He didn't go. But he didn't burn it either. That's what they couldn't forgive. He was a good man who had kept the door open." He stopped. He said, "I never decided what to think."*

*Everyone was quiet. Rosa put her hand on his sleeve.*

*Zoysa was sitting in the corner by the stove, as she always is, with her back against the wall and her boots under her. Seventeen. She has the posture of a girl who was taught to stand to attention before she could read. She did not move during the story. At the end, when everyone began to murmur, she stood up, and said, very formally, "Thank you, Pan Marek," and went out. She did not take her coat. I heard her go down all three flights without, I think, touching the rail.*

*I have been collecting things for a fortnight and I'd like to set them down in order. I do not wish to call them evidence.*

*One. A fortnight ago, at Rynek, Magda was at her sets and said, not to me, "There's a Polish voice on the Szucha net." I said that was not unusual. She said, "It's unusual that he's called colonel." She wrote a line, and she didn't say the name, and she turned the page.*

*Two. Zoysa has been absent for two nights. Not unaccounted for: Goya knows where she is, or says he does. She has been on the north shore, he says, on a patrol. But the patrol roster has no entry.*

*Three. On Tuesday she asked Magda, very politely, whether it was possible to listen to a particular frequency on a particular night. Magda said that it was. Zoysa wrote down a time. I saw the time. It was the hour of the Szucha check-in.*

*Four. She keeps a photograph in the inside pocket of her jacket. A man in a dress uniform, grey at the temples. She has shown it to nobody. I saw it by accident, when she took off the jacket at the stove. She looked at it, and she put it back, and her face did the thing that I do with a notebook.*

*I have a name and a surname. I will not write them. They're the girl's. I will write only that I know a particular thing about a particular officer's voice, and that I have not told anyone, and that I think she may know it too. I think she's known for some time. I think that this is why she did not take her coat.*

*I don't know what to do. I don't think it's mine to do anything. I have found that there are times when the work is simply to be in the room, and to be the person who does not ask.*

*I will sit next to her at the next Thursday. I'll bring tea.*

*\[in pencil, margin: the captain kept the door open. who is the door for.\]*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · Dęblin, February 2036

Before the first question she stood up and said, "You will walk with me. It is part of the answer."

The academy at Dęblin is a long brick complex put up in the 1920s for a different air force, and it has the manner of a place that has been repaired a great many times by people who disapproved of the damage. We went down a staircase of worn terrazzo and out through a side door, and the cold hit us like a flat hand. It was February. There was frozen mud on the parade ground, ridged where the boots of the morning drill had come and gone, and along its far edge ran a line of grey hangars with their doors shut. She walked at a pace of about one hundred and twenty steps to the minute. I know, because I fell into it, and after some time I began to count, and the count came out the same at every turn of the path.

"Four hundred and six students," she said. "The youngest is fourteen. The oldest is thirty-one, a woman who was a hospital pilot and is retraining. We have eleven aircraft that fly, and nineteen that don't. We keep the nineteen because they teach more." She did not look at me as she said it. She looked ahead, at the hangars, like a woman reading from a printed board.

I asked how a person came to hold the rank at nineteen.

"By being useful at the right moment to the wrong number of people," she said. "It isn't a distinction. It's a logistics problem. There weren't enough officers, and there was a girl who could read a map." And then, after a pause that she had plainly measured: "Please don't write that I said it was modest."

We passed a building with a flagpole and no flag. We passed a row of cadets, six of them, boys and girls, standing in line outside a door with their caps in their left hands and their eyes fixed on a point about a metre above the lintel. They were waiting to be inspected. None of them moved. A girl of perhaps fifteen, at the end, had a smear of ink on her thumb, and I saw Zoysa see it, and register it, and say nothing, and go on. I thought that she had not forgotten. I thought she had put it on a list.

The canteen was in a low annexe, a hall with long tables of scrubbed pine and a serving counter at the end, and it was not yet noon, so it was empty except for two women in white caps who were stacking trays. She took one and handed me another. We queued like everyone else. I watched what she put on it, which was a bowl of barley soup, a slice of rye bread, a cup of tea with no sugar, and, from a steel tray at the end of the counter, a single boiled egg, which she lifted with a spoon and set in a china cup. I took the same. I didn't know what else to do.

She sat with her back to the wall, facing the door. I saw that every table in the hall had been laid, by custom or by design, so that the chair with its back to the wall was the one nearest the door, and that everyone, in this building, knew who sat there. She turned the egg in its cup so that the point faced her, and struck the shell once, sideways, with the edge of the spoon, and lifted the cap clear.

"My mother did it like this," she said. It was the first thing she had said that was not an answer.

I did not reply.

"Don't look so careful," she said. "I'm allowed to say it." She ate half of the egg. "I have a rule. I may say a thing about my mother if I say it while eating. Then the mouth is occupied and can't say more. It's worked for fifteen months."

The soup was good. It had a ham bone in it, and a bay leaf, and a taste of something I could not name, a little sour and a little smoky, and I said so, and she said that it was the cook's mother's recipe and that the cook had been a cadet at fourteen and was now thirty-eight, and had never left. "People say we are a place for the young. We are a place for those who stayed."

After the soup she took the pad from her breast pocket, the small one, bound at the top, and laid it on the table and squared it with the edge. She uncapped the pen. On the first page, in a small clean hand, were a column of figures, a column of dates, and at the very top, underlined, a single word. I read it upside down. It was *Order.*

"You will want to know what this is," she said.

I said that I would.

"It is a list of things that are in their place." She turned the pad round so that I could see. There were, I would say, forty items. *Boots, lined. Radio, charged. Bread, stored. Gate, shut. Father, alive.* The last was a line like the others. It had a tick against it, a small black tick, drawn with care, and beside it a date. It was last week's. She turned the pad back before I could read the rest.

"Each morning I go through the list," she said. "If all the items are in place, I tick them. If one is not, I do not tick it, and I deal with it before breakfast. It is a way of knowing what to be afraid of." She put the cap on the pen. "I have found that most of the time, if I attend to the list, there is nothing to be afraid of. I attend to it for that reason."

I said it seemed a sound method.

"It is a method that has one flaw," she said. "It works only if the list is true."

I don't ask what she meant. She doesn't say. We finished the tea. A bell rang somewhere in the building, three times, a flat tin sound, and I heard a great sudden noise of chairs and feet from the floors above us, as a hundred cadets rose at once. She looked at her watch. It was a plain steel one, with a leather strap that had been replaced, and she looked at it for exactly as long as it took to read it.

"Now we go back," she said. "You may record. I will answer what is asked. I would ask that the questions are put in order."

It was only then that I understood that she had said these words to me an hour earlier, in the office, in the same order, in the same voice, and had walked me round the whole of her world in order to be able to say them again.

\* \* \*

**ZOYSA** · recording, Dęblin, February 2036

**SY/ZZ/0001\_GR** · *Zoysa Zieliński, recorded in Dęblin, February 2036*

I will begin with my family, because the sequence matters.

My father was a career officer. Colonel Andrzej Zieliński. My mother was a teacher of history. We lived in Rembertów, near the garrison, in a house with a flag on the gate. I want to be exact about this. It wasn't that my father was cold. He was formal. He believed that a family was a unit, that a unit had a standard, and that the standard was a sufficient form of love. He read to me every night. He read from a book of the lives of Polish resistance fighters: the January Uprising, the Home Army, the Cursed Soldiers. He read it with great seriousness, as one reads liturgy. I believed every word. I could recite the dates before I could do long division.

On the first night of the invasion of Poland my mother was killed. A bomb on the school where she was working late. I was eleven. I don't wish to describe it further. I will say only that I was told by a neighbour and not by my father, who was already on his way to the front. He left a note. It said that I was to be brave, and that he would come back. I kept it.

He didn't come back. In the retreat his unit was cut off at the Bug, and there was a report, in the third week, that the colonel had been killed holding a bridge. I was thirteen. I was given the report by a young officer who stood very straight and whose voice shook, and I thanked him. I thanked him formally. I've always been proud of that.

I was raised, in that period, by the stories. I mean the stories he had read me. Marek's stories. They were the only thing I had that was my father's, and I carried them like a rifle. When I came to the zone I was fifteen and I asked for the Kosciuszko Group, and I was told I was too young, and I said that I knew the lives of every insurgent in the Home Army, in order, with their dates, and Goya said, "Can you shoot?" And I said I didn't know. He took me to the range. I could. I don't wish to say what that tells you about a person.

I will say that I was good. I will say that I was the best that Goya had, and that he said so, once, in a voice that I hear still.

Now.

I will tell you how I learned that he was alive. It was Magda. I don't hold it against her. She came to me on a night in November, at Rynek, and she said, "I have something I don't know how to say." I said that she should say it in order. She said that a man had been named on the Szucha net, as a consultant, with the rank of colonel, and that he spoke Polish with the accent of Rembertów. I said that there were many colonels. She said, "He says *Zielinski*, when he introduces himself. He puts the stress on the second syllable. Like your father's record."

I didn't say anything. I went on standing. I thought, I will wait, until she says that it isn't him. She didn't.

I crossed on the second of December. I won't say how. I will say that Goya didn't know and that Diana did. I will say that I went alone, in the dark, with a pistol that I had no intention of using, and that I told myself throughout that I was going to kill a traitor, and that it was a lie, and that I didn't know what I was going to do.

He was in a flat in Śródmieście. A good flat. It had a stove and two armchairs and a bowl of oranges. He was sitting in one of the armchairs, in a cardigan. He looked up when I came in, and I saw that he wasn't surprised. He said, "Zoysa." He didn't stand. I think that was what I couldn't forgive: that he didn't stand.

I'm going to give you what we said. I have it exactly.

I said, "I was told you died on a bridge."

He said, "I did not die on a bridge. I surrendered at the Bug with four hundred men, because the alternative was four hundred deaths. I was taken to a camp. In the camp I was offered terms." He said, "I accepted. Not at once. I accepted in the fourth month."

I said, "You kept the door open."

He said, "I do not understand."

I didn't explain it. I said, "Why?"

He said, "Because I wanted to live. Because I had read the lives of a great many heroes and I had noticed that most of them were dead." He looked at me for a long time. "I am not the man in the book, Zoysa. I never was. I am the other one. The one who reads the book to the child."

I think that was the nearest thing to the truth he had ever said to me.

I had the pistol in my hand. I'll say that. I hadn't taken it out. It was simply in my hand. He saw it. And he said, with that formality, "If you intend to use it I will ask that you do it quickly. I have no wish to be a trouble." I found that I couldn't. Not from mercy. I'd like it understood that it wasn't mercy. It was that I realised that if I shot him I would be doing what the book did. I would be making him into a hero, a man who died for a cause, and that was the one thing he had spent six years refusing to be.

So I did the other thing.

I said, "You will go on. You will go on working for them. You will tell them what I tell you to tell them. And you will tell me everything they tell you."

He said nothing for a minute. Then he said, "That is a great deal to ask of a man at my age."

I said, "It is what you were trained to do. Intelligence. You will be good at it."

And he laughed. It was a short, astonished laugh. I hadn't heard it since before the war. He said, "You are my daughter." I said that I was a captain of the Kosciuszko Group and that he would address me as such. He said, "Yes, Captain." And I walked out, and down the stairs, and at the bottom I was sick on the pavement, which I didn't report.

For seven months he fed us. The fuel stocks at the Palace in winter. The convoy at Michałów on Tuesdays. The blind spot in the gunboat patrol during the shift change, which lasted eleven minutes and which the Brigade used on forty-one nights. The fact that the garrison at Otwock hadn't been paid for three months, which I believe is why Otwock fell so quickly in the end. It isn't a small thing. I don't claim it was the liberation. I claim it shortened it, and I've been told by people qualified to judge that it shortened it by weeks.

I told Goya after the third month. He was silent for a long time. He said, "You should have told me." I said I knew. He said, "I'd have shot him." I said, "I know that, too." He didn't speak to me for four days, and on the fifth he came to the range and stood beside me, and said, "That was well done," and I didn't trust my voice.

That's what's on the record. I would ask you to stop the tape.

*(end of recording)*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · Dęblin, February 2036

I stopped it. She sat with her hands flat on the notepad, as she had since the beginning. The pen was still across the top. It had not been moved.

"I haven't told you the rest," she said. "I'd like to. Not for the book. I'd like it said once to a person."

She was quiet for a time. When she spoke, she spoke so quietly that I leaned forward to hear it.

"He sits in a flat in Lublin now. He is fifty-six. I visit on Sundays. We have lunch. We do not discuss any of it. I bring the bread. He has a bowl of oranges." She looked at the wall. "I'd like to tell you that I forgave him. I didn't. I 'd like to tell you that I hate him. I don't. I have not found a word. I have found that I go on honouring a man who doesn't exist and I go on visiting the one who does. They are in the same chair."

I asked whether the stories had been true.

"Marek says they are," she said. "He says it doesn't matter whether the man in the story was a hero. It matters that the story was told. I've thought a great deal about that. It's the only thing I've ever been told that wasn't an order."

She stood. She tidied the notepad. She aligned the pen.

"I should like it known," she said, "that on the night I crossed, I did not take my coat. I realised, about halfway across, and I was very cold. I thought: I will go on, I will not turn back for a coat, it would be a kind of cowardice. And when I got to the flat, he stood. Not when I came in. When I turned to go. He stood up from the armchair and took off his cardigan and he put it round my shoulders. And I let him."

She had not mentioned that on the tape. She had not mentioned it, I understood, to anyone.

Diana's note against the transcript is short. It was written, I believe, on the night she knew. It is in pencil. *She did not take her coat. It was the only thing she forgot in two years. I do not think it was an accident.* Under it, in ink, in a different hand, which I have not been able to identify, a single word. *Honoured.*

## Interlude V: Account Lead, Cohort Review

*What follows has been rebuilt rather than recorded, and I should say how. This man set down nothing about himself, and I have not given him a name, a country, a company or a language. The documents do not need them, and I have come to think that the reader supplies the right ones unprompted. What I have is the following: a calendar, exported from a laptop, covering nineteen weeks; a slide deck of sixty-three slides, of which I have read forty; an expense report; the delivery log of an office kitchen; a lanyard, with a card, and a photograph on the card of a man who is smiling in the manner of a person who has been told to smile; and a handwritten list, on the back of a boarding pass, of nine words. I also have the voices of two people who worked beside him, who asked not to be named, and who spoke to me after the war. I do not take either at their word, and I say so where I do not. I have written one day of his life in his voice, in the present tense, because a calendar is not a man. I have set it on** a Thursday, because the calendar does. My own interruptions are in brackets and in italics, each beginning with its source: calendar, deck, expenses, kitchen, card, pass, interview, or mine, for what I have supplied.*

I wake at 05:40, four minutes before the alarm, and I lie on my back in the dark and do nothing for sixty seconds. The bed is 180 centimetres wide. The sheets are a long-staple cotton, 400 thread count, white. The mattress is medium-firm, with a pillow-top, and I replaced it in March. The room is at 18.5 degrees. The air has been filtered through a system that I selected and that reports, on a small white panel by the door, a particulate level of 3 micrograms per cubic metre, and a green light.

I do the skin first. I wash with a gel cleanser, pH 5.5, fragrance free, and rinse with tepid water. I pat the face dry with a white towel, not rub. I apply a toner with a cotton pad, in upward strokes. I apply a serum of 10 per cent vitamin C, three drops, pressed in with the fingertips. I apply a moisturiser with a ceramide complex, SPF 30, a pearl-sized amount. I do the eye area with the ring finger, because it exerts the least pressure. It is a routine of six steps and takes seven minutes. It 's not vanity. I am aware that people think that it is vanity. A man who is going to be looked at by a room should be a man who has nothing to apologise for in the face.

I weigh myself on the glass scale. 78.4 kilograms. Body fat 12.1 per cent. Resting heart rate 51. I record the figures in the application on the phone, which draws a line.

*\[Calendar. The calendar begins at 06:10, with a block called Gym, and it repeats every weekday for nineteen weeks. The hours before it are mine, and so are the sheets, the skin and the scale. I have given him a morning because the calendar will not.\]*

At 06:10 I go to the gym on the third floor of the building. It is empty. It has a rubberised floor of the colour of slate and a wall of mirror and eight machines in two rows. I do forty minutes. A rowing machine for ten, at 2:05 per five hundred metres. A circuit of squats, rows, presses and lunges, three sets of twelve. Twelve minutes of stretching. I do not listen to music. I listen to the machine, which has a sound, a rising whirr as the flywheel comes up, and which I find, at about the sixth minute, to be restful, a sound without information in it.

At 07:20 I eat. Two eggs, poached, on one slice of seeded bread. A bowl of berries, 120 grams. A coffee from the machine in the kitchen, made with oat milk, because dairy gives me a sluggishness at about eleven. I eat standing, at the counter, which is a single slab of white stone, and I look at the window, which looks at the river, which is wide and a pale brown and has, this morning, a small orange boat.

The apartment is on the twenty-sixth floor. It is 140 square metres, open plan, floor to ceiling glass on two sides, with a view that is described in the sales particulars as *panoramic*. The furniture is a charcoal sofa in a bouclé fabric, two armchairs in a tan leather, a coffee table of smoked glass with a single object on it, which is a glass bowl, empty. There is a painting over the sofa. It is large, abstract, in greys, with a thin red line crossing it from lower left to upper right. I bought it in the spring. I do not know the name of the artist. I was told it was a good investment.

*\[Mine. The eggs, the counter, the sofa, the painting. The expense report gives me a car and a hotel. It gives me no apartment, and no one I asked would tell me where he slept.\]*

At 08:15 I take the car. It is an electric car, black, with a cream interior and a range of 520 kilometres, and the cabin is quiet, so that when the door closes there is the sense of a pressure change in the ears. I put my hand flat on the leather of the seat. It is warm. It has been warmed, on a schedule, since 08:10.

*\[Expenses. The car, the charging and the hour he left are in the report, each with a receipt. The leather, and the schedule on the seat, are mine.\]*

At 08:50 I am at my desk.

The office is on the fourteenth and fifteenth floors of a tower. It is open plan. It has two hundred and eleven desks, in banks of six, each bank facing a glass wall on which a schedule is displayed. The desks are white and are adjustable in height, and I stand at mine, at 112 centimetres. The carpet is a grey-blue tile, in squares of 50 centimetres, and the lights are 4000 kelvin. There is an area by the window with beanbags and a table-football machine, and a small plant in a pot by every fourth desk, which is a type of fern, and is changed monthly. The kitchen has a tap that dispenses water at four temperatures and in three states: still, sparkling, and a state that is described on the tap as *lightly sparkling.* I use the third.

*\[Kitchen. The delivery log gives the oat milk, the berries, the sparkling water in glass, and the ferns, which are changed monthly. It gives the lemon squares on a Thursday. The tap, the beanbags and the table-football are mine.\]*

I should explain what I do.

I am the account lead on a contract that we call, in the internal documents, *the Programme*. In the contract itself it is called by a name which I will not give, and the name is a string of two words, the first of which is a pair of syllables meant to suggest the brain, and the second a verb for the gathering of a crop. It is a standard method of naming. We have found that a name should be a little ahead of a thing, so that a person who sees the name for the first time is not sure what the thing is and, not being sure, does not ask.

*\[Deck. The Programme, the brief on slide four and its three bullets are as they are on the slides. The reasoning about names is on slide two, in a box, and it is not mine. I would not have thought of it.\]*

The brief is on slide four. It has been on slide four since the beginning. It reads: *Population compliance optimisation in a high-friction environment.* Beneath it are three bullets. *Reduce non-cooperative behaviour. Increase voluntary disclosure. Maintain throughput.* We have a model of the environment, and a method, and a client, and the client is, in the language of the Programme, *the operator.* I have never used another word for them. I have met them four times. They are courteous.

*\[Calendar. Four entries in nineteen weeks are titled Operator Alignment. The courtesy is his, or mine. It is also the first word on the pass.\]*

At 09:30 there is a stand-up. We stand, in a ring, by the window, nine of us, with the coffee. The stand-up is of ten minutes. Each of us has a sentence. I have prepared mine. *We're tracking to plan on cohort stage progression. Disclosure is up. One flag on attrition, which I'll cover in the review.*

At 10:00 I open the dashboard.

The dashboard is a screen, 38 inches, curved, on the wall in the small conference room, and it is a map. It is a map of a district in a city that I have never visited and could not point to on a globe without a moment's thought. It is drawn in thin white lines on a dark grey ground, and over it there are squares, green, one for each block, in a grid, and each square has a number. At the top is a bar. The bar says *COHORT STATUS* and next to it is a figure. The figure is a percentage, in a font that I chose, because I was asked to choose a font, and a thin clean round one with a quiet feeling seemed right. It says 71.2.

This is the metric. It is the proportion of the population of the district that has been assessed at a certain level on a scale we have called the Stage Index. The Stage Index runs from 0 to 5. A person at 0 is unaffected, which is to say that they are an unmodified human being. A person at 1 has been exposed. A person at 2 shows a measurable change in affect. A person at 3 will disclose information when asked. A person at 4 will disclose information without being asked, and will, in a range of tests, accept an instruction from a stranger who is polite. At 5, the person does not, in the language of the specification, *require an interface.*

*\[Deck. The map, the bar and the figure are slide twelve, as a screenshot. The Stage Index is slide fourteen, and I have kept its words.\]*

I will say that I don't find it difficult to look at. I have been asked this. It is a map of squares. The squares are green when the percentage in the block at Stage 3 or above is greater than 60, and amber when it is between 30 and 60, and red when it is below 30. There are 212 squares. On this Thursday there are 148 green and 49 amber and 15 red. The red ones are on the north-east edge, and the percentages in them have not moved in nineteen days.

At 10:20 I click on a red square. It opens a panel. The panel has a list of the persons in the block, by identifier, in a column, each with a Stage, and I look at the list. The identifiers are numbers. They have nine digits and no letters. A person has been assigned the number 400018211. They are at Stage 0. There is a note beside it in the field marked *Remarks*. It says: *Resistant. Declined offer. Declined water. Maintains eye contact. Asks questions.*

*\[Mine. The identifier, and the note in the field marked Remarks. The deck shows the panel with the field blanked. The words in it are the words I found in the margins of the forms, and I have used them because I could think of no others.\]*

I look at this for some time. I look at it with the interest of a man who has found an anomaly in a spreadsheet.

I should add that in the whole of the cohort there are 81 persons who have been, at some time, a source of such a note. Of these, three are, by the most recent assay, of a kind that we have not been able to account for. I have written their identifiers on the back of a boarding pass, with a pen, and put it in the inside pocket of my jacket. I don't know why. I have also written, on the same boarding pass, nine words. It is a list. I have been unable to explain it to myself. The words are: *Courteous. Quiet. Warm. Kind. Orderly. Safe. Calm. Fair. Beautiful.* They are the words that we had in the earliest design document, in a section headed *Target Affect.*

*\[Deck and pass. The 81 flagged persons, and the three that could not be accounted for, are on slide thirty-one. The nine words are on the back of the pass, in ballpoint, in the order given. Why a man would write them down I cannot say.\]*

At 11:00 the review.

There are fourteen people in the room. It is a long table of pale ash, with a sheet of white paper at each place, a glass of water, and a pen. The slides are on the screen. I present for forty minutes. I have prepared them. They are clean and well spaced, a single message on each, a chart, a small caption. I use the words I use. I say *throughput*, and *friction*, and *attrition*. I say: *Our attrition in this period is 3.1 per cent against a forecast of 2.4, which I would attribute to the collection cycle in the north-east, and I'd suggest that we look at the interval.*

The word *attrition* is on the slide in a blue box.

A woman at the end of the table asks a question. She asks it in a pleasant, level voice. She asks whether the attrition figure includes those who are *collected.* I say that it does not. She says, *And where are those?* I say that those are tracked under *Transfer.* She says, *And what is the rate of transfer?* I say that it is 11 per cent. She says, *Thank you.* She writes a number on her sheet. I notice that the number she writes is not the number that I have said.

*\[Interview. One of the two who worked beside him was in the room, and remembers the question about transfer, and that the woman at the end of the table wrote something down. Not what. I do not take that on trust: the same person remembered his figure for attrition to the decimal, and nothing else. The figure and the blue box are on slide twenty-two.\]*

I don't know what is done in the places to where the persons are transferred. I have not asked, and it is not in my scope. I have the numbers on either side of the place. They go in, and some of them do not come out, and some come out in a different state. At one end of the column there is a figure and at the other there is a figure, and between them there is a blank, a white space of two centimetres on the slide, with a label in a small grey type: *Process.*

*\[Deck. The blank is slide twenty-six. The label is Process, in a small grey type, and the gap is two centimetres at the size I printed it. I have measured it.\]*

At 13:00 I have lunch at my desk. It is a salad of grains and roasted vegetables in a compostable box, with a dressing in a small pot, and a bottle of sparkling water, in a glass bottle, with a green label. I eat it standing, at 112 centimetres. I read, as I eat, the daily summary from the operator. It is a document of a page and a half, in a translation that is good, and it ends, as they all do, with a sentence that I have come to look for: *We are grateful for the partnership.*

*\[Deck. The sentence at the end of the summary is in the appendix, on every sample page. I did not need to invent it.\]*

At 14:30 there is a call with two colleagues who have come back from the site visit. They are outdoors, I think, beside a vehicle, with the wind in the microphone, and one of them, the man, is eating something. They have a case with them. It is a case of grey plastic, with brass corners. They tell me that it has been a success. They tell me that the operator is delighted, and that the major is a very courteous man, and that the district is much quieter than expected. I ask about the quiet. The woman says, *It's remarkable. You can hear a pin drop. You can hear a hum, actually. Quite low. We thought it was the equipment.*

I say that it is probably the equipment.

*\[Calendar and interview. The call is in the calendar at 14:30, titled Site Debrief, with the two colleagues by their initials. The wind, the vehicle and the case I have from the interview. I was told of a case like it in another place. The hum, and what was said of it, both of the people I spoke to remembered without being asked.\]*

At 17:00 there is a quiet hour in the building, a program of the firm, and a woman comes round with a tray of small cakes. I take one. It is a lemon square on a paper doily. I eat it in three bites. It is excellent.

At 18:30 I leave. I take the car. The river is bright with the last of the light, and a long way off, on the far bank, where the buildings are low, a single plume of white vapour rises from a stack in the evening air, and goes straight up, and does not move.

At 19:40 I have dinner at a restaurant on the eleventh floor of a hotel. I have a table by the window. I have the sea bass, with a purée of cauliflower, and a glass of white. At the next table a couple are celebrating something. The woman has a hand over her mouth. The man is crying, a little, and laughing. I watch them without any particular feeling. It is a thing I do. I watch a man in tears, and I record, in the small ledger of the mind, the colour of his face, which is red, and the angle of his head, and the way that the woman's hand has come to rest on his sleeve, and I think, *there is nothing wrong with this.* I am aware of a small, level pleasure at the neatness of the scene.

*\[Expenses, and mine. The hotel, the table for one, the sea bass and the glass of white are on the report, with the date. The couple at the next table are mine. I have no authority that he watched anyone cry. Both of the people I spoke to said that he was kind. I note it. I do not take it at its word.\]*

At 22:30 I am home. I take off my shoes, and I place them on the rack. I wash my face, in the six steps. I get into the bed, of 180 centimetres, with a white cotton sheet. I lie for sixty seconds on my back in the dark.

I do not dream. I sleep very well.

§&#32;

The calendar says the day above was a Thursday. What follows is the pass, and then what I have supposed. The voice is mine again, not his.

*I have kept the boarding pass. On the front of it is the name of a city, and a flight number, and a gate. On the back are nine words, and under them, in the same pen, in a hand that is slightly less steady, a tenth. I didn't** notice it for a long time. It is written very small, in the corner, as if the writer did not wish to be seen writing it. It is a single word, and it is not in the language of the other nine. It is the Polish for sorry.*

I do not know when he wrote the tenth word, or that he wrote it knowingly. The ink is the same pen and the hand is slightly less steady, and that is all the pass tells me. Both of the people I spoke to said that he never asked what was done in the place between the numbers. One said it as praise. The man in the room is mine, and he must not be taken for a witness.

## Chapter 12: The Codex (Protocol 004)

**ALEX** · 2036

*Binder one. Reproduced as found.*

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · Protocol 004

**SYNPTCL/PROTOCOL/004 · THE CODEX**

**Compiled: D. Antonoff · Status: ACTIVE · Reliability: PROB\_VAL\_0.8**

**1. SCOPE**

This document describes the archive in which it is itself filed. It records the structure of the Codex, the rules by which items enter it, the marks by which they are classified, and the evidence chain on which its principal finding rests. It does not record what the Codex is for. That is the question the Codex cannot answer from inside.

The reader should be aware that this document is a recursion. It is an item in the archive which describes the archive. If it contains an error, the error is also in the thing it describes. I have decided to leave this as it is.

**2. WHY THERE IS A CODEX**

A recording is not a record. A recording is an entity that has been kept. It becomes a record when it is connected to the other things that were true at the time: the date, the place, the weather, the speaker's condition, the source of the information, the reason to believe it. Without those connections it is a voice in a box. It may be the truth. It cannot be shown to be.

The principal risk to this work is not that items will be lost. It is that they will survive and mean nothing. I have written this on the inside cover of the first binder, and I'll set it down here for the same reason: that a thing that cannot be tied to the rest of what is known is, in the end, indistinguishable from an invention.

Everything in the Codex is therefore connected to everything else. No item is incidental. This is a design principle and also a discipline, and it is hard on the person who keeps it.

**3. NUMBERING**

Three layers are in use.

**3.1. Document level.** Protocols are numbered SYNPTCL/PROTOCOL/nnn. Binder one holds 001 to 003 and this document. Further Protocols will be added as the work requires.

**3.2. Item level.** Every diary entry, recording, signal and photograph is numbered SY/xx/nnnn\_yy, where xx is the initials of the person the item concerns (DA if it concerns no one else), nnnn is a four-digit sequence number within the type, and yy is the type. Diary entries share one sequence. Every other type keeps its own.

| **Type** | **Meaning** |
| --- | --- |
| DD | Diary entry |
| GR | Ground recording. A testimony, in the speaker's own voice |
| SG | Signal. A transmission sent or intercepted |
| PH | Photograph |
| RF | Reference. A document not made by me |

Sequence numbers are assigned in the order of filing, not the order of the events recorded. This is deliberate. An entry about June may be filed in November. The number says when it was understood.

**3.3. Field log.** Each diary entry carries a header of date, place, conditions, temperature, pressure and flood state. It is the same for every entry. It is the only part of the Codex that is not written but measured. I have found that it steadies the hand.

**4. THE FOUR MARKS**

Every item is classified under one of four master marks. Each has secondary marks beneath it.

**4.1. SYNC\_PATH. Locations.** Two circles joined by a line. The mark does not show a place. It shows the distance between places, and what the distance costs. Secondary marks: PROX\_SCAN for outposts, TRIGGER\_V1 for Russian positions, ACT\_SYNC for escape routes.

**4.2. PROB\_VAL\_0.9. Characters.** The reliability matrix. Every person is rated for the reliability of what they say. No one receives 1.0. The ratings are provisional and are revised on new evidence. Secondary marks: CHROMA\_VAL for the primary figures, TORUS\_VAL for those who orbit them, GRID\_FAIL for the occupier.

**4.3. FLUX\_LOCK\_09. Objects.** Flow captured. Something contained, transmitted, classified. Objects in this city are of two kinds, vessels and weapons, and often both. Secondary marks: SHIFT\_REF\_04 for the Dust, MAG\_REF\_400 for the organism itself, LINK\_SYNC for communications, FLUX\_SYNC for the archive's own documents.

**4.4. ION\_PATH. Lore.** The helix. History, biology, the vocabulary of the zone. It runs beneath the other three. Secondary marks: LOAD\_STRESS for effects on the mind, DIFF\_REF\_89 for historical parallels, REF\_SYNC\_400 for cross-references, PORE\_SEQ for the language.

I have been asked, by one person, why the mark for lore should resemble the organism. I answered that I had not designed it to. I have since reconsidered. The helix is the shape of what makes things alike. It is the shape the Dendron has, and the shape of the thing that carries a family's features from one generation to the next. I have no explanation for why those two should coincide. I notice that the coincidence is exact.

**5. RELIABILITY**

All persons are rated. The scale runs from 0 to 1. No person has been rated 1.0 and none will be.

I have rated myself. I record it here, because a Codex in which the compiler is exempt from its own method is not a Codex.

| **Person** | **Rating** | **Reason** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| D. Antonoff | 0.8 | One observer. No independent check. Observer has a stake in the outcome |

The observer has a stake in the outcome. I have thought about whether to strike that line. I have left it in.

**6. DEGRADATION**

The condition of a mark carries meaning.

| **Condition** | **Applied to** |
| --- | --- |
| Clean | Russian military documents. Pre-occupation records |
| Lightly worn | The first Protocols and early entries |
| Significantly degraded | Later testimonies. Items damaged by flood |
| Barely legible | Marks of the organism, which were never meant to be read by those reading them |

The marks in this binder are lightly worn. I expect them to degrade. I have noted that it will not matter, provided the connections hold.

**7. THE EVIDENCE CHAIN**

The principal finding of the Codex is that the organism was not an accident of war and was not a Russian weapon alone. The finding rests on the chain below. It is partial. It is documented, link by link, so that a reader who doubts any link can examine it and, if necessary, remove it, and see whether the chain still holds.

| **Link** | **Item** | **Description** | **Rating** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| WT-01 | SY/DA/0001\_RF | The crate and its label (English; "Consignee", "Project", "for post-exposure assessment") | 0.3 |
| WT-02 | SY/DA/0001\_GR | The testimony of the Russian biochemist, 30 October | 0.5 |
| WT-03 | SY/MW/0012\_SG | An intercept, Szucha net: a reference to "the partners" and to "the second delivery" | 0.5 |
| WT-04 | SY/DA/0002\_RF | A page of equipment inventory with English labels, recovered from a dead courier | 0.4 |
| WT-05 | SY/DA/0001\_SG | The route of a single supply flight into the airport, three times, at night, with no declared cargo | 0.6 |
| WT-06 | SY/DA/0003\_RF | An absence: the organism is not in the open scientific record before 2024, though it must have been known | 0.7 |
| WT-07 | SY/DA/0004\_RF | A list of funds, partially legible, moving through a research foundation, passed to me by a person I will not name | 0.4 |
| WT-08 | (reserved) | The brief | n/a |

Link WT-08 is reserved. It is the one that would make the chain a case. I do not have it. I am told that I am going to be given it.

I have recorded the weakness of each link in the column on the right. A chain is as strong as its weakest link, and this one has several. But a chain in which every link points the same way, in which every link has been rated honestly and in which the rating has not been rounded up, is a different thing from a rumour. I think that is the whole of the difference between a rumour and a Protocol.

**8. LIMITATIONS**

This document does not record what the Codex is for. It does not record who will read it. It does not record whether the signal to Kyiv is being received, or whether, if it is, the bureau has a place to put it.

**9. NOTES TO FILE**

9.1. The method is my father's. I have said so in Protocol 003 and I'll say it more fully here, because it is the only thing I can give him.

He kept the archive of a chemical works in Kharkiv for thirty years. He was not a chemist. He was a person who knew where the chemistry was. When a new compound was made in the laboratory, it came to him on a card, and he gave it a number and a place, and the number and the place were, in effect, its name. He believed that a thing which was named correctly had been understood, and that a thing which had been understood was, in a sense that he did not often put into words, forgiven.

He worked in the evenings. After dinner he took his tea to the study at the back of the flat, a small room with a lamp and a radiator and charts pinned to every surface, and I sat on the floor with my back against the radiator. He did not tell me to be quiet. He did not tell me to go. He let me sit there and watch a man impose order on a thing that had not had it, and I thought, I am watching the most loving act in the world.

I have never told anyone that I thought so, and I'm not sure that I was wrong.

9.2. On Sundays he took me to the river. It was at the end of our street, and in summer it was warm and brown and slow, and there were dragonflies over the reeds. He taught me to count them. I'm not sure that I knew it was a lesson. He would stand beside me with his hands in his pockets, and say, quietly, "Which is that?" and I would tell him, and he would say, "How do you know?" And I would say how, in the way a child does: by the wings, by the way it held them, by the colour of the tail. He said that this was the whole of science. He said: look, name it, say how you know.

There are four species at the Żerań dock. The banded demoiselle, which is the dark one, with the blue-black wings. The four-spotted chaser, which hovers. The ruddy darter, which turns the colour of brick in August. And the emperor, which is the big one, and which does not, I have found, stay still for anyone.

I have counted them on thirty-one mornings. The counting was not required by this document.

9.3. The Żerań canal has a beaver. I have recorded it. I have not told anyone how much I look for it.

9.4. I used to think that the Codex was a way of making sure that nothing was forgotten. I see now that I had it the wrong way round. It is a way of making sure that nothing is forgotten wrongly. There's a difference, and my father knew it, and I did not until this month.

9.5. There will be a fifth Protocol. I don't yet know what it will record. I have an idea that it will be the one that records what happens to the others. I have also started to write the thing that comes after the Protocols. It is not a Protocol. I do not yet know what it is.

9.6. I think I'm afraid that this is the last thing that I'll be allowed to write in this form.

**10. ITEMS NOT IN SCOPE**

10.1. The location of the Codex's second copy.

10.2. The identity of the person who will be given it.

10.3. The date.

10.4. Item 14 (reserved).

*End of Protocol 004.*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · 2036

I have a confession to make, and it is that I read this Protocol last.

I found the Codex in the barn, in a waterproof case, in order. I read the diaries first, because they were first. I read the testimonies, because they were testimonies. I read the first three Protocols with the care I give to a legal document. And when I came to this one, I turned it over and read the evidence chain, and I did not read the notes. I took them for the housekeeping that follows every report.

It was months before I went back. It was a Sunday, I remember, in a rented room with the window open, and I read the notes through to the end, and I put the page down, and I went out and stood for a time on the pavement in the sun.

She had told me. That was what I understood. In the third Protocol she had told me, in a line about a beaver and a line about dragonflies, that the thing she had built was a kind of love. And in this one she had told me whom for. And I had stepped over it in a hurry, with the evidence chain in my hand, because it was the part that looked like the part that mattered.

The last note was the one I could not leave alone. *I think I'm afraid that this is the last thing that I'll be allowed to write in this form.* I looked at the date on the header. It was filed, according to the sequence number, three weeks before the flood.

I have kept the page. I have not changed its rating. I have never, in all my reading, found a document to which I would give a lower number and a higher trust.

## Chapter 13: The Youngest

**ALEX** · Warsaw, March 2036

Alisia Lewandowska stole my pen.

I mention it first because she mentioned it first. I had put it on the table in a glass-walled meeting room in the Warsaw office of an anti-corruption foundation, between my notebook and a plate of biscuits, and when we had shaken hands and sat she looked at it for about a second and said, "Is it one you mind losing?"

I said that I did.

"Then you should put it in your pocket," she said, with great kindness, and I looked down and saw that it was no longer on the table. It was in front of her, beside her elbow. She pushed it back across to me with one finger. "Sorry. It's a reflex. Nobody told my hands." And she grinned.

She was sixteen. She was small and quick, with a ponytail and a lanyard and a sweatshirt that said *WARSAW SPRING FILM FESTIVAL 2029*, which I think must have come from somewhere other than a shop, and she had a way of leaning back in her chair with her arms crossed that made her look both younger and a good deal more dangerous than her years. She was the youngest investigator on a team of eleven. They had hired her, her supervisor told me in the corridor, because she could find the paper trail in a building where nobody else could, and because, as he put it, "she knows where people hide things. It's a skill with an unusual origin."

I said that I had been told she was honest.

"I'm very honest," said Alisia, behind me, who had come out with the biscuits. "It's the one thing I've done that I can't be arrested for."

Diana's entry is from late October, from the Zoo. It is the only one in the archive in which she describes a thing she saw and did nothing about. I have put it first because I have found that readers, if they have not read it, tend to draw the wrong conclusion about the girl.

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 26 October 2034

**SY/AL/0027\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date: **26 October 2034, 16:50*

***Location: **OP-Zoo, the keeper's house*

***Conditions: **overcast, cold, a thin rain*

***Temperature: **7°C*

***Pressure:** 1015 hPa, falling slowly*

***Flood:** stable. Staff gauge 84.1 m.*

*I have to write this down exactly. I'm afraid that if I do not, I'll improve it.*

*I was at the keeper's house to see Anna. It is a small brick house at the south end of the Zoo, inside the old reptile garden, with a pitched roof and a tin chimney, and it now belongs** to an elderly couple, Pan and Pani Wróbel, who have taken in two children. The elder, Anna, is perhaps thirteen. The younger is Nikita, who is six, and who doesn't speak. I had come with a message from Catalina. I'd been told that I should knock twice and wait, because the little one is frightened of a single knock.*

*I knocked twice. Pani Wróbel was out at the cistern. Pan Wróbel was asleep in a chair. Anna was in the back room with a book, and she said I should wait, and I sat on the step in the porch, with the door ajar, from **where I could see the main room.*

*I saw this.*

*A girl came in at the side window. It is the one **window that has no catch. She was fourteen, perhaps, small, in a grey anorak that was too big, and she came in the way a cat comes into a kitchen: without making any sound, and without looking as if she was doing anything. She didn't **see me. I was in shadow, beyond the door. She crossed the room to the shelf by the stove. She took a half loaf of bread, a paper bag of flour, and, from the end of the settle, a folded grey blanket. She put them in a sack that she had under her arm. It took about eleven seconds.*

*I knew who she was. I had heard about **her from Catalina. Alisia, the girl whose mother had been ill. I did not know that she was the one who took.*

*I did not move a muscle**. I want that set down. I was four metres away, in daylight, with a clear view, and I did not say anything and I did not stand up. I have thought about why. I think the reason is this. I had my notebook open on my knee. And I was aware, quite clearly, that I was a person who writes things down. That I was in the zone as a witness, not as a participant, that it was not my place to put my hand on a child's arm. And I held on to that principle, which I had made, with a very firm grip, and I watched a fourteen-year-old take a blanket from two old people and a six-year-old.*

*I'm not proud of it. I'm going to leave the word principle in the entry, because it is the word I used, and I want to see what it looks like on the page.*

*She was at the window when the small one came out of the back room.*

*Nikita. She had been, I think, in the room all the time, on the floor behind the stove, where she goes when she is frightened. She stood in the doorway in a pair of socks, with a ragged toy rabbit in one hand. She looked at the girl. The girl looked at her.*

*Nothing was said. I'd like to be precise: not a word was said, in either direction, for what I counted as twenty-two seconds. Nikita did not cry out. She did not call. She came across the room, in her socks, quite slowly, until she was standing in front of the girl at the window. She looked at the sack. Then she put her hand, which is very small, on the girl's wrist, flat, as one puts a hand on a stove to see whether it is hot. And she left it there. And she looked up.*

*I have never seen a look like it. I will not describe it. I'll say that there was no anger in it, and no fear, and that the girl at the window, who had, I think, been looked at by a great many angry people, went a colour I have not seen on a human face. She did not move. She did not take her wrist away.*

*After a time, Nikita took her hand back. She turned, and went to the stove, and picked up the grey blanket from the sack, and carried it back to the settle, folding it as she went. Then she stood and looked at the bread.*

*The girl put the bread back on the shelf. She put the flour on the shelf. She stood in the middle of the room and did not seem to know what to do with her hands. And then she went out, not by the window, but by the front door, which put her about a metre from me. She saw me. She had not known. I watched her realise that I had been there the whole time, and that I'd seen, and that I had done nothing, and I'm not able to say which of those she minded most.*

*"You're the journalist," she said.*

*I said that I was.*

*"Are you going to write it down?"*

*I said that I didn't **know.*

*She said, "You should. It's what happened." And she went down the path, quite fast, not running.*

*I didn't go in at once. Anna called from the back room, and asked who had been at the door, and I said that it was nobody, which is a lie, and the first I have written in this book.*

*\[in pencil, margin: I held a principle and watched. what is a witness for. what is a witness for, if not this.\]*

\* \* \*

**ALISIA** · recording, Warsaw, March 2036

**SY/AL/0001\_GR** · *Alisia Lewandowska, recorded in Warsaw, March 2036*

I'll be fast, because I get bored, and because I want you to put the funny bits in. People never put the funny bits in.

I grew up in Hel. The peninsula, on the Baltic. My dad was a fisherman. He had a boat called the *Marzena*, after my mum, who was furious. We lived in a house with a blue door and a smell of fish, and I'd say I was a happy child, if I hadn't been told that I was a terrible one. I stole from the age of five. It started with sweets. I thought it was the same as picking blackberries. Everything was free if you took it.

When the flood came to the coast, I mean the first one, the one where the sea did the surge and the river did the rest, my dad went out in the *Marzena* to get a neighbour off a roof. He got the neighbour off. He didn't get himself back. They found the boat. I was nine.

We came to Warsaw because my mum had a sister in Praga. That's a story. I'll skip it. The sister had gone. The flat had gone. My mum and I walked around with two bags for three weeks, and I realised that nobody was going to give us anything, and that if I wanted my mum to eat I was going to have to get it myself. It wasn't a decision. It was a fact. I got very good, very fast. I was the best thief in the district by twelve. I'm not boasting. It was the only thing I was the best at.

And I was a good thief. I want that on the record, because people sometimes assume that a thief is a bad person who does bad things. I was a person who did a thing that was bad, and it kept two people alive, and I didn't think about it much. That's a position. You can maintain it for a surprisingly long time.

My mum got sick in the autumn of 2034. It was pneumonia. It was very bad. I went to the hospital at Brodno, because I'd heard that the woman there didn't turn people away, and I was told, at the door, by a boy with a mop, that she was out. I stood there. I didn't knock. I don't knock. I'm not built for it. I just stood. And she came out, a tall woman with grey hair, and she looked at me, and she said, "Your mother?" And I said yes. She said, "I'll come." And she did. She came to the Zoo, to the shed we were in, and she looked at my mum, and she went away again, and the next night a man in a coat came and put something in my hand. I've never asked how. I know now, because Catalina told me, and I didn't know where to look.

My mum lived. I want to say that. She's in Gdynia now. She makes jam.

I went to the shelf in the Wróbels' house on the twenty-sixth of October. I want to be clear that I knew exactly whose it was. I didn't take it because I didn't know. I took it because it was easy, and because I had taken it from forty other people, and I hadn't looked at a single one of them. That's the thing about the habit. It's designed so you don't look.

Nikita looked.

I'll tell you what it was like. I've been looked at by a lot of people. Teachers, police, a man at Praski with a stick. I know what a look is. It's a thing you can push against. What she did wasn't that. She put her hand on me, and it was cold, and it was the smallest hand I have ever felt, and there was nothing in it, and there was nothing in her face. She wasn't accusing me. Do you see? She didn't need to. She was just letting me be there. In front of her. With the sack. And I realised that I had never been in the room before. All the other times I had been taking things from a shelf. This time I was taking them from a person, and the person was six, and she'd folded the blanket back up for me because she thought I was cold.

I don't know what I'd have done if she'd shouted. I think I'd have run. She didn't shout.

I went to the Brigade the next morning and told them. I said, I'm the one who's been taking things. I said that I would like to put it back. I thought I was going to be thrown out. They had a hearing, in the chapel, and a very old woman said, "What can you do?" And I said, "I know where everything is." There was a silence. Then Pani Olszewska said, quite gently, "Then you can tell us."

I became the supply coordinator for the Solidarity Brigade at fifteen. I'm still told that it's the youngest appointment in the history of the Brigade, and also the best. I knew every hiding place, every cache, every thing that anyone had stashed. I'd stolen from most of them. I kept a ledger, a proper one, in a hard-backed exercise book. It was Catalina's idea.

You'll want to know about the peaches? Everyone does.

When my mum was better, I wanted to say thank you to Catalina, and I didn't know how. I'm not good at it. So I got a tin of peaches. Not from a shop. I took it from a man at Praski who had a tin of peaches on a stall and who I knew was a crook. I put it on her dispensary shelf at night. She never knew who. And then, two years later, in Kraków, her partner Pawel tells me, at a dinner, that the tin of peaches was stolen. And I said, I know. And he said, no, *from me*. He'd stolen it from the same man. And I'd stolen it from him, by accident, in a different shop. So the peaches had been through four hands and every one of us had stolen them. I think it's still going.

*(end of recording)*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · Warsaw, March 2036

She had been laughing, a little, throughout. When the recorder was off she did not stop at once, and then she did, and she looked at her hands, which were flat on the table, and I noticed that the pen was back beside her elbow.

"I still take things," she said. "I'd like to be straight about that. I took a stapler from your hotel. No, I'm joking. I'm not joking. I took a pen." She turned it over. "It's under control. I've got it channelled. I do it for work now. If you put a person in a building where the paperwork's been hidden, and give her a reason, and a warrant, then it's a gift. I just had to find something to steal that was worth the trouble."

I asked if Nikita had ever said anything about that day.

"No. She's never said anything." She was quiet. "I've been to Wrocław. Twice. I took her a present, a tin of paints, I paid for it, I've got the receipt. She took it. She looked at me. She put her hand on my wrist." She laid her own hand on her wrist, flat, as one might on a stove, and looked at it. "I think I've been forgiven. I think that's what that was. But I'll tell you, I've never been sure. It's a thing you can only ever be sure of if she says. And she won't."

I said that perhaps she had.

"Maybe," said Alisia. "I'll think about it. I think about everything for a long time. It's the other half of the reflex."

Diana's note on this transcript is the only one in the archive that she wrote after, not during. It is dated, in ink, three weeks later. *I did nothing, and it was the right thing, and I do not know why. She had to be looked at by the one who had the right. I was only the one who saw.* And under it, in pencil, which I have found hardest to read, as though the hand had pressed down and then lifted: *Next time I will stand up.*

I don't know whether there was a next time. I have not been able to find one.

## Chapter 14: The Mouse

**ALEX** · Wrocław, February 2036

Nikita agreed to see me on one condition, and it was relayed in a letter signed with a drawing of a small bird.

*Anna must be there.*

I should say that I would have agreed to a good deal more. I had been told by four people, in four cities, what to expect, and none of them had been able to say it in a way that prepared me. Fritha said that she looked at you as if she were listening. Tanja said that she was the only person who had ever heard her play. Alisia said, after a silence, that she had a very cold hand. Magda said only, "She knows when you're lying. She's eight. Don't."

They lived in a flat in Wrocław, on the second floor of a block near the river. It was clean, small and orderly, with a table in the kitchen and a window onto a courtyard in which a tree had been planted by someone who believed in it. Anna opened the door. She was fifteen, tall for it, with a long plait and the careful wary look of a girl who has been in charge of another human being since she was thirteen, and she took my coat and hung it, and said, "She's in the kitchen. She'll want you to sit on the left. The chair on the right is hers."

Nikita was at the table. She was eight, small for it, with enormous grey eyes and straight fair hair that someone had cut very evenly, and in front of her were a pad of lined paper, a tin of watercolours with a ribbon on it, and a ragged toy rabbit, sitting upright against the sugar bowl as if it too had been invited.
