She looked at me. I do not know how long. I have been looked at by a number of people for the purposes of deciding whether I could be talked to, and I have never been so thoroughly read.

Then she picked up a pencil and wrote something and pushed the pad across.

*Sit on the left.*

I sat on the left.

Diana's entries on her are the most unusual in the archive. I will say why. Diana's work was to record what people said. She had been given a person who did not speak, and she did not, as far as I can establish, make a single attempt to make her. She made, instead, a method. I have put the first entry here, from the first week of November, and I would ask the reader to notice what she records and what she leaves out.

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 4 November 2034

**SY/NS/0029\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date:** 4 November 2034, 15:30*

***Location: **OP-Zoo, the keeper's house*

***Conditions:** cold, bright, a sharp wind from the north*

***Temperature:** 4°C*

***Pressure:** 1019 hPa, rising*

***Flood: **stable. First ice at the margins.*

*Nine days since the incident with the blanket. I have been coming every second afternoon. I have not been asked to. I have not asked to be allowed. I have simply knocked twice and been let in.*

*I have a problem of method, and I will state it plainly. How does one document a person who does not speak?*

*My instruments are a recorder, which has nothing to record, and a notebook, which is a thing the child can see. On the first afternoon I took neither out. I sat on the floor, by the stove, with my hands in my lap, as Fritha does, and waited. I waited an hour. Nikita did not look at me. She played with a bit of string. On the second afternoon I did the same. On the third she came and sat about a metre away, with her back to me, and I understood that this was the first sign.*

*On the fifth afternoon she tapped the floor twice with her finger.*

*I tapped twice.*

*She turned round. I think it is the first time I have seen her face with any expression in it. It was not a smile. It was the look of a person who has finally found out that the other person has understood the signal.*

*We have a language now. It is eleven items long. I will set it down, because I do not trust myself not to forget, and because it is the only record of the exchange that exists.*

*Two taps: yes.*

*One tap: no.*

*Three taps: I don't** know.*

*A flat hand on the floor: stop. (She also uses this, I have noticed, to mean "it's too loud.")*

*A flat hand against her own ear: listen.*

*A finger drawn along the floor in a line: water.*

*A fist, opened slowly: something is coming.*

*Two fingers walking: a person.*

*A circle drawn on the floor: the sound.*

*A hand on my wrist, flat: I am here.*

*A hand taken away: that is enough for today.*

*I have not taught her any of it. I only answered. She made the language and I learned it.*

*I'd like to record the thing that she did on the seventh afternoon, which I think is a finding. She had a pencil by then, which Anna gave her, and a notebook, and when I made the sign for water she took the pencil and drew a line, and then she drew a circle with a wave inside it, and then a cloth over the top of the circle, like a hood, and she held it up to me.*

*I didn't** understand at first**. I said, "The sound?" She tapped twice.*

*She was drawing the sound. The one that comes at night from across the canal, the broad, low, sustained note I have recorded eleven times on the roof of the control room. I have not told anyone that I hear it. Not Catalina, not Lucasz. She had drawn it. And she had drawn the cloth. I have written, in my own notes, on the 2nd of September, the words "a siren with its mouth stopped," and I did not tell her. She has not read my notes. She cannot read a great deal.*

*I asked, using the signs, whether she could hear it now. She tapped twice. I asked whether other people could. She tapped once. I asked whether it was loud. She put her flat hand over her ear and shook her head, and then she held up her hand, thumb and forefinger apart by a few centimetres, and I understood: small, but always.*

*I do not know how to record that a six-year-old has been hearing, all this time, what the instruments pick up and the adults do not. It is not in any category I have. I have put it under MAG\_REF\_400, the mark for the organism itself, and I have used her drawing for the mark. I have copied it in my own hand. It is the first time I have taken a symbol from a child.*

*I think she is the most accurate instrument in the zone. I haven't** told her that. I'll tell her in the only way I can: that I'll keep what she draws.*

*At the end of the afternoon she took her hand from my wrist, which means enough for today, and I went out into the cold, and I realised that I had, for the first time since arriving, been in a room for two hours without writing anything down. It is the only record I have of that.*

*\[in pencil, margin: what is a witness for? this one. she is the witness and I am the stenographer.\]*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · Wrocław, February 2036

She did not write at once. I had put my question to her on a single sheet, in block capitals, folded in half, and she had read it and laid it face down on the table, and then she had taken the lid from the tin of watercolours and begun to paint.

Anna gave me tea. She did it without asking, and without sitting, and she put the cup on my left, where the saucer would not be in the way of my elbow, and I understood that I was being managed with the same exactness as a piece of furniture, and was grateful for it. She stood by the stove with her arms folded. "It'll be an hour," she said. "Maybe two. She isn't slow. She's waiting for something. I don't know what. I've given up asking."

I asked what she waited for.

"The right sound in her head," said Anna. "She will not write a word until she can hear it. I used to think that was a game."

There were rules in the flat. I learned them in the first quarter of an hour, not because anyone told me but because they were so evident, once seen, that it would have taken effort to miss them. The kitchen door was kept half open, at an angle of about forty-five degrees, never shut and never flat to the wall. The chair at the head of the table was Nikita's, and no one sat in the chair on her right. The cutlery drawer ran on a wheel that squealed, and had been oiled, and then oiled again, until it made no sound at all. The tap had a rubber washer on the spout so that the water fell on to it and not on to the steel. The radiator had been bled. The curtain rings had been taken off the rail and replaced with ribbons. Even the clock, which was on the wall above the cooker, was an electric one with a silent sweep, and I noticed that the second hand moved so smoothly that I could not tell where it was.

"She can't bear a tick," said Anna, following my eye. "She can bear a bell. She can bear a bang, a door, a dog. But not a tick. It's the regularity. She says it's like someone counting."

I said that I could see how a person might.

"No," she said, quite gently. "You can't."

Nikita, meanwhile, painted. She had laid a sheet of thick paper on a board and wetted it all over with a flat brush, and now she was dropping colour into the wet, a little at a time, from a brush no larger than a grain of rice. First a grey. A very light one, blue in the shadow. Then a darker grey, in a band across the lower third. Then she stopped and sat back, and for a minute she looked at the paper as one looks at a sleeping child. The colour moved. The grey crept outward from the band like breath on a window and softened at the edges, and she let it, and did not touch it.

Anna said, "She does this every day. She has done it since Diana gave her the tin." And she told me, standing at the stove, with her arms still folded, about the tin: that it had come in a parcel with a ribbon, a flat one of tin with twelve pans of colour and a brush in a groove, that Diana had had it a year before from a woman who ran a shop on the old Chmielna, and that for a long time Nikita would not open it. She kept it by the bed. She slept with a hand on it. In the third month she opened the lid, looked at the colours for a day, closed it, and put it back. In the fourth month she took out the grey.

"Only the grey?" I asked.

"For a year," said Anna. "Only grey. Then a blue. Then, last spring, she asked for yellow. She did not ask. She tapped three times at the window, where the sun was."

I said that I did not know the taps.

She unfolded her arms. She came and sat, finally, at the chair on my left, and put her hands flat on the oilcloth, and showed me. "One is no. Two is yes. Three is wait, or I'm thinking, or I don't know. Four is for someone who has gone out of the room. It isn't often used." She rapped them on the table with a fingernail, softly, one after another, and as she did it I saw Nikita's head turn by a few degrees, without her eyes leaving the paper, and I understood that she had heard every one of them as I would have heard a word.

"And five?" I asked.

Anna did not answer. She looked at the table. After a time she said, "There isn't a five. Diana had one. She used to tap it at the door when she came in. Five quick. It meant, I am here, and I am not leaving. Nikita hasn't asked for it since."

The painting had begun to dry. I saw now what it was. It was a plain grey circle, drawn freehand, with a small wave inside it, a single line, and over the whole of it a pale square the colour of mist, laid on as though it were a cloth. She made another beside it. She made a third. She laid them out on the table in a row, three circles with a wave and a cloth, and left a space between the third and the edge, as if there were to be a fourth and she was not yet sure whether to make it.

"It's the sound," said Anna. "The one from the pipes. She started to draw it after the Zoo. Diana saw it. She had it put on a card. She said it was the most accurate drawing of a hum she had ever seen."

I looked at it for some time. It was an odd sensation. I had listened to eleven hours of tape in which that hum was named a dozen ways, in the language of physics and of tradecraft and of fear, and here it was in watercolour, in three shades of grey, and it was accurate. It was the sound of someone crying with a hand over their mouth.

Nikita rinsed the brush. She tapped the water jar, once, with the handle of it, and the sound was so small and so clear that I felt it in my back teeth. Then she took the pencil and the pad, and she turned over my sheet of block capitals, and she read my question again, slowly, with her lips closed.

Anna said, very low, "She's ready."

I had not seen anything change. But the room had changed, in the way a room does when a clock that has been ticking stops, and the quiet was a sort of inclination, the whole of the kitchen leaning towards a small girl with a pencil. I picked up my own pen and then put it down. Anna touched my shoulder. It was a very light touch. It said, as clearly as any word, *do not*.

I sat with my hands in my lap. And she began.

\* \* \*

**NIKITA** · written, Wrocław, February 2036

**SY/NS/0001\_GR** · *Nikita Sokołowski, written, Wrocław, February 2036. Transcribed by A. Kovalchuk. Spelling corrected only where it prevented sense.*

I am going to write this on the pad. Anna says I can stop when I want to. I will stop when I want to.

First, I will say that I can talk. Anna says people think I can't. I can. I do not want to. It is not a sickness. It is a decision. Anna is the only person who knows this, and now you.

I am writing it in order.

My mum and dad lived with me and Anna in a flat. It was on a high floor. There were many stairs. I used to sit on the landing and listen to people coming up. I can hear a person from three floors away. I can tell them apart. My dad walked heavily, on the heels. My mum walked on her toes. Anna is quick and light, like a bird, which is why they call her Little Bird.

One night I heard some people on the stairs. They were not ours. There were five of them. They were wearing boots and I knew the boots. They were the ones from the checkpoint. I heard them from the bottom of the building. I counted the floors as they came up. I didn't say anything. I thought I should say something and I didn't know what. I thought it might not be them.

When they were on the floor below, I told my dad. I whispered it, in his ear. I said, there are men. And my dad looked at me. He put his finger on his lips. He said, very quietly, "Don't make a sound." And he put me in the cupboard.

I stayed in the cupboard. I won't write the next part. I will write that it was a long time, and that I did what he said, and that when it was over the flat was very quiet, and my parents were not there.

I thought about it after. I thought about it for a very long time. My dad had said, don't make a sound. And I had made one. I had whispered. And then the men had come. I thought that the sound had made them come. I know now that it was not that. Anna says it was not that. But I was six and I thought about it every day. I thought: if I make a sound, they come.

So I stopped. It was not hard. It was like putting a blanket on a lamp.

Anna found me in the cupboard the next day. She was thirteen then. She took me out. She did not say anything about it. She put me on her back, and she carried me down all the stairs, and she took me to the sewers, because that was where you went. I want to write about the sewers.

It was dark. It was always dark. You couldn't even see your hand. And that was when I found out what I was for. I couldn't see, but I could hear. I could hear the water in the pipe, and I could hear people breathing from far off, and I could hear when someone was frightened because it makes the breath short. I could hear what was above us. I could hear the boots. Anna said, "What is it?" And I put my hand on her wrist, and I held up two fingers, and then three. She understood.

I was the one who knew. We went through the sewers for three weeks. I was the one who said stop. I was six. I was the best ear in the whole of the tunnels. I was proud of that, though I'm not sure I should say so.

And I could hear the other thing. The sound. It was in the pipes. It was low, and it went on and on, and it had a cloth on it. It was like a person crying with their hand over their mouth. I could hear it all the time. It was not frightening. It was only sad.

And then I came out into the light, and I heard the violin.

It was the first sound that did not frighten me. It came across the water and into the keeper's house, and I was on the floor behind the stove, and it did not make the men come. It did the opposite. It made a place. I don't know how else to put it. I could sit in it, like a room.

That is Tanja. I never said so, and she never knew, but she was the only person who did not make me say anything. She played, and I listened, and that was enough. I think she knew. She looked at me once, and nodded.

Diana came. I knew her feet. She walked on the outside of the foot, a bit carefully, like someone who is afraid to hurt the floor. She did not make me say anything. She tapped, and I tapped. I want to write that she was the only grown-up who did not need me to be better. She wrote things in her notebook, but she did not write about me. She wrote what I told her. And she kept the drawing. She told me she kept it. She put it in a box and wrote a number on it.

Alisia came to the house one night and I put my hand on her wrist. I want to say that I was not angry. I wasn't. I was only checking. I could feel her pulse. It was very fast. I thought: she is more afraid than I am. I don't know what I wanted. I wanted her to know that somebody could see her. That is all. It is what I wanted for me.

Now I am eight and I live with Anna in Wrocław. I go to school. I do not talk at school. They have decided it is a condition. I let them think that.

You asked what the world owes people like me. I have thought about it. I do not think it owes me anything. I think it owes the people who were in the cupboard, and the tunnels, and the places where nobody was looking, a thing that is very small. It owes them a person who will sit on the floor and not ask.

You can stop writing now.

I do not want my voice back. I have thought about it. I have decided to keep it. It is the one thing nobody came up the stairs for.

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · Wrocław, February 2036

She had written for nearly an hour. Her handwriting was upright and large and had grown steadily smaller, as if she were drawing herself in. When she put down the pencil she did not look at me. She looked at Anna, who had been standing at the sink, with her back to us, for the whole of it. Anna turned. Her face was wet and perfectly composed.

"Is that all of it?" she said, to Nikita.

Nikita tapped twice.

"Can he print it?"

Nikita considered this for what felt like a minute. Then she turned the pad over, took the pencil, and wrote one word on the back, and held it up for me, not for Anna.

*Yes.*

She looked at me with the grey, unhurried look I had met at the door. And then she did a thing I had not expected, and which Anna told me afterwards she did only for people she had decided about. She reached across the table and put her small hand flat on my wrist.

It was very cold. I will say that. It was the smallest hand I have ever felt. And she left it there for as long as I had been in the room, and I did not move, and Anna, at the sink, did not move, and the rabbit, against the sugar bowl, sat upright and attended.

Diana's note against the transcript is the only one in the archive in a hand I do not recognise as hers. It is in pencil, in large upright letters, and under it, in Diana's own, a line of explanation. The first reads, *I can hear you writing.* The second reads: *She wrote it on the back of my notebook on the last afternoon. I did not know what she meant. I think I do now.*

## Interlude VI: Wiktor Szeliga, Shelter 9

*This too is a reconstruction, and I will be as plain about it as* *always.** I did not expect to write about **him again. Wiktor Szeliga set down nothing about himself, then or later. What I have is this: the intake ledger of Shelter 9, which is a former sports hall on the left bank, in a different pencil from the first warden's; a kitchen rota; a child's drawing, in felt tip, of a lemon; a gold ring on a shoelace, found in a coat pocket in the cloakroom; and the leaflet, A5, matt, with the photograph of a bed, which he had kept folded in four in his wallet and which has gone soft at the creases. I also have the girl, who is grown, and who spoke to me in a kitchen. She is the only witness I have to anything he said. I believe her about one sentence. For the rest she was ten, and has had a long time to keep it. I have set the day on the fourteenth of June, 2035, which is ten months after the first, because the ledger does. I have kept the voice the same. It was the only thing about him that did not change. My interruptions are bracketed and in italics, and each names its source: ledger, rota, drawing, ring, leaflet, interview, or mine, for what I have added.*

I wake at 06:30 without the alarm. I do not have the alarm. The travel clock was left in the flat. I wake at 06:30 because there is a strip of light that crosses the floor of the hall, from a high window, and reaches the third line of the basketball court at that hour, and I have noticed this, and it is a fact I am happy to have.

*\[Mine. The light, the third line of the court and the hour. I gave him the same waking I gave him the first time. The ledger does not say when he woke, and I do not think anyone was awake to see.\]*

The hall is forty-two metres by twenty-four. It has a sprung floor of pale maple, marked in yellow, white and blue, and a high ceiling of exposed steel trusses, painted white, from which hang two scoreboards and a line of strip lights, each with a wire cage. There are two hundred and sixty camp beds, in rows of ten, at an interval of eighty centimetres. They are folding, with a frame of aluminium and a canvas of olive drab, and each has a grey blanket with a red stripe and a number stencilled on it. My bed is number 118. It is the fourth from the end of the row, beside the wall bars. I have the bed on the left of me, number 117, which is the bed of the girl.

I fold the blanket. I fold it in three, lengthwise, and then in half, and I lay it at the foot of the bed with the red stripe uppermost and the number to the front. I do it in nineteen seconds. I have timed it. In the ledger of the hall I am listed in the column *Orderly*, which is the third of five categories, and which I am told is a good category to be in.

*\[Ledger. The bed numbers are in it, 118 for him and 117 for the girl, and so is the column Orderly, which is the third of five. A plan of the floor is pasted inside the cover. The maple, the trusses and the nineteen seconds are mine.\]*

At 06:40 I wash. The washroom is the old changing room. There are twenty-four basins in a row, with taps of brushed steel, and a rail, and a bench with a line of coat pegs. The water is cold. It comes from a pipe which was laid by the shelter's engineers and which has a small label, in marker, that says *CLEAN*. I wash with one-fifth of a litre, which is the allowance. There is a cake of soap, white, on a saucer, and a mirror above the basin, a long one, in which I can see the row of faces to my left and right at the same angle, all attentive, all with their eyes on the tap.

I shave with a disposable razor. I have had it since April. It is blue. It is within tolerance.

I put on the grey trousers, and the white shirt, and the grey cardigan. I have the same three items of clothing and I have, by the warden's inventory, been issued with a second set, which are on a hook above the bed, in a clear plastic bag, and which I have not unwrapped.

*\[Leaflet. It was folded in four in his wallet, and it has gone soft at the creases. I have left it in the wallet. He does not take it out in this day, and I could not find a reason to make him.\]*

At 07:00 I eat. The food is served from a trolley by the double doors, at a table of two trestles, by two women in white hairnets, and I stand in a queue of one hundred and ten. I have a bowl of porridge, a slice of bread, and a spoon of jam. The porridge is made with water and a little salt. It is a pale grey, with a skin. I eat it slowly, standing, with the bowl in my left hand and the spoon in my right. The bowl is of white enamel, with a blue rim and a chip at the position of four o'clock.

The girl sits on the end of her bed, with her feet not quite touching the floor. She is ten now. She was ten in February. She has grown four centimetres, which the warden recorded on the wall bars, in pen, with a date: a row of small lines up the third bar from the left. She eats the porridge. She eats it all. I have been watching, for the past four weeks, how much she eats, and it has been all of it, and I note this, on the back of the intake form, in pencil, though I am not required to. It is not within tolerance. It is above.

*\[Ledger. The heights are in it, monthly, in pen, as the warden recorded them on the bars. There is a column for food returned, and her line is empty. That he watched is mine.\]*

She says: *Wujek.*

I say, yes.

She says: *Can I have the jam?* It is the first thing she has asked me for since the sixth day of the summer before.

I give her my spoon of jam. It is plum, in a dark red, and she takes it with a small, round, flat movement of the spoon, and puts it in her mouth, and her face does something that I do not know how to describe. It is not a smile. It is the place where a smile will go.

*\[Interview. The girl remembers the jam. She remembers that he gave her his spoon. She does not remember asking. The way her face moved is mine, and I have no right to it, since she was the one wearing it.\]*

I say that it is quite all right.

At 08:00 the loudspeaker. It is on the wall above the double doors, a grey horn on a bracket, and the voice comes out of it at a volume that is much louder than on the east bank, and clear. It is a woman's voice. It is warm in the lower register, and there is a slight roughness in the vowels. *Good morning. The shelters on the left bank are open. There are beds. There is hot water. There is no need to be afraid.*

I heard it four hundred and thirty times in the old flat, and since, in this hall, three hundred and seven. I know where she breathes. I find that, in this hall, the room is so quiet during the pause after *water*, with two hundred and sixty persons standing with a spoon in the hand or a bowl or nothing, that I can hear my own watch.

There is no watch. I do not own one. It is the sound of a thing that was in the bone of the wrist.

*\[Transcript. The words are Magda Wiśniewska's, and the loudspeaker in the hall is in the ledger as a fitting, with the date it was put up. The count, and the breath, and the watch that is not there are mine.\]*

At 09:00 I work. I am on the potato detail. It is in the kitchen, which was the canteen, a long room with a counter and a steel serving hatch and six deep sinks, and I have a stool, and a knife, and a sack. The knife is a paring knife with a green plastic handle, six centimetres of blade, and it has been sharpened so many times that it has a slight curve like a new moon. I peel the potatoes. I do it in a single piece, the peel coming off in a long, unbroken spiral, which falls on the floor of the pail in a coil. I do eleven in a quarter of an hour. I have been told that this is faster than anyone else on the detail. I have been told that I have a gift for it.

The woman next to me is called Pani Grażyna. She is sixty, perhaps. She has a lump on her neck under the jaw and she hums as she works, though it is the same four notes, and I do not think that she knows that she is doing it. I should say that the hum is quite pleasant. It is the same hum that I hear in the evenings, from the vent, at the end of the hall, below the scoreboard. It is a little above the generator. It has a beat of one in six seconds. I have noticed that Pani Grażyna's hum, if one listens, keeps the beat.

*\[Rota and interview. The rota has a Grażyna beside his name on the potato detail from the autumn. The lump and the four notes are the girl's, who remembers her. The hum in the vent is in the ledger once, in the margin, in the warden's pencil: vent, again. That the humming kept the beat is mine.\]*

At 11:30 I have a visit from the warden. It is a man this time, Pan Cichocki, a retired surveyor, with a clipboard in a plastic sleeve and a ballpoint on a string. He asks the same questions in the same order. *Number of persons. Water. Food. Fuel. Health.* I give the same answers. I say that my health is quite all right.

He writes, and then he does not stop writing. He writes a longer line than the others. I watch the ballpoint. It is a new ballpoint, with a strong blue ink, and the line that he writes is dark.

He says, in a level voice, *Pan Wiktor, you have been in this hall for ten months.* I say that I have. He says that in ten months, he has seen me on the potato detail for 302 mornings out of 304. He says that he has not seen me absent. He says that he has not seen me angry. He says that I am, in the opinion of the staff, a model resident.

I say that I am glad.

He says: *Do you ever think about the flat?*

I say that I do not.

He says: *Or your mother?*

I look at the ballpoint. I look at the clear barrel and the line of ink that is left, which is two-thirds, and I look at the string.

I say that I have thought about her, in so far as it was necessary.

He writes this. He writes it exactly. I see the words appear in his small neat hand, on the form, in the box marked *Remarks*: *Thought about mother in so far as necessary.* And after a pause, he draws a line underneath.

*\[Ledger. The line in the box marked Remarks is as it is in the ledger, with the line under it. The warden's name is the ledger's too, and the 302 mornings out of 304 are the rota's. The conversation that leads to the line is mine, and so is every answer in it except the last, which is written down.\]*

At 13:00 I sit. I sit in the folding chair in the corner of the hall by the wall bars, in the sun that comes from the high window, with my hands on my knees. I do not read. I do not think about the kitchen equipment. I have not thought about it since the fifteenth of August. Eighteen gauge, I say. Sixteen. It is a pleasant thing to have a fact that stays where you put it, though I notice that, since the spring, the number has had, at the edge of it, a slight colour, as of a thing that has been held in the hand for too long.

I have a ring. I have it on a shoelace, round my neck, under the shirt. It is a thin gold band, very small. I took it from her hand, on the fifteenth, before I went down to the bridge. I do not remember doing so. I know that I did it because it is there. It lies against the sternum. It is a little warmer than the skin.

*\[Ring. It was found in a coat pocket in the cloakroom, on a shoelace, and the lace was knotted for a neck. I do not know whose coat. I have put it round his neck because I did not want to leave it in a pocket. That he took it, and does not remember taking it, is mine.\]*

At 15:00 the girl comes to sit beside me. She has a felt-tip pen, and a piece of paper, the back of a ration form, and she draws. She draws a lemon. It is a yellow oval with a point at each end, and a green leaf at the top. She draws another. She draws thirty-two, in rows of eight, and then she stops. She looks at me. She says: *Wujek, why are there thirty-two?*

I say that it is the pattern on the oilcloth.

She says: *What oilcloth?*

I say that it is in the flat.

She says: *We don't have a flat.*

I look at the drawing. The lemons are very neat. I say that we did, once.

*\[Drawing and neighbour. The drawing I have is of a single lemon, with a leaf, on the back of a ration form. The thirty-two are the neighbour's, from the first flat, and so is the oilcloth. I joined them. The girl does not remember the lemons. She remembers that she drew.\]*

She considers this. She puts the cap back on the pen.

She says: *Wujek. Are you quite all right?*

It is the first question that she has put to me in ten months that I do not have the answer to. I say that I am. I say it in the same voice. And I am aware, in the pause that follows, of the sound from the vent below the scoreboard, a slow swelling and an ebbing, once in six seconds, and aware that the sentence has come out of me whole, and unchanged, and that there is, for the first time, a gap between it and me, a space of perhaps two centimetres, in which something might be put.

I say, after a time, in a different voice, very quietly: *No.*

She nods. She does not seem surprised. She takes the drawing and puts it in the pocket of my cardigan.

*\[Interview. This is the girl's. She told me that her uncle once said to her, in a very odd voice, that he was not all right. She told it the same way twice. Where he was, what he was holding and what hour it was are mine. That she nodded, and the pocket, are mine. I have put him in the hall because the ledger has him there that night. I believe her about the sentence.\]*

At 17:30 I queue for the evening meal. At 18:30 I wash. At 20:00 the loudspeaker. *Good evening. The shelters on the left bank are open. There are beds.* And I find that, in the pause after *water*, for the first time, I breathe in with her, and I hold it, for a second, and I let it go.

*\[Mine. He breathes in with the voice. I have no authority for it. The ledger says only that he was awake, and the girl does not remember the evening. It is the one thing in this day that I wanted to happen, and I say so here so that it is not mistaken for something I found.\]*

At 21:15 I normally fold the blanket. Tonight I do not fold it. I lay it flat on the bed, with the red stripe uppermost and the number to the front, and then I take it up, and I put it round the girl's shoulders, though it is warm, and I sit on the end of the bed, number 118, and I do not do anything at all.

At 22:00 the lights go down to a third. At 22:10 the warden writes in the ledger, in the column for the night: *118. Awake. Sitting up.* It is the only entry of its kind in the ten months. In the next column, under *Action*, he has written, and then ruled through, a single word. It is *Report.* Beside it, in a different pencil, in a hand that I do not know, is a note that was added in the morning. It says: *Leave him.*

*\[Ledger. The night entry, the word ruled through and the other pencil are as I found them: 118. Awake. Sitting up. Report. Leave him. The blanket round the girl's shoulders is mine.\]*

§&#32;

The fourteenth of June 2035 is above. What follows is the page that is missing, and then what I have supposed. This is my voice, not his.

*The ledger ends there. The following page has been torn from the binding. I have asked in four shelters on the left bank and I do not know what became of Wiktor Szeliga. I know what became of the girl. She is in Gdańsk, and she is a nurse, and she told me, in a kitchen, over a glass of tea, that her uncle had once said to her, in a very odd voice, that he was not all right. She said that it was the most frightening thing that anyone had ever said to her. And she said that she had been so glad.*

I do not know who wrote Leave him, or whether it was kindness, or whether it was only a way of not writing the other word. I know what I would like it to have been. The sentence is the girl's. The man who says it in the hall is mine, and I would not offer him as a witness.

## Chapter 15: The Aftermath (Protocol 005)

**ALEX** · 2036

*Binder one. Reproduced as found. The last item filed.*

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · Protocol 005

**SYNPTCL/PROTOCOL/005 · THE AFTERMATH**

**Compiled: D. Antonoff · Status: ACTIVE · Reliability: PROB\_VAL\_0.8, with exceptions noted**

**Compiled at: OP-Czerniaków. Opened 23:50, 31 December 2034. Closed 05:40, 1 January 2035.**

**1. SCOPE**

This document records the destruction of the Żerań hub, the third flood, and the finding that explains both. It is compiled from nine sources, none of which was present at Żerań at the time. The author was not present at Żerań at the time. The author was at Czerniaków, fifteen kilometres to the south, with a radio, a recorder, a notebook and a candle.

It records what was done and in what order. It records what is known about why. It does not record how many persons were at Żerań. I do not have that figure. I have decided that I will not estimate it.

**2. SOURCES**

| **No.** | **Source** | **Rating** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | The staff gauge series, L. Kowalski, Termika and Żerań docks | 0.9 |
| 2 | Radio net, survivor, nine stations | 0.8 |
| 3 | Radio net, Russian, intercepts by M. Wiśniewska | 0.7 |
| 4 | Kontakt reports, KB, KK, KH | 0.8 |
| 5 | Runner reports, three Runners, first-hand | 0.7 |
| 6 | Observation of the sky to the north, my own | 0.6 |
| 7 | The operational brief, Neural Harvest (see section 7) | 0.8 |
| 8 | The Vistula Canal gate sequence, as given by L. Kowalski | 0.9 |
| 9 | The testimony of M. Olszewski, on 1944 (see section 6) | 0.7 |

**3. THE EVENT LOG**

Times are local. The first entry is from the series kept by Lucasz Kowalski, who read the Żerań gauge every hour until the gauge was no longer there.

| Time | Event | Source |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 31 Dec, 18:00 | Żerań gauge 84.3 m. Normal. Canal frozen at the margins | 1 |
| 31 Dec, 21:00 | Gauge 84.3 m. The Russian net goes quiet. All five posts check in on the hour and none speaks after | 3 |
| 31 Dec, 23:10 | Gauge 84.4 m. Kontakt KB reports engines to the north, at RM Tarchomin. Many | 4 |
| 31 Dec, 23:50 | This document opened | n/a |
| 1 Jan, 00:00 | Fireworks over the west bank. Eleven minutes. The occupier celebrates the new year | 6 |
| 1 Jan, 00:40 | Gauge 84.9 m. Rise of 0.5 m in 90 minutes. No rain | 1 |
| 1 Jan, 01:05 | Gauge 85.8 m. Lucasz's note against the reading: "The gates are open. All of them. In sequence." | 1 |
| 1 Jan, 01:20 | The gate sequence of the Vistula Canal, run in full. The eastern relief channel brings the crest into the Żerań basin | 8 |
| 1 Jan, 01:40 | Gauge 87.1 m. Docks overtopped. Ice breaking in the canal. Runner reports the lower quay gone | 1, 5 |
| 1 Jan, 02:05 | Gauge 88.6 m. The power station basement is flooded. The lights at Żerań go out. I note that it was the last place in the corridor that had them | 2 |
| 1 Jan, 02:30 | Searchlight from the Citadel, held on Żerań. Not swept. Held | 6 |
| 1 Jan, 02:40 | Artillery. Eleven rounds, spaced, aimed at the chimney, the control room and the dock. The searchlight does not move | 4, 5 |
| 1 Jan, 03:05 | Radio contact with the Żerań station ends in mid-sentence | 2 |
| 1 Jan, 03:20 | Gauge not read. The gauge is no longer there | 1 |
| 1 Jan, 04:00 | Runner reports gunboats entering the canal from the north, lights off | 5 |
| 1 Jan, 05:10 | The sky to the north is a different colour | 6 |
| 1 Jan, 05:40 | This document closed | n/a |

I have recorded the entry at 01:20 in the form in which it was given to me, by Lucasz, in the last transmission he sent. He said: "It's my sequence. They have swapped two lines. They have swapped the same two lines." And then, after a pause that I have timed at nine seconds: "It's very good work."

**4. THE MECHANISM**

The third flood was not an accident of weather. It was an act.

The Vistula Canal was designed to carry a flood crest away from Warsaw, round the east of the city, in a controlled channel. Its gates were sequenced by a logic written by the engineer Kowalski. In the first flood the occupiers took the control room and ran the logic in reverse, so that the canal brought water in.

On the night of 31 December they ran it again, in full, and on this occasion they did not need to run it in reverse. The river was high. They simply opened the gates at the upper end in an order that would deliver the maximum volume, with the maximum rate of rise, into the single basin at which the survivors' hub stood. They held the lower gates shut. The water had nowhere to go but up.

The mechanism required one thing: a sluice that could be opened from the Citadel. It had been prepared. It had been prepared for a long time.

**5. THE DESTRUCTION**

I record the following in the order in which it appears on the log, and I note that the sequence is not the sequence of a panic. It has three stages.

**Stage 1. The water.** To drive the population to the high ground, to destroy the dock, the boats, the stores, and to put the power station out of action.

**Stage 2. The fire.** To destroy what the water did not: the chimney stack, the control room, the warehouses, the cistern. Eleven rounds. No more. It was done with economy.

**Stage 3. The gunboats.** To enter the basin when the water was high and the fire was out and to deal with those who remained.

I am not able to record what occurred in stage 3. I have the Runner's report of the boats entering. I have no further information. I have noted this as an absence and not as a gap.

The occupier had the whole of Żerań in view from the Citadel, with its searchlight, for two and a half hours. At no point was an order given to stop.

**6. THE PARALLEL**

I have been asked by one person, whom I will not name, why I describe the destruction of Żerań in terms of 1944. I'll say now that I did not choose the comparison. It was given to me, and I have declined to refuse it.

Marek Olszewski, who was a child in the city in August 1944, has told me the following. In 1944 the Home Army rose against the German occupation. It fought for sixty-three days. It was destroyed. In those sixty-three days the Red Army stood on the east bank of the Vistula, within sight of the city, and did not cross. It watched. And when the rising had failed, the German command, on an order from above, razed what remained of the city, block by block, with explosives and with fire, as a punishment. Of the city on the left bank, eighty-five per cent was destroyed.

Marek said: "We were not killed by the Germans alone. We were killed by the ones who watched." He said it very quietly, over tea, in the kitchen, with the children under the table. He said that he had spent ninety years waiting for a person to say it aloud. I told him that I would put it in a Protocol.

I have set the two events alongside one another below. I do not claim they are the same. I claim that they rhyme, that the author of the second knew the first, and that the author was not embarrassed by the rhyme.

|  | **Warsaw, 1944** | **Żerań, 2035** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The population | A city of civilians in arms | A hub of civilians in arms |
| The trigger | The rising | The survival of a hub the occupier could not control |
| The instrument | Fire, explosive, by order | Water, fire, by order |
| The watcher | The Red Army, on the east bank | The occupier, at the Citadel, with its searchlight |
| The purpose | Punishment, by erasure | Punishment, by erasure |
| The aftermath | A city razed and later rebuilt from photographs | To be recorded |

I note that in 1944 the city was rebuilt from photographs and paintings. It is one of the things of which Warsaw is proudest. I note that Fritha Cielinski has several thousand photographs. I make no further comment.

**7. THE FINDING**

I record here the finding that the Codex was built to carry. It is the last link of the chain given in Protocol 004, section 7, the link I called reserved.

On 28 December I was given a document. I will not say by whom. It was passed to me through the Russian scientist whom I have called, in Protocol 003, a source. It is a brief. It is eleven pages, in Russian and in English, on the stationery of a research directorate. It is stamped and numbered. It is the operational brief for a programme, and the programme is called **NEURAL HARVEST**.

I have reproduced it in the second binder in full. I give here only what is necessary.

The programme is a joint one. It was conducted between the Russian state and at least two Western states, through their agencies, their military research establishments and a number of private biotechnology contractors. The Russians supplied the organism, taken from the permafrost. The Western partners supplied the biotechnology: the means by which the organism's effects on the human nervous system could be enhanced, stabilised in a water supply, and optimised.

The brief states the objective. I give it as it is written.

> *Objective: population compliance optimisation.*

I record this phrase without comment.

The brief describes the survivor population of Warsaw as a test cohort. It describes the flood as a delivery system. It describes the organism's effects, which I have classified in Protocol 003 as four stages, in a table of its own, under the heading *behavioural endpoints*. It describes stage 4 as *the target state*.

It gives the reason for the choice of city. I give this also.

> *Isolated. Contained. Non-attributable. A population of approximately 40,000 under controlled observation, with no external access and no prospect of relief.*

I note that the figure is correct to within two thousand.

The brief gives the name its second meaning. A harvest is the gathering of what has grown. The programme was not only a weapon. It was a study. The population in a state of compliance was observed, and what was observed was recorded: its decisions, its speech, its rates of deterioration, the points at which it resisted and the points at which it did not. The results are held, according to the brief, in a joint repository. The brief states that they will be *shared with partners on completion of the cohort phase*.

I have asked myself what the cohort phase is. I believe that it ended on 1 January 2035. I believe that the destruction of Żerań was the end of the study. I believe that it was destroyed, in part, because it had begun to resist, because it had a hospital that counted its patients and a violin and an engineer who kept a table of water levels and a girl in a loft who listened, and because a community that sustains these things is not a cohort. It is a population that has stopped being measurable.

I would like to be wrong about this. I have given the belief a rating of 0.6.

**8. LIMITATIONS**

This document does not record the number of the dead. It does not record the names of the Western partners, which are in the brief and which I have not copied into this binder, on the ground that a binder can be found. It does not record what the Codex cannot answer. That is the subject of the remainder.

**9. WHAT THE CODEX CANNOT ANSWER**

9.1. Who gave the order, in the Western capitals. The brief is signed with initials.

9.2. Whether the results have already been shared.

9.3. Whether the people in the corridor who remain at stage 4 will recover.

9.4. Whether it matters, to a person who has been told that she is quite all right, that she has been made so.

9.5. Whether the Codex will arrive.

**10. NOTES TO FILE**

10.1. At 02:30, when the searchlight stopped, I went out on the embankment. I could not see Żerań. I could see the beam, in the north, standing on the sky. It did not sweep. It stood. I thought, it is looking. I thought, it is being looked at. I do not know which I meant.

10.2. I have placed the second copy of the Codex where it will be found. I'll say no more about this.

10.3. At 03:05, when the Żerań station ceased in the middle of a sentence, the sentence was: "Tell Diana the chimney is".

10.4. I have not completed the sentence. I do not know how it ends. I have examined every possible ending, and I find that I cannot choose between them, and that this is, finally, the nature of the thing.

*End of Protocol 005.*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · 2036

This is the last item that Diana filed. I did not find it in the safe. It reached me by another route, in a waterproof pouch, from a Runner who had been handed it at Czerniaków that morning with an instruction to take it to the houseboat, which by then could not be reached. She was fifteen when she kept it, in a boot, for fourteen months. There is a stub of candle wax on the back of the final page. It is out of sequence. It has a header and a closing line, and between them it is, as I have said, complete.

For a long time I did not know what to do with the sentence at 10.3. I know now what it is for. I will say that the chimney, the red and white stack at the power station, is standing. I have seen it. It is the only thing at Żerań that was not brought down. I do not know why it was spared. I have been told that eleven rounds is not enough for a chimney of that size, and I have been told that it was never aimed at.

Ewa told me, in her garden, the first morning, that she had watched it come down in her dreams every night for two years. She has not seen it fall. It stands, in the light, at the end of the canal, in the water, and the reeds have grown up around it. When I went there last, there was a heron at the foot of it.

I have not been able to complete the sentence either.

## Chapter 16: The Protocols

**ALEX** · 2036

I will say very little here.

Everything in this book has been assembled by me, from what people told me and what she left. This chapter is the one place where I have not assembled it. I have put the items in the order in which she filed them, and I have added nothing. Where she stopped, I have stopped.

I will return, once, at the end.

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · transmission, 20 June 2034

**SY/DA/0001\_SG** · *Transmission to the Suspilne desk, Kyiv*

*From: D. Antonoff, Warsaw. Sent: 20 June 2034. Priority: routine. Length: 311 words.*

I am writing from a houseboat on the Żerań canal, in the north-east of the city. I have been here three weeks. I will give you the facts as I have them and then, because you will ask, the other thing.

The facts. Warsaw is divided by the Vistula. The west bank is dry and occupied. The east bank is partly flooded and holds a community of about forty thousand survivors, in a corridor of dry places that run from Żerań in the north to Wilanów in the south. They are organised. They have a hospital, a water station, a radio net, a system of couriers, and a resistance that has been fighting for six years. They are not starving, though they are hungry. They are not besieged, exactly. They are watched.

The water stands at 84 metres. It does not recede. The reason is infrastructural and I will send it in detail.

There are rumours about an organism in the water. I have not yet seen it. I will report what I see.

Now the other thing. The canal is beautiful. I did not expect that, and I'd like to say it plainly, because every report you have received from this city has described it in the language of ruin. It is a very warm June. The reeds are shoulder-high. There are lily pads on the water as wide as dinner plates, and frogs on the pads, and dragonflies over the frogs, and at dusk, from the deck of the boat, I have seen a beaver, a large one, swimming steadily across with its head just above the surface, leaving a perfect V. It looked at me. It went on.

I want you to understand that the people here are not living among the ruins of a city. They are living in a garden that has been flooded. The garden is lovely, and it is hiding something. I do not yet know what. I will find it, and I will document it, and I will tell the truth.

I am well. I am sleeping. The boat is warm.

Please do not send anyone.

D.

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 9 December 2034

**SY/DA/0041\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date: **9 December 2034, 06:20*

***Location:** the houseboat, OP-Żerań, on the deck*

***Conditions: **clear, very cold, a mist on the water, the first light*

***Temperature:** −3°C*

***Pressure:** 1024 hPa, rising*

***Flood:** stable. Canal ice at Żerań 11 cm, walkable at the margin.*

*I want to put down something that happened this morning, and I will do it openly**, because it is not a finding.*

*I was on the deck at six. It is where I write when I can. The mist was lying on the canal at about the height of my knee, so that the reeds stood up out of it like the masts of a drowned fleet, and the chimney at the far end had its top lit pink and the rest of it in shadow. There was a heron on the ice. It stood very still. I had my notebook on my knee and a flask beside me, and I realised that I had not opened the notebook.*

*I said a prayer.*

*I'm going to write exactly what I mean by that. I said the first line of the prayer I was taught, in Ukrainian, aloud, on the deck, with my breath showing. I had not said it since March 2022. I did not decide to. It was there, in my mouth, the way a word is there that you have not used for years and that you find you still know.*

*I did not go on to the second line. I stopped. I sat there. The heron had not moved. And I found that I was not afraid of having said it, which was the thing I had been afraid of for two years: that if I said it, there would be nobody there, and I would hear it go into the air and not come back.*

*Something came back. I'll not say what. I'm not going to claim it was an answer. It was more like the sound of a door that has been left on the latch for a long time, and the sense that it was not locked.*

*I thought of Janek. I thought of the dock, in August, and his saying that the city was in God, and not the other way about. I thought, that is what he meant. It is smaller than I thought, and it makes no difference to where you stand.*

*I thought of my fiancé. I allowed myself to, for the first time in some months, without stopping. I will not write what I thought. I'll write that I said his name, once, aloud. It did not hurt less. It hurt exactly the same amount. But it was, for the first time, a thing that was in the world, and not in my chest.*

*I have been going about this work as if truth were the only thing I was answerable to. I've held it very tightly. I think I held it so tightly because I had nothing else to hold. And I see this morning that a god you grip with both hands is not a god. It is a rail.*

*I'm not going to stop. I will go on writing. But I am going to loosen my hands.*

*The heron went up, and the mist went with it.*

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 28 December 2034

**SY/DA/0044\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date:** 28 December 2034, 22:40*

***Location: **the houseboat, OP-Żerań, the cabin*

***Conditions:** dark, clear, a hard frost. Stars.*

***Temperature:** −9°C*

***Pressure: **1031 hPa, steady*

***Flood:** stable. Canal frozen from bank to bank.*

*I have the brief. I have read it four times. I'll write down what I did afterwards, because it is the only part that I can do anything about.*

*I made tea. I did that very carefully. I warmed the pot. I measured the leaves. I stood at the stove and watched the kettle, and I understood that I was behaving like a person who is trying to remain a person.*

*I want to record that I did not cry. I thought I might. What I felt was not grief. It was a kind of vertigo. For six months I have been building a chain of ratings, 0.3, 0.5, 0.4, each link honest and each link weak, and tonight the last link was put in my hand, and it is the strongest of all, and it is, in every respect, worse than anything I have imagined. I had thought that when I finally had the proof I would feel vindicated. I feel instead that I have been handed a very heavy object and told to carry it, without being told where.*

*Population compliance optimisation. I read the phrase aloud, in the cabin, to hear what it sounded like. It sounded like nothing. It sounded like the name of a department. That is the thing that I cannot get over. Not the cruelty. The tidiness.*

*Forty thousand. A test cohort. The woman at the Zoo who leaves a saucer of milk for the organism every morning at seven and has been at stage 1 for three months because the saucer is for someone else. She is in the brief. She is a datum. The tram driver who wept in the lion house is in the brief. Nikita is in the brief, in the line that says that a small proportion of the population will display atypical resistance, and Nikita is that, and so are the three at Brodno, and so, I suppose, am I.*

*I'm going to make the entry now that I owe myself, and then I will go to bed.*

*I do not know whether I'll be able to get this out. The signal to Kyiv has been silent for eleven days. I have made two copies of the Codex. One is in the safe, here, on the shelf behind the bunk. The second I have given to a person I trust, who has a plan for it that I did not ask about.*

*I have decided something. I have found, over these months, that I have been a person who writes down what she sees, and I've believed that this was enough. It is not. I held a principle once, and watched, and I wrote it down. I said, in the margin, that next time I would stand up.*

*I don't** know yet what standing up looks like. I think that I will know it when it is time.*

*I'm going to bed. I will not sleep.*

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 30 December 2034

**SY/DA/0045\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date:** 30 December 2034, 06:45*

***Location:** the houseboat, OP-Żerań, on the deck*

***Conditions: **clear, very cold, no wind. The ice has a skin of frost on it that glitters*

***Temperature:** −11°C*

***Pressure:** 1029 hPa, steady*

***Flood:** stable. Staff gauge 84.3 m.*

*I want to be very exact about this morning, because it may be the last time I write from this place, and I would like it to be a good record.*

*The deck is eleven paces long and three wide. The planks have been gone over by the frost so that each has a bloom of white along its grain. There is a smell of cold iron and wood smoke from the Brigade kitchen. The chimney stands at the end of the canal. The sun is not up, but it is going to be, and the top of the stack has gone the colour of the inside of a shell.*

*I have counted the dragonflies on thirty-one mornings and there is not one now. They are in the reeds, as larvae, under the ice. They will be there in June. I have made a note of the species so that whoever comes can look for them: the banded demoiselle, the four-spotted chaser, the ruddy darter, the emperor. I'm not sure who I'm writing it for. I have the idea that it's a person who will want to know what the place was like before it was a place that had to be remembered.*

*There is a woman who lives in the garden at the end of the lane. She has a gate with a chain on it, and a sign that says that unauthorised persons are forbidden. I have seen her, from the deck, on perhaps twenty mornings. She is in her sixties, and she hangs out her washing, and she looks across the water. She has never spoken to me. I have never spoken to her. Once or twice she has raised a hand, and I have raised mine, and neither of us has gone further. I have decided that I am going to leave it like that. I think that it is the right amount.*

*I packed this morning. It did not take long. A bag. The recorder. The second binder. The camera, which I haven't **used. I've put the notebooks in the safe, with the first copy of the Codex, and the spare battery, and the key to the boat, and a photograph in a small square frame that I will not describe. I would like it to be found with the rest. The key to the safe is in the place that I told the person I trust.*

*I have been asked to go to Czerniaków. There is a Kontakt point there that has been silent for two days, and the net needs someone who can read what the silence means. I said that I would go on the thirty-first. It is a journey of a day. Goya says that it's sensible. I think he is wrong about why, and I think that it is the right thing to do anyway.*

*I stood on the deck for some time after I had finished. I said the prayer again. I got to the second line.*

*I'm not afraid. I'd like that on the record. I thought that I would be. I find that I am watching the light on the chimney, and that it is lovely, and that it is, for the moment, enough.*

*\[in pencil, below: the boat. the beaver. the V.\]*

\* \* \*

**DIANA** · diary, 1 January 2035

**SY/DA/0046\_DD** · *From the diary of Diana Antonoff*

***Date:** 1 January 2035, 05:55*

***Location:** OP-Czerniaków, the embankment*

***Conditions:** pre-dawn, clear, very cold, a red glow to the north*

***Temperature:** −12°C*

***Pressure:** 1027 hPa, steady*

***Flood: **THIRD FLOOD. Not at this post. See Protocol 005.*

*I have closed the Protocol. I have the time. I'll put it in the pouch.*

*The Runner is here. She is perhaps fifteen, and she has been very quiet, and she has a boot with a lining that I have asked her to use. She says that she knows the culvert. She says that it is thirty minutes to the first dry place. I have told her to go now. I have given her the pouch, and the page, and I've told her that if she is asked, she is to say that she has been to buy bread. She looked at me for some time, and she did not say anything, and then she said, "And you?" and I said that I'd follow.*

*That is not true, and I find that I don't** mind having said it. I'd like it recorded that it is the only thing that I have said in the whole of this notebook that I knew to be false at the time. I will put it down to love.*

*There is an engine on the water. It is to the north, in the channel, coming slowly. I can hear it quite clearly, because the air is very still. It has no lights. A gunboat. It's going to come round the bend and see the embankment, and it will see the Runner's culvert, which is about two hundred metres from where I'm sitting. It will certainly see me. I'm the only thing on the embankment with a lamp.*

*The light is coming. It is a very thin colour, at the edge of the sky. The ice on the river has gone grey and then a kind of pale rose.*

*I have thought about what I should write last. I think that there is nothing in particular. I think that it is right that this should be an ordinary entry, with the header, and the weather, and the time. I have the sense that if I wrote anything grand, my father would look at it and say, how do you know. I do not know. I have written what I saw.*

*I said the prayer again. I got all the way to the end.*

*I'm going to stand up now. I am going to go down to the edge of the water, where the boat can see me, because it is the only way I can think of to be certain that it looks at me and not at*

\* \* \*

**ALEX** · 2036

The entry stops there. There is no full stop. The pencil has not lifted: there is a short trail of graphite, a few millimetres long, running on from the last letter across the grain of the page, as if the hand had been moved. There is nothing on the following leaf. There is nothing in the second binder, or the first. The box in the safe, when I opened it, contained a camera, a spare battery, the key to a boat, and a small square photograph in a frame, of a woman on a shoreline with her hair in her eyes, looking at the water, in the middle of deciding.

The Runner was called Oliwia. She got out. She kept the pouch in her boot for fourteen months. I won't say where she is. She asked that I say nothing about her except that she did as she was told, and that she has never, to her knowledge, been to buy bread.

I found a house outside Kraków, early spring. A woman was working in the garden. Her back was to me. I didn't call out. I'm not sure why. I watched her for perhaps two minutes, then I got back in the car and drove.&#32;

I have not returned.&#32;

My report ends here.

## Acknowledgments

This book began as a map. I want to thank the people who helped me turn it into something that can be read.

I am grateful to the people of Warsaw, who have let me live in their city, walk its edges, record its water and its silences. Much of what is true in these pages came from standing in the places described and listening.

I am grateful to my brother, Ian, who knows me better than anyone, and who has read more of my enthusiasms than any person should be asked to.

I am grateful to Kathryn, for her patience with a man who is sometimes in two places at once.

I am grateful to the friends who saw early versions of the map, the field log and the codex, and who told me which parts breathed and which did not. They know who they are.

I am grateful to *Cargo*, for the work on the backend and the infrastructure that sit behind the page and the website. And to *Loopscope* for providing the sounds accompanying the audio version of this work.

I am grateful to Anthropic, for giving me the tools to bring my imagination to life, and for helping me to see that my autism is not so much a restriction as a way of being that allows me to dream, to create and to be free.

I owe a debt to the books and the films that taught me how to hold many voices at once, and above all to Max Brooks, Krzysztof Kieślowski, Nevil Shute, Hideo Kojima and Viktor Frankl, whom I have borrowed from and tried to repay with something of my own.

> “Everything can be taken from a man but one thing: the last of the human freedoms – to choose one's attitude in any given set of circumstances, to choose one's own way.”
>
> Viktor E. Frankl, *Man's Search for Meaning*

I am grateful to every witness, real and imagined, who survived a catastrophe and then agreed to describe it to a stranger. The idea that testimony is a form of care came from them.

And to Diana, wherever she is.
